Machine Learning For Elliptic PDEs: Fast Rate Generalization Bound, Neural Scaling Law and Minimax Optimality
Yiping Lu, Haoxuan Chen, Jianfeng Lu, Lexing Ying), Jose Blanchet

Abstract
In this paper, we study the statistical limits of deep learning techniques for solving elliptic partial differential equations (PDEs) from random samples using the Deep Ritz Method (DRM) and Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs). To simplify the problem, we focus on a prototype elliptic PDE: the Schrödinger equation on a hypercube with zero Dirichlet boundary condition, which is applied in quantum-mechanical systems. We establish upper and lower bounds for both methods, which improve upon concurrently developed upper bounds for this problem via a fast rate generalization bound. We discover that the current Deep Ritz Method is sub-optimal and propose a modified version of it. We also prove that PINN and the modified version of DRM can achieve minimax optimal bounds over Sobolev spaces. Empirically, following recent work which has shown that the deep model accuracy will improve with growing training sets according to a power law, we supply computational experiments to show similar-behavior of dimension dependent power law for deep PDE solvers.



keywords

Machine Learning, Non-parametric Statistics, Deep Ritz Methods, Physics Informed Neural Network

†
†
runningheads:
Statistical Numerical PDE / Preprint Version

62G05, 65N15, 68T07

1
Introduction

Partial differential equations (PDEs) play a prominent role in many disciplines of science and engineering. The recent deep learning breakthrough and the rapid development of sensors, computational power, and data storage in the past decade has drawn attention to numerically solving PDEs via machine learning methods
[
45
,
44
,
58
,
26
,
68
,
38
]
, especially in high dimensions where conventional methods become impractical. The set of applications that motivate this interest is wide-ranging, including computational physics
[
26
,
45
,
58
]
, inverse problem
[
82
,
22
,
17
]
and quantitative finance
[
30
,
21
]
. The numerical methods generated by the use of deep learning techniques are mesh-less methods, see the discussion in
[
78
]
. A natural deep learning technique in the problems that are based on a standard feed-forward type of architecture takes advantage (when available) of a variational formulation, whose solution coincides with the solution of the PDE of interest. Despite the success and popularity of adopting neural networks for solving high-dimensional PDEs, the following question still remains poorly answered.

For a given PDE and a data-driven approximation architecture, how large the sample size and how complex the model are needed to reach a prescribed performance level?

In this paper, we aim to establish the numerical analysis of such deep learning based PDE solvers. Inspired by recent works which showed that the empirical performance of a model is remarkably predictable via a power law of the data number, known as the neural scaling law
[
37
,
31
,
65
]
, we aim to explore the neural scaling law for deep PDE solvers and compare its performance to Fourier approximation.

Among the various approaches of using deep learning methods for solving PDEs, in this work, we focus on the Deep Ritz method (DRM)
[
15
,
38
]
and the Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINN) approach
[
68
,
58
]
, both of which are based on minimizing neural network parameters according to some loss functional related to the PDEs.
To provide theoretical guarantees for DRM and PINN, following
[
47
,
14
,
2
]
, we decompose the error into approximation error
[
80
,
70
,
66
]
and generalization error
[
3
,
79
,
18
,
63
,
70
]
. However, instead of the
O(1/\sqrt{n})
(
n
is the number of data sampled) slow rate generalization bounds established in prior work
[
47
,
66
,
78
,
67
]
, we utilize the strongly convex structure of the DRM and PINN objectives and provide an
O(1/n)
fast rate generalization bound
[
3
,
79
]
that leads us to a non-parametric estimation bound. Our theory also suggests an optimal selection of network size with respect to the number of sampled data. Moreover, to illustrate the optimality of our upper bound, we also establish an information-theoretic lower bound which matches our upper bound for PINN and a modified version of DRM.

We also test our theory by numerical experiments. Recent
works
[
31
,
37
,
62
,
51
]
studying a variety of deep learning algorithms all find the same polynomial scaling relation between the testing error and the number of data. As the number of training data
n
increases, the population loss
\mathcal{L}
of well-trained and well-tuned models scales with
n
as a power-law
\mathcal{L}\propto\frac{1}{n^{\alpha}}
for some
\alpha
.
[
65
]
also scans over a large range of
\alpha
and problem dimension
d
and finds an approximately
\alpha\propto\frac{1}{d}
scaling law. In Section 4, we conduct numerical experiments to show that this phenomenon still appears for deep PDE solvers and this neural scaling law tests more idiosyncratic features of the theory.

1.1
Related Works

Neural Scaling Law

The starting point of our work is the recent observation across speech, vision and text
[
31
,
37
,
62
,
61
]
that the empirical performance of a model satisfies a power law scales as a power-law with model size and dataset size.
[
65
]
further finds out that the power of the scaling law depends on the intrinsic dimension of the dataset. Theoretical works
[
63
,
70
,
72
,
9
,
33
,
18
,
36
]
explore the optimal power law under the non-parametric curve estimation setting via a plug-in neural network. Our work extends this line of research to solving PDEs.

Deep Network Based PDE Solver.

Solving high dimensional partial differential equations (PDEs) has been a long-standing challenge due to the curse of dimensionality. At the same time, deep learning has shown superior flexibility and adaptivity in approximating high dimensional functions, which leads to state-of-the-art performances in a wide range of tasks ranging from computer vision to natural language processing. Recent years, pioneer works
[
26
,
58
,
45
,
68
,
38
]
try to utilize the deep neural networks to solve different types of PDEs and achieve impressive results in many tasks
[
46
,
42
]
. Based on the natural idea of representing solutions of PDEs by (deep) neural networks, different loss functions for solving PDEs are proposed.
[
26
,
27
]
utilize the Feynman-Kac formulation which turns solving PDE to a stochastic control problem and the weak adversarial network
[
81
]
solves the weak formulations of PDEs via an adversarial network. In this paper, we focus on the convergence rate of the Deep Ritz Method (DRM)
[
15
,
38
]
and Physics-Informed neural network (PINN)
[
58
,
68
]
. DRM
[
15
,
38
]
utilizes the variational structure of the PDE, which is similar to the Ritz-Galerkin method in classical numerical analysis of PDEs, and trains a neural network to minimize the variational objective. PINN
[
58
,
68
]
trains a neural network directly to minimize the residual of the PDE, i.e., using the strong form of the PDE.

Theoretical Guarantees For Machine Learning Based PDE Solvers.

Theoretical convergence results for deep learning based PDE solvers raises wide interest recently. Specifically,
[
47
,
23
,
50
,
77
,
78
,
67
,
2
]
investigate the regularity of PDEs approximated by neural network and
[
47
,
48
]
further provide a generalization analysis.
[
54
]
introduces a prior over the solution of the PDE and considers an equivalent white noise model
[
6
]
.
[
54
]
provides the rate of convergence of the posterior. Our paper does not need to introduce the prior on the target function and provides a non-asymptotic guarantee for finite number of data. At the same time,
[
54
]
can only be applied to linear PDEs while our proof technique can be extend to nonlinear ones. All these papers also fail to answer the question that how to determine the network size corresponding to the sampled data number to achieve a desired statistical convergence rate.
[
32
,
49
]
consider the similar problem for the optimal transport problem,
i.e.
Monge-ampere equation. Nevertheless, the variational problem we considered is different from
[
32
,
49
]
and leads to technical difference. The most related works to ours are two
concurrent
papers
[
14
,
34
,
35
]
. However, our upper bound is faster than
[
14
,
34
,
35
]
. In this paper, we also show that generalization analysis in
[
47
,
14
,
48
]
are loose due to the lack of a localization technique
[
12
,
3
,
39
,
78
]
. With observation of the strong convexity of the loss function, we follow the fast rate results for ERM
[
63
,
79
,
18
]
and provide a near optimal bound for both DRM and PINN.

1.2
Contribution

In short, we summarize our contribution as follows

•

In this paper, we first considered the statistical limit of learning a PDE solution from sampled observations. The lower bound shows a non-standard exponent different from non-parametric estimation of a function.

•

Instead of the
O(1/\sqrt{n})
slow rate generalization bounds in
[
47
,
14
,
34
,
36
]
, we utilized the strongly convex nature of the variational form and provided a fast rate generalization bound via the localization methods
[
75
,
3
,
39
,
69
,
79
]
. We discovered that the current Deep Ritz Methods is sub-optimal and propose a modified version of it. We showed that PINN and the modified version of DRM can achieve nearly min-max optimal convergence rate. Our result is listed in Table
1
.

•

We tested the recently discovered neural scaling law
[
31
,
37
,
62
,
28
]
for deep PDE solvers numerically. The empirical results verified our theory.

2
Set-up

We consider the static Schrödinger equation with zero Dirichlet boundary conditions on the domain
\Omega
, which we assume to be the unit hypercube in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. In order to precisely introduce the problem, we recall some standard notions. We consider our domain as
\Omega=[0,1]^{d}
and use
L^{2}(\Omega)
to denote the space of square integrable functions on
\Omega
with respect to the Lebesgue measure. We let
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
be the space of essentially bounded (with respect to the Lebesgue measure) functions on
\Omega
and
C(\partial\Omega)
denotes the space of continuous functions on
\partial\Omega
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
,
V\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, and ,
g\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. Our focus is on the analysis of Deep-Learning-based numerical methods to solve the elliptic equations

\displaystyle-\Delta u+Vu

\displaystyle=f\quad\text{ in }\Omega,

(2.1)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=g\quad\text{ on }\partial\Omega.

2.1
Loss Functions for Solving PDEs and Induced Evaluation Metric

In this paper, we mainly focus on analyzing Deep Ritz Methods (DRM) and Physics Informed Neural Network (PINN). In this subsection, we first introduce the objective function and algorithm of the two methods.

Deep Ritz Methods

[
15
,
68
]
Recall that the equation
2.1
is equivalent to following variational form

u^{\ast}=\arg\min_{H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)}{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{DRM}}(u):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}+V|u|^{2}\ dx-\int_{\Omega}fudx,

(2.2)

where
u
is minimized over
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
with boundary condition given by
g
on
\partial\Omega
.

This variational form provides the basis for the DRM type method for solving the static Schrödinger equation based on neural network ansatz. More specifically, the energy functional given in equation
2.2
is viewed as the population risk function to train an optimal estimator approximation of the solution to the PDE within a parameterized hypothesis function class
{\mathcal{F}}\subset H^{1}(\Omega)
. In this paper, we also rely on the strong convexity of the DRM objective respect to the
H^{1}
norm.

Proposition 1
.

We further assume
0<V_{\min}\leq V(x)\leq V_{\max}
, then we have

\frac{2}{\max\{1,V_{\max}\}}\left({\mathcal{E}}^{\text{DRM}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{DRM}}(u^{\ast})\right)\leq\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\leq\frac{2}{\max\{1,V_{\min}\}}\left({\mathcal{E}}^{\text{DRM}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{DRM}}(u^{\ast})\right)

holds for all
u\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)

Physics-Informed Neural Network

[
58
,
68
]
. PINN solves
2.1
via minimizing the following objective function

u^{\ast}=\arg\min_{H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)}{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{PINN}}(u):=\arg\min_{H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)}\int_{\Omega}|\Delta u(x)-V(x)u(x)+f(x)|^{2}dx.

The objective function
{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{PINN}}
can also be viewed as the population risk function and we can train an optimal estimator approximation of the solution to the PDE within a parameterized hypothesis function class
{\mathcal{F}}\subset H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
. In this paper, we also rely on the strong convexity of the PINN objective with respect to the
H^{2}
norm, for which we need some additional assumptions on the potential.

Proposition 2
.

For PINN, we further assume
V\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
with
0<C_{\min}<V^{2}-\Delta V,0<C_{\min}<V(x)\leq V_{\max}
and
-\Delta V(x)\leq V_{\max}
, then we have for all
u\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)

\frac{1}{2\left(1+V_{\max}+V_{\max}^{2}\right)}\left({\mathcal{E}}^{\text{PINN}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{PINN}}(u^{\ast})\right)\leq\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\\
\leq\frac{2}{\max\{1,C_{\min}\}}\left({\mathcal{E}}^{\text{PINN}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{PINN}}(u^{\ast})\right).

2.2
Estimator Setting

Empirical Loss Minimization

In order to access the
d
-dimensional integrals, DRM
[
15
,
38
]
and PINN
[
58
,
68
]
employ a Monte-Carlo method for computing the high dimensional integrals, which leads to the so-called
empirical risk minimization
training for neural networks. To define the empirical loss, let
\{X_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
be an i.i.d. sequence of random variables distributed according to the uniform distribution in domain
{\mathcal{P}}_{\Omega}
. We also have access to
f_{j}=f(X_{j})+\xi_{i},j=1,\cdots,n
is the noisy observation of the right hand side of the PDE (
2.1
) and
\xi
is a bounded random variable with mean zero and independent with
X_{j}
. Define the empirical losses
{\mathcal{E}}_{n,S}
by setting

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{DRM}}_{n}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big[|\Omega|\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{2}|\nabla u(X_{j})|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}V(X_{j})|u(X_{j})|^{2}-f_{j}u(X_{j})\Big)\Big],

(2.3)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{PINN}}_{n}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big[|\Omega|\cdot\Big(\Delta u(X_{j})-V(X_{j})u(X_{j})+f_{j}\Big)^{2}\Big],

(2.4)

where
|\Omega|
represent the Lebesgue measure of the sets.

Once given an empirical loss
{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{n}
, we apply the empirical loss minimization to seek the estimation
u_{n}
, i.e.
u_{n}=\argmin_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}}{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u)
where
{\mathcal{F}}
is the parametrized hypothesis function space we consider. For example, reproducing kernel Hilbert space
[
10
]
and tensor training format
[
60
]
. In this paper, we consider sparse neural network and truncated fourier basis, which can achieves min-max optimal estimation rate for the non-parametric function estimation
[
74
,
63
,
18
,
70
,
9
,
36
,
55
]
.

Sparse Neural Network Function Space

In this paper, the hypothesis function space
\mathcal{F}
is expressed by the neural network following
[
63
,
70
,
18
]
. Let us denote the ReLU
3
activation by
\eta_{3}(x)=\max\{x^{3},0\}~(x\in\mathbb{R})
which is used in
[
15
]
, and for a vector
x
,
\eta(x)
is operated in an element-wise manner.
Define the neural network with height
L
, width
W
, sparsity constraint
S
and norm constraint
B
as

\displaystyle\Phi(L,W,S,B):=\{(\mathcal{W}^{(L)}\eta_{3}(\cdot)+b^{(L)})\circ\cdots(\mathcal{W}^{(2)}\eta_{3}(\cdot)+b^{(2)})\circ(\mathcal{W}^{(1)}x+b^{(1)})\ |\

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{(L)}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times W},b^{(L)}\in\mathbb{R},\mathcal{W}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{W\times d},b^{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{W},\mathcal{W}^{(l)}\in\mathbb{R}^{W\times W},b^{(l)}\in\mathbb{R}^{W}(1<l<L),

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{L}(\|\mathcal{W}^{(l)}\|_{0}+\|b^{(l)}\|_{0})\leq S,\max_{l}\|\mathcal{W}^{(l)}\|_{\infty,\infty}\vee\|b^{(l)}\|_{\infty}\leq B\},

(2.5)

where
\circ
denotes the function composition,
\|\cdot\|_{0}
is the
\ell_{0}
-norm of the matrix (the number of non-zero elements of the matrix) and
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\infty}
is the
\ell_{\infty}
-norm of the matrix (maximum of the absolute values of the elements).

Truncated Fourier Basis Estimator

We also considered the Truncated Fourier basis as our estimator. Suppose the domain we interested
\Omega\subseteq[0,1]^{d}
. For any
z\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
, we consider the corresponding Fourier basis function
\phi_{z}(x):=e^{2\pi i\langle z,x\rangle}\ (x\in\Omega)
. Any function
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
can be represented as weighted sum of the Fourier basis

f(x):=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}f_{z}\phi_{z}(x)

where
f_{z}:=\int_{\Omega}f(x)\overline{\phi_{z}(x)}dx\ (\forall\ z\in\mathbb{N}^{d})
is the Fourier coefficient. This inspired us to use the Fourier Basis whose index lies in a truncated set
Z_{\xi}=\{z\in\mathcal{Z}|\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi\}
to represent the function class
{\mathcal{F}}
as
{\mathcal{F}}_{\xi}=\{\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}a_{z}\phi_{z}|a_{z}\in\mathbb{R},\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi\}
.

3
Lower Bound

In this section, we aim to consider the statistical limit of learning the solution of a PDE. As discussed in Propositions
1
and
2
, we directly consider the
H^{1}
norm for DRM and
H^{2}
norm for PINN as the evaluation metric. The lower bound shown as follows.

Theorem 3
(Lower bound).

We denote
u^{\ast}(f)
to be the solution of the PDE
2.1
and we can access randomly sampled data
\{X_{i},f_{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,n}
as described in Section
2.2
. We further assume
u^{\ast}(f)\in H^{s}
for a given
s\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, we have the following lower bounds.

DRM Lower Bound.

For all estimators
\psi:\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)^{\otimes n}\times\mathbb{R}^{\otimes n}\rightarrow H^{s}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\psi}\sup_{u^{\ast}\in H^{s}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}\|\psi(\{X_{i},f_{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,n})-u^{\ast}(f)\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\gtrsim n^{-\frac{2s-2}{d+2s-4}}.

(3.1)

PINN Lower Bound.

For all estimators
\psi:\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)^{\otimes n}\times\mathbb{R}^{\otimes n}\rightarrow H^{s}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\psi}\sup_{u^{\ast}\in H^{s}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}\|\psi(\{X_{i},f_{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,n})-u^{\ast}(f)\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\gtrsim n^{-\frac{2s-4}{d+2s-4}}.

(3.2)

Proof.

We construct the following bump function to construct the multiple hypothesis test used for proving the lower bound. Consider a simple
C^{\infty}
bump function supported on
[0,1]^{d}

g(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\xi(x_{i}),x=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{d}),

where
\xi:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a non-zero funtion in
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with support contained in
[0,1]
and satisfies
\xi(x)\not=0,\frac{d}{dx}\xi(x)\not=0
. Then
\nabla g(x)\not=0
and the support of function
g
is
[0,1]^{d}
.

Next, we take
m=[n^{\frac{1}{2s-4+d}}]
and consider a regular gird
x^{(j)},j\in[m]^{d}
. According to the Varshamov-Gilbert lemma, there exist
2^{m^{d}/8}

(0,1)
-sequences
\tau^{(1)},\cdots,\tau^{(2^{m^{d}/8})}\in\{0,1\}^{m^{d}}
such that
\|\tau^{(k)}-\tau^{(k^{\prime})}\|^{2}\geq\frac{m^{d}}{8}
for all
0<k\not=k^{\prime}\leq 2^{m^{d}/8}
. Then we construct the multiple hypothesis as

u_{k}(x)=\sum_{j\in[m]^{d}}\tau_{j}^{(k)}\frac{\omega}{m^{s+\frac{d}{2}}}g(m(x-x^{(j)})),k=1,2,\cdots,2^{m^{d}/8},

where
\omega
is a constant to be determined later. It is easy to find out that
u_{k}\in C^{s}
.

Then we reduce solving the PDE to a multiple hypothesis testing problem, which considers all mappings from
n
sampled data to the constructed hypothesis
\Psi:\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)^{\otimes n}\times\mathbb{R}^{\otimes n}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}:=\{u_{i}|i=1,2,\cdots,2^{m^{d}/8}\}
. Then we apply the local Fano method and check that we can obtain a constant lower bound of
\mathcal{P}(\hat{V}\not=V)
for any estimator
\hat{V}
. From the local Fano method, we know that

\displaystyle I(V;X)\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{V}|^{2}}\sum_{z}\sum_{v\not=v^{\prime}}D_{KL}(P_{v}||P_{v}^{\prime}),

where
P_{k}
denotes the joint distribution of the sampled data
(x,y)
. In specific,
x
follows a uniform distribution on
[0,1]^{d}
and
y=f(X)+\epsilon
, where
\epsilon
is independently sampled from a standard Gaussian distribution
N(0,1)
. Then we have

KL(P_{k}||P_{k^{\prime}})=\mathbb{E}\log(\frac{dP_{k}}{dP_{k^{\prime}}})=\|\Delta u_{k}+Vu_{k}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\leq\frac{C\omega}{m^{2s-4}}.

Using Fano inequality, if we select
m\propto[n^{\frac{1}{2s-4+d}}]
then we have the following lower bound when
\omega
is taken to be sufficiently large:

\mathcal{P}(\hat{V}\not=V)\geq 1-\frac{I(V;X)+\log 2}{\log(|\mathcal{V}|)}\geq 1-\frac{\frac{8C\omega}{m^{2s-4}}}{m^{d}\log 2}\geq 1/2.

At the same time, we can estimate the separation of the hypotheses in two different norms:

•

Deep Ritz Method:

\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\|\nabla u_{k}-\nabla u_{k^{\prime}}\|^{2}dx=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{m^{2s-2+d}}\sum_{j\in[m]^{d}}\|\tau_{j}^{(k)}-\tau_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}\|_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\nabla g(x)\|^{2}dx\gtrsim\frac{1}{m^{2s-2}}.

•

Physic Informed Neural Network:

\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\|\Delta u_{k}-\Delta u_{k^{\prime}}\|^{2}dx=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{m^{2s-4+d}}\sum_{j\in[m]^{d}}\|\tau_{j}^{(k)}-\tau_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}\|_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Delta g(x)^{2}dx\gtrsim\frac{1}{m^{2s-4}}.

Plugging in
m\propto[n^{\frac{1}{2s-4+d}}]
, we know that with constant probability we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\psi}\sup_{u^{\ast}\in H^{s}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}\|\psi(\{X_{i},Y_{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,n})-u^{\ast}(f)\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\gtrsim n^{-\frac{2s-2}{d+2s-4}},

(3.3)

\displaystyle\inf_{\psi}\sup_{u^{\ast}\in H^{s}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}\|\psi(\{X_{i},Y_{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,n})-u^{\ast}(f)\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\gtrsim n^{-\frac{2s-4}{d+2s-4}}.

(3.4)

Given that
n^{-\frac{2(\beta-k)}{d+2\beta}}
is the minimax rate of estimation of the
k
-th derivative of a

\beta
-smooth density in
L_{2}

[
43
,
57
,
53
]
, the lower bound obtained here is the rate of estimating the right hand side function
f
in terms of the
H^{-1}
norm. Given the
H^{-1}
norm error estimate on
f
, we can achieve estimate of
u
, which provides an alternative way to understand our upper bound. The lower bound is non-standard, for the
2s-2
in the numerator is different from the
2s-4
in the denominator.

4
Upper Bound

To theoretically understand the empirical success of Physics Informed Neural Networks and the Deep Ritz solver, in this section, we aim to prove that the excess risk
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{n}:={\mathcal{E}}(u_{n})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})
of a well-trained neural networks on the PINN/DRM loss function will follow a precise power-law scaling relations with the size of the training dataset. Similar to
[
78
,
47
,
14
,
34
,
35
]
, we decompose the excess risk into approximation error and generalization error. Different from the concurrent bound
[
14
,
34
]
, we provided a fast rate
O(1/n)
by utilizing the strong convexity of the objective function established in Section
2.1
and achieved a faster and near optimal upper bound.

4.1
Proof Sketch

Error Decomposition.

We first decompose the excess risk
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{n}:={\mathcal{E}}(u_{n})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})
of a well-trained neural network on the PINN/DRM loss function into approximation error and generalization error, similar to
[
78
,
47
,
14
,
34
,
35
]
. The regularity results used in the decomposition are proved in Appendix
A.2
. Explicitly, for any
u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
, we can decompose the excess risk as

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}(\hat{u})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})

(4.1)

\displaystyle=\big[{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\hat{u})\big]+\big[{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\hat{u})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})\big]+\big[{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})\big]+\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\big[{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\hat{u})\big]+\big[{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})\big]}_{\text{Generalization Error}}+\underbrace{\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]}_{\text{Approximation Error}},

where the expectation is on uniformly sampled data,
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
is the space of parametrized estimators we used like truncated Fourier series or sparse neural networks,
\hat{u}
is the minimizer of the empirical loss
{\mathcal{E}}_{n}
in
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
and
u^{\ast}
is the minimizer of the population loss
{\mathcal{E}}
(i.e, ground truth solution). The inequality in the third line follows from the fact that
\hat{u}
is the minimizer of the empirical loss
{\mathcal{E}}_{n}
in the space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
, which implies
{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\hat{u})\leq{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})
. We call the first term generalization error as it’s measuring the difference between
{\mathcal{E}}_{n}
and
{\mathcal{E}}
. We call the second term approximation error as it seeks for a parametrized estimator
u_{{\mathcal{F}}}
that approximates the ground truth solution
u^{\ast}
well in
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
. The upper bounds on generalization and approximation error that we achieved in this paper are listed in Table
2
.

Let
n
denote the number of sampled datapoints. For the generalization error, different from the concurrent upper bound
O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}})

[
14
,
34
]
, we provide a faster and near optimal upper bound
O(\frac{1}{n})
by utilizing the strong convexity of the objective function established in Appendix
A.2
. Via using the Peeling Lemma (Lemma
28
, for completeness, we also provide a proof), we show that the generalization error can be bounded by the fixed point of the local Rademacher complexity

\phi(r)=R_{n}(\{\mathcal{I}(u)\ |\ \|u-u^{\ast}\|_{A}^{2}\leq r\}),

where
R_{n}
is the Rademacher complexity,
\mathcal{I}(u)=\Delta u+Vu,\|\cdot\|_{A}=\|\cdot\|_{H^{2}}
for PINN and
\mathcal{I}(u)=\|\nabla u\|^{2}+Vu,\|\cdot\|_{A}=\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}}
for DRM. Once we show that
\phi(r)
is of magnitude
O(\sqrt{\frac{r}{n}})
, we can achieve the
O(\frac{1}{n})
convergence rate via solving the fix point equation
\phi(r)=O(\sqrt{\frac{r}{n}})=r\Rightarrow r=O(\frac{1}{n})
. Using the solution of the fixed point equation of the local Rademacher complexity to bound the generalization error is a standard result in empirical process
[
3
,
69
,
39
,
79
,
18
]
. The difference is that we used the
H^{1}
/
H^{2}
norm to define the localized set, while the previous papers used the
\ell_{2}
distance. The way to obtain the fast rate generalization bound is using the Peeling Lemma.

We present the error decomposition results as a meta theorem, which is shown in Theorem
17
for PINN (proof in Appendix
A.4
), Theorem
14
for DRM and Theorem
20
for MDRM (proof in Appendix
A.5
), respectively. To make the final rate depend on the data number only, we need bounds of the approximation error in Section
4.2
and bounds of the local Rademacher complexity in Appendix
A.3
.

Approximation Error.

The proof of the approximation results of truncated Fourier series is easy and intuitive. For completeness, we provide it in Section
4.2.1
. The proof of the approximation results of neural networks follows from the fact that a B-spline approximation can be formulated as a ReLU3 neural network efficiently. Our proof basically follows
[
14
,
34
]
, while the only difference is the activation function. Our proof is also very similar to
[
80
,
70
]
, but the depth of our network is of constant magnitude instead of
O(\frac{1}{\log\epsilon})
magnitude, where
{\varepsilon}
denotes the desired approximation error. Such improvement of depth results from the fact that ReLU3 activations can approximate B-splines more easily than the ReLU activations, which is useful in our generalization analysis. Although the proof of the approximation results of neural networks in the Sobolev space is standard, we still list it in Appendix
4.2.2
.

Generalization Error.

As we discussed above, the generalization error can be bounded by the fix point of the local Rademacher complexity,
i.e.
the solution of
\phi(r)=r
. Once we have a
O(\sqrt{\frac{r}{n}})
bound of
\phi(r)
, we can achieve the
O(\frac{1}{n})
fast rate generalization bound we want. It remains to upper bound the the local Rademacher complexity
\phi(r)
.

For the upper bound on the local Rademacher complexity of truncated Fourier series estimators, our proof technique is similar to that of the kernel estimators, whose Rademacher complexity can be bounded by the trace of the Gram matrix (
i.e.
the effective number of basis). One interesting thing we showed is that the final upper bound of the Rademacher complexity localized by
H^{1}
norm is
\sqrt{\frac{\xi^{d-2}r}{n}}
. The term
\xi^{d-2}
in the numerator is smaller than
\xi^{d}
, which is the exact number of Fourier basis. This improvement results from the
H^{1}
norm localization. The detailed proof is given in Lemma
9
, Lemma
10
and Lemma
11
.

For the upper bound on the local Rademacher complexity bound for neural network, we follow
[
63
,
70
,
18
]
to use a Dudley integral theorem and a covering number argument. The covering number arguments are shown in Theorem
39
, Theorem
43
and Theorem
46
. The final local Rademacher complexity bounds are given in Lemma
12
and Lemma
13
. The difference is that the complexity of gradient of ReLU3 activation function makes the covering number depend exponentially on the neural network’s depth. However, the improvement of neural network’s depth to constant magnitude mentioned above in the approximation results saves this problem. One drawback of our proof is that the
H^{1}
norm localization wouldn’t improve the bound for Rademacher complexity and leads to sub-optimal upper bounds. We hypothesize that our bound is tight for sparse neural network and put seeking a right complexity measure of neural network for solving PDEs as a future work.

4.2
Bounding the Approximation Error

4.2.1
Approximation using Truncated Fourier Basis

Lemma 4
.

Given
\alpha>0
and a fixed integer
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. For any function
f\in H^{\alpha}(\Omega)
, we let
f_{\xi}=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}f_{z}\phi_{z}
be the best approximation of
f
in the space
F_{\xi}(\Omega)
. Then for any
0<\beta\leq\alpha
, we have the following inequality:

\displaystyle\|f-f_{\xi}\|_{H^{\beta}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\xi^{-2(\alpha-\beta)}\|f\|_{H^{\alpha}}^{2}.

Proof.

For
f\in H^{\alpha}(\Omega)
, we know the Fourier coefficient satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\geq\xi}|f_{z}|^{2}\|z\|^{2\alpha}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{\alpha}}^{2}.

We directly construct
f_{\xi}=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}f_{z}\phi_{z}
to be the truncated Fourier series of the function
f
, then we have

\displaystyle\|f-f_{\xi}\|_{H^{\beta}(\Omega)}^{2}\lesssim\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\geq\xi}|f_{z}|^{2}\|z\|^{2\beta}\leq\xi^{-2(\alpha-\beta)}\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\geq\xi}|f_{z}|^{2}\|z\|^{2\alpha}\leq\xi^{-2(\alpha-\beta)}\|f\|_{H^{\alpha}}^{2}.

4.2.2
Approximation using Neural Network

In this section, we aim to provide approximation bound for deep neural network. Our proof of the approximation upper bound is based on the observation that the B-spline approximation
[
12
,
64
]
can be formulated as a ReLU3 neural network efficiently
[
70
,
25
,
14
,
34
]
. Although the proof of the approximation of the neural network to the Sobolev spaces is a standard approach, we still demonstrate the proof sketch here.

Definition 5
.

(Univariate and Multivariate B-splines)
Fix an arbitrary integer
l\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. Consider a corresponding uniform partition
\pi_{l}
of
[0,1]
:

\displaystyle\pi_{l}:0=t_{0}^{(l)}<t_{1}^{(l)}<\cdots<t_{l-1}^{(l)}<t_{l}^{(l)}=1,

where
t_{i}^{(l)}=\frac{i}{l}\ (\forall\ 0\leq i\leq l)
. Now for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, we can define an extended partition
\pi_{l,k}
as:

\displaystyle\pi_{l,k}:t_{-k+1}^{(l)}=\cdots t_{-1}^{(l)}=0=t_{0}^{(l)}<t_{1}^{(l)}<\cdots<t_{l-1}^{(l)}<t_{l}^{(l)}=1=t_{l+1}^{(l)}=\cdots=t_{l+k-1}^{(l)}

Based on the extended partition
\pi_{l,k}
, the univariate B-splines of order
k
with respect to partition
\pi_{l}
are defined by:

N_{l,i}^{(k)}(x):=(-1)^{k}(t_{i+k}^{(l)}-t_{i}^{(l)})\cdot\Big[t_{i}^{(l)},\cdots,t_{i+k}^{(l)}\Big]\max\{(x-t),0\}^{k-1},\ x\in[0,1],\ i\in I_{l,k}

(4.2)

where
I_{l,k}=\{-k+1,-k+2,\cdots,l-1\}
and
\Big[t_{i}^{(l)},\cdots,t_{i+k}^{(l)}]
denotes the divided difference operator.

Equivalently, for any
x\in[0,1]
, we can rewrite the univariate B-splines
N_{l,i}^{(k)}(x)
in an explicit form:

N_{l,i}^{(k)}(x)=\begin{cases}\frac{l^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}\sum_{j=0}^{k}(-1)^{j}{k\choose j}\max\Big\{x-\frac{i+j}{l},0\Big\}^{k-1},\ (0\leq i\leq l-k+1)\\
\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}a_{ij}\max\Big\{x-\frac{j}{l},0\Big\}^{k-1}+\sum_{n=1}^{k-2}b_{in}x^{n}+b_{i0},\ (-k+1\leq i\leq 0)\\
\sum_{j=l-k+1}^{l}c_{ij}\max\Big\{x-\frac{j}{l},0\Big\}^{k-1},\ (l-k+1\leq i\leq l-1)\end{cases}

(4.3)

where
\{a_{ij}\ |\ -k+1\leq i\leq 0,\ 0\leq j\leq k-1\}
,
\{b_{in}\ |\ -k+1\leq i\leq 0,\ 1\leq n\leq k-2\}
and
\{c_{ij}\ |\ l-k+1\leq i\leq l-1,\ l-k+1\leq j\leq l-1\}
are some fixed constants.

For any index vector
\bm{i}=(i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{d})\in I_{l,k}^{d}
, we can define a corresponding multivariate B-spline as a product of univariate B-splines:

N_{l,\bm{i}}^{(k)}(\bm{x}):=\Pi_{j=1}^{d}N_{l,i_{j}}^{(k)}(x_{j}).

(4.4)

Definition 6
.

(Interpolation Operator
[
64
]
)
Take some domain
\Omega\subset[0,1]^{d}
and two arbitrary integers
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. Consider the extended partition
\pi_{l,k}
and the corresponding set of multivariate B-splines
\{N_{l,\bm{i}}^{(k)}(x)\}_{\bm{i}\in I_{l,k}^{d}}
defined in Definition
5
. For any
\bm{i}\in I_{l,k}^{d}
, we define the domain
\Omega_{\bm{i}}:=\{\bm{x}\in\Omega:x_{j}\in[t_{i_{j}},t_{i_{j}+k}],\ 1\leq j\leq d\}
. There exists a set of linear functionals
\{\lambda_{\bm{i}}\}_{\bm{i}\in I_{k,l}^{d}}
, where
\lambda_{\bm{i}}:L^{1}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\ (\forall\ \bm{i}\in I_{k,l}^{d})
, such that for any
\bm{i}\in I_{k,l}^{d}
and
p\in[1,\infty]
, we have:

\lambda_{\bm{i}}(N_{l,\bm{j}}^{(k)})=\delta_{\bm{i},\bm{j}}\text{ and }|\lambda_{\bm{i}}(f)|\leq 9^{d(k-1)}(2k+1)^{d}\Big(\frac{k}{l}\Big)^{-\frac{d}{p}}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega_{\bm{i}})},\ \forall\ f\in L^{p}(\Omega).

(4.5)

The corresponding interpolation operator
Q_{k,l}
is defined as:

\displaystyle Q_{k,l}f:=\sum_{\bm{i}\in I_{k,l}^{d}}\lambda_{i}(f)N_{l,\bm{i}}^{(k)},\ \forall\ f\in L^{1}(\Omega).

Theorem 7
.

[
[
64
]
]

Fix
f\in W^{s}(\Omega)
with
\Omega\subseteq[0,1]^{d},s\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
and
p\in[1,\infty)
. Then for any
k,l,r\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
with
k\geq s
and
0\leq r\leq s
, we have that there exists some constant
C=C(k,s,r,p,d)
, such that:

\displaystyle\|f-Q_{k,l}f\|_{H^{r}(\Omega)}\leq C\Big(\frac{1}{l}\Big)^{s-r}\|f\|_{H^{s}(\Omega)}.

Theorem 8
.

(Approximation result of Deep Neural Network)

Fix some dimension
d\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, some domain
\Omega\subseteq[0,1]^{d}
. We pick some
l=N^{\frac{1}{d}}\geq 2
, for any
s,r\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
with
0\leq r\leq s
and any function
u^{\ast}\in H^{s}(\Omega)
, there exists some sparse Deep Neural Network
u_{\text{DNN}}\in\Phi(L,W,S,B)
with
L=O(1),W=O(N),S=O(N),B=O(N)
, such that:

\|u_{DNN}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{r}(\Omega)}\lesssim N^{-\frac{s-r}{d}}\|u^{\ast}\|_{H^{s}(\Omega)}.

(4.6)

Proof.

We firstly show that the given function
u^{\ast}
can be approximated well by some linear combination of multivariate splines, which is denoted by
u_{\text{sp}}
. Note that
N
is assumed to be sufficiently large. Hence, we may pick
l=\lceil N^{\frac{1}{d}}\rceil=\Theta(N^{\frac{1}{d}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
to be the partition size of the B-splines. Moreover, by picking
k=4
and
p=2
in Theorem
7
, we have that the linear combination
u_{\text{sp}}:=Q_{4,l}u^{\ast}=\sum_{\bm{i}\in I_{4,l}^{d}}\lambda_{i}(u^{\ast})N_{l,\bm{i}}^{(4)}
satisfies:

\displaystyle\|u^{\ast}-u_{\text{sp}}\|_{H^{r}(\Omega)}=\|u^{\ast}-Q_{4,l}u^{\ast}\|_{H^{r}(\Omega)}\leq C\Big(\frac{1}{l}\Big)^{s-r}\|u^{\ast}\|_{H^{s}(\Omega)}=CN^{-\frac{s-r}{d}}\|u^{\ast}\|_{H^{s}(\Omega)}.

We will then show that the linear combination
u_{\text{sp}}=\sum_{\bm{i}\in I_{4,l}^{d}}\lambda_{i}(f)N_{l,\bm{i}}^{(4)}
can be implemented by some Deep Neural Network
u_{\text{DNN}}\in\Phi(L,W,S,B)
with
L=O(1),W=O(N),S=O(N)
and
B=O(\log N)
. Firstly, note that for
x\geq 0
, both
x
and
x^{2}
can be expressed in terms of the ReLU3 activation function
\eta_{3}
with no error:

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{12}[\eta_{3}(x+3)-5\eta_{3}(x+2)+7\eta_{3}(x+1)-3\eta_{3}(x)+6]

\displaystyle x^{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6}[\eta_{3}(x+2)-4\eta_{3}(x+1)+3\eta_{3}(x)-4]

Applying the explicit formula listed in equation
4.3
implies that for any
-3\leq i\leq l-1
, the univariate B-spline function
N_{l,i}^{(4)}(x)\ (x\in[0,1])
can be implemented by some ReLU3 Deep Neural Network
v_{\text{DNN}}
with both scalar input and scalar output. We have that for
v_{\text{DNN}}
, the depth
L_{v}
is
2
and the maximum width
W_{v}
is upper bounded by
11
.

Secondly, for any
x,y\geq 0
, we have that the product operation
x\cdot y
can be expressed in terms of the ReLU3 activation function
\eta_{3}
with no error:

\displaystyle x\cdot y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[(x+y)^{2}-x^{2}-y^{2}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{12}\Big[\eta_{3}(x+y+2)-4\eta_{3}(x+y+1)+3\eta_{3}(x+y)

\displaystyle-\eta_{3}(x+2)+4\eta_{3}(x+1)-3\eta_{3}(x)-\eta_{3}(y+2)+4\eta_{3}(y+1)-3\eta_{3}(y)+4\Big]

In
[
64
]
, it has been proved that the B-splines are always non-negative, i.e
N_{l,i}^{(4)}(x)\geq 0,\ \forall\ x\in[0,1]
. Therefore, by multiplying the non-negative univariate B-splines, we can implement any multivariate B-spline
N_{l,\bm{i}}^{(4)}=\Pi_{j=1}^{d}N_{l,i_{j}}^{(4)}(x_{j})
with some ReLU3 Deep Neural Network
p_{\text{DNN}}
. We have that for
p_{\text{DNN}}
, the depth
L_{p}=\lceil\log_{2}d\rceil+2
and the maximum width
W_{p}=\max\{11d,\frac{9}{2}d\}
.

Hence, we can further claim that
u^{\ast}=\sum_{\bm{i}\in I_{4,l}^{d}}\lambda_{i}(u^{\ast})N_{l,\bm{i}}^{(4)}
, which is a linear combination of the multivariate B-splines
N_{l,\bm{i}}^{(4)}
, can be implemented by some ReLU3 Deep Neural Network
u_{\text{DNN}}
. It remains to check that
u_{\text{DNN}}\in\Phi(L,W,S,B)
with
L=O(1),W=O(N),S=O(N)
and
B=O(N)
. Note that
we can ensure that the hidden layers of
u_{\text{DNN}}
are of the same dimension
W
by adding inactive neurons.

For the depth
L
of
u_{\text{DNN}}
, we have that
L
is equal to
L_{p}+1
, where
L_{p}
denotes the depth of the ReLU3 Deep Neural Network
p_{\text{DNN}}
. Thus, we have
L=L_{p}+1=\lceil\log_{2}d\rceil+3
, which implies that
L=O(1)
.

For the width
W
of
u_{\text{DNN}}
, we have that
W\leq|I_{k,l}^{d}|W_{p}
, where
W_{p}
denotes the width of the ReLU3 Deep Neural Network
p_{\text{DNN}}
. This implies:

\displaystyle W\leq|I_{k,l}^{d}|\times 11d=11d(l+k)^{d}=11d(l+4)^{d}=O(l^{d})\Rightarrow W=O(N)

For the sparsity constraint
S
of
u_{\text{DNN}}
, starting from the third layer, the number of activated neurons is half of the number of activated neurons at previous layer. This yields the following upper bound on
S
:

\displaystyle S\leq 2(W+W+\sum_{j=0}^{L-2}\frac{W}{2^{j}})\leq 8W\Rightarrow S=O(W)=O(N)

For the norm constraint
B
of
u_{\text{DNN}}
, we have the following upper bound on
B
from equation
4.3
and equation
4.5
:

\displaystyle B=O(\max\{l^{k-1},\sup_{\bm{i}\in I_{k,l}^{d}}\lambda_{\bm{i}}(u^{\ast})\})=O(\max\{l^{3},l^{d}\})=O(N)

Now we have shown that parameters
L,W,S,B
of the Deep Neural Network
u_{\text{DNN}}
are of the desired magnitude, which completes our proof.

4.3
Bounding the Local Rademacher Complexity

4.3.1
Local Rademacher Complexity of Truncated Fourier Basis

In this subsection, we aim to bound the local Rademacher complexity of the Truncated Fourier Basis estimator. The proof is standard and we put the proof in the appendix.

Lemma 9
.

(Local Rademacher Complexity of Localized Truncated Fourier Series)
For a fixed
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, we consider a localized class of functions
{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)=\Big\{f\in F_{\xi}(\Omega)\ \Big|\ \|f\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\rho\Big\}
, where
\rho>0
is fixed. Then we have the following upper bound on the local Rademacher complexity:

R_{n}({\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega))={\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f(X_{i})\ \Big|\ X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]\right]\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d-2}{2}}.

(4.7)

Lemma 10
.

(Local Rademacher Complexity of Localized Truncated Fourier Series’ Gradient)
For a fixed
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, we consider a localized class of functions
{\mathcal{G}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)=\{\|\nabla f\|\ |\ f\in F_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)\}
, where
\rho>0
is fixed. Then for any sample
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\Omega
, we have the following upper bound on the local Rademacher complexity:

R_{n}({\mathcal{G}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega))={\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\|\nabla f(X_{i})\|\ \Big|\ X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]\right]\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d}{2}}.

(4.8)

Lemma 11
.

(Local Rademacher Complexity of Localized Truncated Fourier Series’ Laplacian)
For a fixed
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, we consider a localized class of functions
{\bm{J}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega):=\Big\{f\in F_{\xi}(\Omega)\ \Big|\ \|f\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\rho\Big\}
, where
\rho>0
is fixed. Correspondingly, we define a localized class of Laplacians
{\bm{K}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega):=\{\Delta f\ |\ f\in J_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)\}
. Then for any sample
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\Omega
, we have the following upper bound on the local Rademacher complexity:

R_{n}({\bm{K}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega))={\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\Delta f(X_{i})\ \Big|\ X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]\right]\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d}{2}}.

(4.9)

4.3.2
Local Rademacher Complexity of Deep Neural Networks

In this subsection, we aim to bound the local Rademacher complexity of the Neural Network estimator. Informally, we showed that the local Rademacher complexity is at the scale of
\sqrt{\frac{N}{n}}
, where
N
is the number of neuron of a neural network. For simplicity, we put the proof in the appendix.

Lemma 12
(Local Rademacher Complexity Bound for Deep Ritz Method).

Consider a Deep Neural Network space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)=\Phi(L,W,S,B)
with
L=O(1),W=O(N),S=O(N)
and
B=O(N)
, where
N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
is fixed to be sufficiently large. Moreover, assume that the gradients and function value of
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega),V
and
f
are uniformly bounded

\max\Big\{\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\|u^{\ast}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},V_{max},\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\Big\}\leq C.

(4.10)

For any
\rho>0
, we consider a localized set
L_{\rho}
defined by:

{\bm{L}}_{\rho}(\Omega):=\{u:u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega),\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\leq\rho\}.

Then for any
\rho\gtrsim n^{-2}
, the Rademacher complexity of a localized function space
{\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega):=\Big\{h:=|\Omega|\cdot\left[\frac{1}{2}\Big(\|\nabla u\|^{2}-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|^{2}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}V(|u|^{2}-|u^{\ast}|^{2})-f(u-u^{\ast})\right]\ \ \Big|\ u\in L_{\rho}(\Omega)\Big\}
can be upper bounded by a sub-root function

\phi(\rho):=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{S3^{L}\rho}{n}\log\left(BWn\right)}\right).

i.e.
we have

\phi(4\rho)\leq 2\phi(\rho)\text{ and }R_{n}({\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega))\leq\phi(\rho).\

(4.11)

holds for all
\rho\gtrsim n^{-2}
.

Lemma 13
(Local Rademacher Complexity Bound for Physics Informed Neural Network).

Consider a Deep Neural Network space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)=\Phi(L,W,S,B)
with
L=O(1),W=O(N),S=O(N)
and
B=O(N)
, where
N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
is fixed to be sufficiently large. Moreover, assume that the gradients and function value of
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega),V
and
f
are uniformly bounded

\max\Big\{\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\|\Delta u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\|u^{\ast}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\|\Delta u^{\ast}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},V_{max},\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\Big\}\leq C.

(4.12)

For any
\rho>0
, we consider a localized set
M_{\rho}
defined by:

{\mathcal{M}}_{\rho}(\Omega):=\{u:u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega),\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\leq\rho\}.

Then for any
\rho\gtrsim n^{-2}
, the Rademacher complexity of a localized function space
{\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}(\Omega):=\Big\{h:=|\Omega|\cdot\left[(\Delta u-Vu+f)^{2}-(\Delta u^{\ast}-Vu^{\ast}+f)^{2}\right]\ \ \Big|\ u\in M_{\rho}(\Omega)\Big\}
can be upper bounded by a sub-root function

\phi(\rho):=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{S3^{L}\rho}{n}\log\left(BWn\right)}\right).

i.e.
we have

\phi(4\rho)\leq 2\phi(\rho)\text{ and }R_{n}({\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}(\Omega))\leq\phi(\rho).\

(4.13)

holds for all
\rho\gtrsim n^{-2}
.

4.4
Final Upper Bound

Deep Ritz Methods.

In this subsection, we provide the proof of upper bounds for DRM. We first provide a meta-theorem to illustrate the approximation and generalization decomposition with a
O(1/n)
fast rate generalization bound
[
3
,
78
]
. Then we use truncated fourier basis estimator and neural network estimator as example to obtain the final rate.

Theorem 14
(Meta-theorem for Upper Bounds of Deep Ritz Methods).

Let
u^{\ast}\in H^{s}(\Omega)
denote the true solution to the PDE model with Dirichlet boundary condition:

\displaystyle-\Delta u+Vu

\displaystyle=f\text{ on }\Omega,

(4.14)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega,

where
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
V\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
with
0<V_{\min}\leq V(x)\leq V_{\max}>0
.
For a fixed function space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
, consider the empirical loss induced by the Deep Ritz Method:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big[|\Omega|\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{2}|\nabla u(X_{j})|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}V(X_{j})|u(X_{j})|^{2}-f(X_{j})u(X_{j})\Big)\Big],

(4.15)

where
\{X_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
are datapoints uniformly sampled from the domain
\Omega
. Then the Deep Ritz estimator associated with function space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
is defined as the minimizer of
{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u)
over the function space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
:

\displaystyle\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}}=\min_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u)

Moreover, we assume that there exists some constant
C>0
such that all function
u
in the function space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
, the real solution
u^{\ast}
and
f,V
satisfy the following two conditions.

•

The gradients and function value are uniformly bounded

\max\Big\{\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\|u^{\ast}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},V_{max},\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\Big\}\leq C.

(4.16)

•

All the functions in the function space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
satisfies the boundary condition

u=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega.

At the the same time, for any
\rho>0
, we assume the Rademacher complexity of a localized function space
{\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega):=\Big\{h:=|\Omega|\cdot\left[\frac{1}{2}\Big(|\nabla u|^{2}-|\nabla u^{\ast}|^{2}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}V(|u|^{2}-|u^{\ast}|^{2})-f(u-u^{\ast})\right]\ \ \Big|\ \|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\leq\rho\Big\}
can be upper bounded by a sub-root function
\phi=\phi(\rho):[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
,
i.e.

\phi(4\rho)\leq 2\phi(\rho)\text{ and }R_{n}({\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega))\leq\phi(\rho)\ (\forall\ \rho>0).

(4.17)

For all constant
t>0
. We denote
r^{*}
to be the solution of the fix point equation of local Rademacher complexity
r=\phi(r)
. There exists a constant
C_{p}
such that for probability
1-C_{p}\exp(-t)
, we have the following upper bound for the Deep Ritz Estimator

\|\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim\inf_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)+\max\Big\{r^{*},\frac{t}{n}\Big\}.

Proof.

To upper bound the excess risk
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}:={\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})
, following
[
78
,
47
,
14
]
, we decompose the excess risk into approximation error and generalization error with probability
1-e^{-t}
:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}(\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}})={\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})

\displaystyle=\big[{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}})\big]+\big[{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})\big]

(4.18)

\displaystyle+\big[{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})\big]+\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]

\displaystyle\leq\big[{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}})\big]+\big[{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})\big]+\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]

\displaystyle\leq\big[{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})+{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u^{\ast})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}})\big]

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{2}\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]+\frac{t}{2n},

where the expectation is on all sampled data. The inequality of the third line is because the
u
is the minimizer of the empirical loss
{\mathcal{E}}_{n}
in the solution set
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
, so we have
{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u)\leq{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})
. The last inequality is based on the Bernstein inequality. The variance of
h=|\Omega|\cdot\left[\frac{1}{2}\Big(|\nabla u|^{2}-|\nabla u^{\ast}|^{2}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}V(|u|^{2}-|u^{\ast}|^{2})-f(u-u^{\ast})\right]
can be bounded by
\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]
due to the strong convexity of the variation objective (
4.20
). According to the Bernstein inequality, we know with probability
1-e^{-t}
we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u^{\ast})-{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})+{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})\leq\sqrt{\frac{t\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]}{n}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]+\frac{t}{2n}.

Note that
4.18
holds for all function lies in the function space
{\mathcal{F}}
. Thus, we can take
u_{{\mathcal{F}}}:=\arg\min_{u_{0}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{0})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)
and finally get

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})+{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u^{\ast})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u)}_{\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{gen}}}+\frac{3}{2}\underbrace{\inf_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)}_{\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}}+\frac{t}{2n}.

This inequality decompose the excess risk to the generalization error
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{gen}}:={\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})+{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u^{\ast})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}})
and the approximation error
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}=\inf_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)
.

We’ll focus on providing fast rate upper bounds of the generalization error for the two estimators using the localization technique
[
3
,
78
]
. To achieve the fast generalization bound, we focus on the following normalized empirical process

\displaystyle\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega):=\big\{\tilde{h}(x):=\frac{\mathbb{E}[h]-h(x)}{{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r}\ |\ h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)\big\}\ (r>0).

First, we try to bound the expectation of the normalized empirical process. Applying the Symmetrization Lemma
24
, we can first bound the expectation as

\displaystyle\sup_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{S}_{r}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}_{x^{\prime}}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{i}^{\prime})\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{x^{\prime}}\left[\sup_{h\in S(\Omega)}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{h(x_{i}^{\prime})-{\mathbb{E}}[h]}{{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r}\Big|\right]\leq 2R_{n}(\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega)).

where the function class
\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega)
is defined as:

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega):=\big\{\hat{h}(x):=\frac{h(x)}{{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r}\ |\ h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)\big\},

where
{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)=\Big\{h:=|\Omega|\cdot\left[\frac{1}{2}\Big(|\nabla u|^{2}-|\nabla u^{\ast}|^{2}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}V(|u|^{2}-|u^{\ast}|^{2})-f(u-u^{\ast})\right]\Big\}.
Then Applying the Peeling Lemma to any function
h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)
helps us upper bound the local Rademacher complexity
R_{n}(\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega))
with the function
\phi
defined in equation
4.17
:

R_{n}(\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega))={\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{x}\Big[\sup_{h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)}\frac{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}h(x_{i})}{{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r}\Big]\right]\leq\frac{4\phi(r)}{r}.

Combining all inequalities derived above yields:

\sup_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{S}_{r}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}_{x^{\prime}}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{i}^{\prime})\right]\leq 2R_{n}(\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega))\leq\frac{8\phi(r)}{r}\ (r>0).

(4.19)

Secondly we’ll apply the Talagrand concentration inequality, which requires us to verify the condition needed. We will first check that the expectation value
{\mathbb{E}}[h]
is always non-negative for any
h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[h]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\int_{\Omega}|\Omega|\cdot(\frac{1}{2}|\nabla u(x)|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}V(x)|u(x)|^{2}-f(x)u(x))dx

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\int_{\Omega}|\Omega|\cdot(\frac{1}{2}|\nabla u^{\star}(x)|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}V(x)|u^{\star}(x)|^{2}-f(x)u^{\star}(x))dx

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\geq 0\Rightarrow{\mathbb{E}}[h]\geq 0.

We will proceed to verify that any
\tilde{h}=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h]-h}{{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega)
is of bounded inf-norm. We need to prove that any
h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)
is of bounded inf-norm beforehand. Using boundedness condition listed in equation
4.16
implies:

\displaystyle\|h\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=|\Omega|\|\frac{1}{2}\Big(|\nabla u|^{2}-|\nabla u^{\ast}|^{2}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}V(|u|^{2}-|u^{\ast}|^{2})-f(u-u^{\ast})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\Omega|}{2}\Big(\|\nabla u\|_{\infty}^{2}+\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|_{\infty}^{2}\Big)+\frac{|\Omega|}{2}V_{\text{max}}\Big(\|u\|_{\infty}^{2}+\|u^{\ast}\|_{\infty}^{2}\Big)+|\Omega|\|f\|_{\infty}\Big(\|u\|_{\infty}+\|u^{\ast}\|_{\infty}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\Omega|}{2}\times 2C^{2}+\frac{|\Omega|}{2}V_{\text{max}}\times 2C^{2}+2|\Omega|C^{2}=|\Omega|(V_{\text{max}}+3)C^{2}

By taking
M:=|\Omega|(V_{\text{max}}+3)C^{2}
, we then have
\|h\|_{\infty}\leq M
for all
h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)
. Note that the denominator can be lower bounded by
|{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r|\geq r>0
. Combining these two inequalities help us upper bound the inf-norm
\|\tilde{h}\|_{\infty}=\sup_{x\in\Omega}|\tilde{h}(x)|
as follows:

\displaystyle\|\tilde{h}\|_{\infty}=\frac{\|{\mathbb{E}}[h]-h\|_{\infty}}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r|}\leq\frac{2\|h\|_{\infty}}{r}\leq\frac{2M}{r}=:\beta.

We will then check the normalized functions
\frac{\mathbb{E}[h]-h(x)}{{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r}
in
\tilde{S}_{r}(\Omega)
have bounded second moment, which is satisfied because of the regularity results of the PDE. We aim to show that there exist some constants
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}>0
, such that for any
h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)
, the following inequality holds:

\alpha\mathbb{E}[h^{2}]\leq\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\alpha^{\prime}\mathbb{E}[h].

(4.20)

The RHS of the inequality follows from strong convexity of the DRM objective function proved in Theorem
22
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[h]={\mathcal{E}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})\geq\frac{\min\{1,V_{\text{min}}\}}{4}\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}

The LHS of the inequality follows from boundedness condition listed in equation
4.16
and the QM-AM inequality:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[h^{2}]

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\left[\frac{1}{2}\Big(|\nabla u|^{2}-|\nabla u^{\ast}|^{2}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}V(|u|^{2}-|u^{\ast}|^{2})-f(u-u^{\ast})\right]^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{4}\int_{\Omega}\Big(|\nabla u|^{2}-|\nabla u^{\ast}|^{2}\Big)^{2}dx+\frac{3}{4}\int_{\Omega}V^{2}(|u|^{2}-|u^{\ast}|^{2})^{2}dx+3\int_{\Omega}f^{2}(u-u^{\ast})^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{4}\int_{\Omega}\Big||\nabla u|-|\nabla u^{\ast}|\Big|^{2}(|\nabla u|+|\nabla u^{\ast}|)^{2}dx+\frac{3}{4}V_{\text{max}}^{2}\int_{\Omega}\Big||u|-|u^{\ast}|\Big|^{2}(|u|+|u^{\ast}|)^{2}dx

\displaystyle+3C^{2}\int_{\Omega}(u-u^{\ast})^{2}dx\leq 3C^{2}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u-\nabla u^{\ast}|^{2}dx+3C^{2}(1+V_{\text{max}}^{2})\int_{\Omega}|u-u^{\ast}|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq 3C^{2}(1+V_{\text{max}}^{2})\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}

By picking
\alpha^{\prime}=\frac{4}{\min\{1,V_{\text{min}}\}}
and
\alpha=\frac{1}{3C^{2}(1+V_{\text{max}}^{2})}
, we have finished proving inequality
4.20
. Then we can can upper bound the expectation
{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{h}^{2}]
as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\tilde{h}^{2}]=\frac{\mathbb{E}[(h-\mathbb{E}[h])^{2}]}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r|^{2}}=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h^{2}]-{\mathbb{E}}[h]^{2}}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r|^{2}}\leq\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h^{2}]}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r|^{2}}.

Using the fact that
{\mathbb{E}}[h]\geq 0
and inequality
4.20
, we can lower bound the denominator
|{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r|^{2}
as follows:

\displaystyle|{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r|^{2}\geq 2{\mathbb{E}}[h]r\geq\frac{2r\alpha}{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}[h^{2}].

Therefore, we can deduce that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\tilde{h}^{2}]\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[h^{2}]}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r|^{2}}\leq\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h^{2}]}{\frac{2r\alpha}{\alpha^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}[h^{2}]}=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2r\alpha}=:\sigma^{2}.

Hence, any function in the localized class
\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega)
is of bounded second moment.

It is easy to check that for any
\tilde{h}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{h}]=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h]-{\mathbb{E}}[h]}{{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r}=0,

i.e.
any function in the localized class
\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega)
is of zero mean.

Now we have verified that any function
\tilde{h}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega)
satisfies all the required conditions. By taking
\mu
to be the uniform distribution on the domain
\Omega
and applying Talagrand’s Concentration inequality given in Lemma
27
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\sup_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{i})\geq 2\sup_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}_{x^{\prime}}\Big[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{i}^{\prime})\Big]+\sqrt{\frac{2t\sigma^{2}}{n}}+\frac{2t\beta}{n}\right]\leq e^{-t}.

By using the upper bound deduced above and plugging in the expressions of
\beta
and
\sigma
, we can rewrite Talagrand’s Concentration Inequality in the following way. With probability at least
1-e^{-t}
, the inequality below holds:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{i})\leq\sup_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}_{x^{\prime}}\Big[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{i}^{\prime})\Big]+\sqrt{\frac{2t\sigma^{2}}{n}}+\frac{2t\beta}{n}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{16\phi(r)}{r}+\sqrt{\frac{t\alpha^{\prime}}{n\alpha r}}+\frac{4Mt}{nr}=:\psi(r).

Let’s pick the critical radius
r_{0}
to be:

\displaystyle r_{0}=\max\{2^{14}r^{\ast},\frac{24Mt}{n},\frac{36\alpha^{\prime}t}{\alpha n}\}.

(4.21)

Note that concavity of the function
\phi
implies that
\phi(r)\leq r
for any
r\geq r^{\ast}
. Combining this with the first inequality listed in
4.17
yields:

\displaystyle\frac{16\phi(r)}{r}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{11}\phi(\frac{r_{0}}{2^{14}})}{2^{14}\frac{r_{0}}{2^{14}}}=\frac{1}{8}\times\frac{\phi(\frac{r_{0}}{2^{14}})}{\frac{r_{)}}{2^{14}}}\leq\frac{1}{8}.

On the other hand, applying equation
4.21
yields:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}t}{n\alpha r_{0}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}t}{n\alpha}\frac{\alpha n}{36\alpha^{\prime}t}}=\frac{1}{6},

\displaystyle\frac{4Mt}{nr_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4Mt}{n}\times\frac{n}{24Mt}=\frac{1}{6}.

Summing the three inequalities above implies:

\displaystyle\psi(r_{0})=\frac{16\phi(r_{0})}{r_{0}}+\sqrt{\frac{t\alpha^{\prime}}{n\alpha r_{0}}}+\frac{4Mt}{nr_{0}}\leq\frac{1}{8}+\frac{1}{6}+\frac{1}{6}<\frac{1}{2}.

By picking
r=r_{0}
, we can further deduce that for any function
u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
, the following inequality holds with probability
1-e^{-t}
:

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathcal{E}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u)+{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u^{\ast})}{{\mathcal{E}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})+r_{0}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\psi(r_{0})<\frac{1}{2}.

Multiplying the denominator on both sides indicates:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{gen}}={\mathcal{E}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u)+{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u^{\ast})\leq\frac{1}{2}\Big[{\mathcal{E}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})\Big]+\frac{1}{2}r_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}+\frac{1}{2}r_{0}.

Substituting the upper bound above into the decomposition
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}\leq\Delta E_{\text{gen}}+\frac{3}{2}\Delta E_{\text{app}}+\frac{t}{2n}
yields that with probability
1-e^{-t}
, we have:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}\leq\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{gen}}+\frac{3}{2}\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}+\frac{t}{2n}\leq\frac{1}{2}\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}+\frac{1}{2}r_{0}+\frac{3}{2}\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}+\frac{t}{2n}.

Simplifying the inequality above yields that with probability
1-e^{-t}
, we have:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\leq r_{0}+3\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}+\frac{t}{n}=3\inf_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)+\max\{2^{14}r^{\ast},24M\frac{t}{n},\frac{36\alpha^{\prime}}{\alpha}\frac{t}{n}\}+\frac{t}{n}

\displaystyle\lesssim\inf_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)+\max\Big\{r^{*},\frac{t}{n}\Big\}

Moreover, using strong convexity of the DRM objective function proved in Theorem
22
implies:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}={\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})\geq\frac{\min\{1,V_{\text{min}}\}}{4}\|\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}

Combining the two bounds above yields that with probability
1-e^{-t}
, we have:

\displaystyle\|\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\lesssim\inf_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)+\max\Big\{r^{*},\frac{t}{n}\Big\}

Theorem 15
.

(Final Upper Bound of DRM with Deep Neural Network Estimator)
With proper assumptions, consider the sparse Deep Neural Network function space
\Phi(L,W,S,B)
with parameters
L=O(1),\ W=O(n^{\frac{d}{d+2s-2}}),\ S=O(n^{\frac{d}{d+2s-2}}),\ B=O(1)
, then the Deep ritz estimator
\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}}^{\text{DNN}}=\min_{u\in\Phi(L,W,S,B)}{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{\text{DRM}}(u)
satisfies the following upper bound with high probability:

\displaystyle\|\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}}^{\text{DNN}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim n^{-\frac{2s-2}{d+2s-2}}\log n.

Proof.

On the one hand, by taking
s=1
and
p=2
in Theorem
8
proved above, we have that there exists some Deep Neural Network
u_{\text{DNN}}\in\Phi(L,W,S,B)
with
L=O(1),W=O(N),S=O(N),B=O(N)
, such that.

\displaystyle\|u_{\text{DNN}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq N^{-\frac{2s-2}{d}}\|u^{\ast}\|_{H^{s}(\Omega)}.

Applying strong convexity of the DRM objective function proved in Section
2.1
further implies:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}\lesssim\|u_{\text{DNN}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq N^{-\frac{2s-2}{d}}.

On the other hand, from Lemma
12
proved above, we know that the function
\phi(\rho)
that upper bounds the local Rademacher complexity of the Deep Neural Network space is of the same magnitude as
\sqrt{\frac{S3^{L}\rho}{n}\log\left(BWn\right)}
. By plugging in the magnitudes of
L,W,S,B
, we can determine the critical radius
r^{\ast}
:

\sqrt{\frac{r^{\ast}3^{L}S}{n}\log(BWn)}\simeq\sqrt{\frac{r^{\ast}N}{n}(2\log N+\log n)}\simeq r^{\ast}\Rightarrow r^{\ast}\simeq\frac{N(\log N+\log n)}{n}.

Combining the two bounds above with Theorem
14
yields that with high probability, we have:

\|\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}}^{\text{DNN}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}+\hat{r}\lesssim N^{-\frac{2(s-1)}{d}}+\frac{N(\log N+\log n)}{n}.

By equating the two terms above, we can solve for the optimal
N
that yields the desired bound:

N^{-\frac{2(s-1)}{d}}\simeq\frac{N}{n}\Rightarrow N\simeq n^{\frac{d}{d+2s-2}}.

Plugging in the optimal
N
gives us the magnitudes of the four parameters
L=O(1),\ W=O(n^{\frac{d}{d+2s-2}}),\ S=O(n^{\frac{d}{d+2s-2}}),\ B=O(n^{\frac{d}{d+2s-2}})
, as well as the final rate:

\|\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}}^{\text{DNN}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim N^{-\frac{2(s-1)}{d}}+\frac{N\log N}{n}\lesssim n^{-\frac{2(s-1)}{d+2(s-1)}}\log n.

Theorem 16
.

(Final Upper Bound of DRM with Truncated Fourier Series Estimator)With proper assumptions, consider the Deep Ritz objective with a plug in Fourier Series estimator
\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}}^{\text{Fourier}}=\min_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\xi}(\Omega)}{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{\text{DRM}}(u)
with
\xi=\Theta(n^{\frac{1}{d+2s-2}})
, then with high probability we have

\displaystyle\|\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}}^{\text{Fourier}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim n^{-\frac{2s-2}{d+2s-2}}.

Proof.

On the one hand, from Lemma
9
and Lemma
10
proved above, we know that the function
\phi(\rho)
that upper bounds the local Rademacher complexity for Truncated Fourier Series can be dominated by the term

\displaystyle R_{n}({\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega))

\displaystyle\lesssim R_{n}\left(\Big\{u-u^{\ast}:u\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega),\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\sqrt{\rho}\Big\}\right)

(4.22)

\displaystyle+R_{n}\left(\Big\{\|\nabla u-\nabla u^{\ast}\|:u\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega),\Big\|\nabla u-\nabla u^{\ast}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\sqrt{\rho}\Big\}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}(u(X_{i})-u^{\ast}(X_{i}))\Big|\|u-\Pi_{\xi}u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\rho\Big]\right]

\displaystyle+{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\|\nabla u(X_{i})-\nabla u^{\ast}(X_{i})\|\ \Big|\|u-\Pi_{\xi}u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\rho\Big]\right]

\displaystyle+{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\|\nabla\Pi_{>\xi}u^{\ast}(X_{i})\|\Big]\right]+{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\Pi_{>\xi}u^{\ast}(X_{i})\Big]\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f(X_{i})\ \Big|\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\rho\Big]\right]

\displaystyle+{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\|\nabla f(X_{i})\|\ \Big|\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\rho\Big]\right]

\displaystyle+{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\|\nabla\Pi_{\xi}u^{\ast}(X_{i})\|\Big]\right]+{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\Pi_{>\xi}u^{\ast}(X_{i})\Big]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d}{2}}+\sqrt{\frac{\|\Pi_{\xi}f\|_{H^{1}}^{2}}{n}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d}{2}}+\sqrt{\frac{\xi^{-2(s-1)}}{n}}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d}{2}}+\frac{1}{n}+\xi^{-2(s-1)},

where
\Pi_{\xi}u:=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}u_{z}\phi_{z}(x)
is the projection to the Fourier basis whose frequency is smaller than
\xi
and
\Pi_{\xi}u:=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}u_{z}\phi_{z}(x)
is the projection to the Fourier basis whose frequency is larger than
\xi
. Then, the critical radius
\hat{r}
can be determined as follows:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d}{2}}+\frac{1}{n}+\xi^{-2(s-1)}\simeq\rho\Rightarrow\hat{r}\simeq\frac{\xi^{d}}{n}+\frac{1}{n}+\xi^{-2(s-1)},

On the other hand, by taking
\alpha=s
and
\beta=1
in Lemma
4
and applying strong convexity of the DRM objective function proved in Theorem
22
, we can upper bound the approximation error
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}
as below:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}\lesssim\xi^{-2(s-1)},

By equating the two terms above, we can solve for
\xi
that yields the desired bound:

\displaystyle\frac{\xi^{d}}{n}\simeq\xi^{-2(s-1)}\Rightarrow\xi\simeq n^{\frac{1}{d+2s-2}},

Plugging in the expression of
\xi
gives the final upper bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}[\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{n}]\lesssim\hat{r}+\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}\lesssim\frac{\xi^{d}}{n}+\xi^{-2(s-1)}\simeq n^{-\frac{2s-2}{d+2s-2}}.

Physics Informed Neural Network.

Then we aim to provide the upper bound for the Physics Informed Neural Network with a Similar meta-theorem followed by upper bounds of DNN and truncated Fourier Estimators. For simplicity, we drop the proof of the meta-theorem to appendix for all the proof follows the similar idea of the DRM one.

Theorem 17
(Meta-theorem for Upper Bounds of Physics Informed Neural Network).

Let
u^{\ast}\in H^{s}(\Omega)
denote the true solution to the PDE model with Dirichlet boundary condition:

\displaystyle-\Delta u+Vu

\displaystyle=f\text{ on }\Omega,

(4.23)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega,

where
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
V\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
with
0<V_{\min}\leq V(x)\leq V_{\max}>0
.
For a fixed function space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
, consider the empirical loss induced by the Physics Informed Neural Network:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big[|\Omega|\cdot\Big(\Delta u(X_{j})-V(X_{j})u(X_{j})+f(X_{j})\Big)^{2}\Big],

(4.24)

where
\{X_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
are datapoints uniformly sampled from the domain
\Omega
. Then the Physics Informed Neural Network estimator associated with function space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
is defined as the minimizer of
{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u)
over the function space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
:

\displaystyle\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}}=\min_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u)

Moreover, we assume that there exists some constant
C>0
such that all function
u
in the function space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
, the real solution
u^{\ast}
and
f,V
satisfy the following two conditions.

•

The gradients and function value are uniformly bounded

\displaystyle\max\Big\{

\displaystyle\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\|\Delta u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},

(4.25)

\displaystyle\|u^{\ast}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\|\Delta u^{\ast}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},V_{max},\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\Big\}\leq C.

•

All the functions in the function space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
satisfies the boundary condition

u=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega.

At the the same time, for any
\rho>0
, we assume the Rademacher complexity of a localized function space
{\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}(\Omega):=\Big\{h:=|\Omega|\cdot\left[(\Delta u-Vu+f)^{2}-(\Delta u^{\ast}-Vu^{\ast}+f)^{2}\right]\ \ \Big|\ \|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\leq\rho\Big\}
can be upper bounded by a sub-root function
\phi=\phi(\rho):[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
,
i.e.

\phi(4\rho)\leq 2\phi(\rho)\text{ and }R_{n}({\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}(\Omega))\leq\phi(\rho)\ (\forall\ \rho>0).

(4.26)

For all constant
t>0
. We denote
r^{*}
to be the solution of the fix point equation of local Rademacher complexity
r=\phi(r)
. There exists a constant
C_{p}
such that for probability
1-C_{p}\exp(-t)
, we have the following upper bound for the Physics Informed Neural Network Estimator

\|\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\inf_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)+\max\Big\{r^{*},\frac{t}{n}\Big\}.

Then we aim to calculate the final upper bound for DNN and truncated Fourier series based PINN estimator.

Theorem 18
.

(Informal Upper Bound of PINN with Deep Neural Network Estimator) With proper assumptions, consider the sparse Deep Neural Network function space
\Phi(L,W,S,B)
with parameters
L=O(1),\ W=O(n^{\frac{d}{d+2s-4}}),\ S=O(n^{\frac{d}{d+2s-4}}),\ B=O(1)
, then the Physics Informed estimator
\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}}^{\text{DNN}}=\min_{u\in\Phi(L,W,S,B)}{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{\text{PINN}}(u)
satisfies the following upper bound with high probability:

\displaystyle\|\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}}^{\text{DNN}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\lesssim n^{-\frac{2s-4}{d+2s-4}}\log n.

Proof.

On the one hand, by taking
s=2
and
p=2
in Theorem
8
proved above, we have that there exists some Deep Neural Network
u_{\text{DNN}}\in\Phi(L,W,S,B)
with
L=O(1),W=O(N),S=O(N),B=O(1)
, such that.

\displaystyle\|u_{\text{DNN}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq N^{-\frac{2s-4}{d}}\|u\|_{H^{s}(\Omega)}.

Applying strong convexity of the DRM objective function proved in Section
2.1
further implies:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}\lesssim\|u_{\text{DNN}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq N^{-\frac{2s-4}{d}}.

On the other hand, from lemma
13
proved above, we know that the function
\phi(\rho)
that upper bounds the local Rademacher complexity of the Deep Neural Networks
u_{\text{DNN}}
is dominated by the term
\sqrt{\frac{\rho 3^{L}S}{n}\log(W(B\vee 1)n)}
. By plugging in the magnitudes of
L,W,S,B
, we can determine the critical radius
\hat{r}
:

\sqrt{\frac{\rho 3^{L}S}{n}\log(W(B\vee 1)n)}\simeq\sqrt{\frac{\rho N}{n}(\log N+\log n)}\simeq\rho\Rightarrow\hat{r}\simeq\frac{N(\log N+\log n)}{n}.

Combining the two bounds above gives us:

\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}[\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{n}]\lesssim\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}+\hat{r}\lesssim N^{-\frac{2(s-2)}{d}}+\frac{N(\log N+\log n)}{n}.

By equating the two terms above, we can solve for the optimal
N
that yields the desired bound:

N^{-\frac{2(s-2)}{d}}\simeq\frac{N}{n}\Rightarrow N\simeq n^{\frac{d}{d+2s-4}}.

Plugging in the optimal
N
gives us the magnitudes of the four parameters
L=O(1),\ W=O(n^{\frac{d}{d+2s-4}}),\ S=O(n^{\frac{d}{d+2s-4}}),\ B=O(1)
, as well as the final rate:

\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}[\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{n}]\lesssim N^{-\frac{2(s-2)}{d}}+\frac{N\log N}{n}\lesssim n^{-\frac{2(s-2)}{d+2(s-2)}}\log n.

Theorem 19
.

(Informal Upper Bound of PINN with Truncated Fourier Series Estimator) With proper assumptions, consider the Physics Informed Neural Network objective with a plug-in Fourier Series estimator
\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}}^{\text{Fourier}}=\min_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\xi}(\Omega)}{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{\text{PINN}}(u)
with
\xi=\Theta(n^{\frac{1}{d+2s-4}})
, then with high probability we have

\displaystyle\|\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}}^{\text{Fourier}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\lesssim n^{-\frac{2s-4}{d+2s-4}}.

Proof.

On the one hand, from Lemma
9
and Lemma
10
proved above, we know that the function
\phi(\rho)
that upper bounds the local Rademacher complexity for Truncated Fourier Series is dominated by

\displaystyle R_{n}({\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega))

\displaystyle\lesssim R_{n}\left(\Big\{|\Delta u-\Delta u^{\ast}|:u\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega),|\Delta u-\Delta u^{\ast}|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\sqrt{\rho}\Big\}\right)

(4.27)

\displaystyle\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}|\Delta u(X_{i})-\Delta u^{\ast}(X_{i})|\ \Big|\|u-\Pi_{\xi}u^{\ast}\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\rho\Big]\right]

\displaystyle+{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}|\Delta\Pi_{>\xi}u^{\ast}(X_{i})|\Big]\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}|\Delta f(X_{i})|\ \Big|\|f\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\rho\Big]\right]

\displaystyle+{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\|\nabla\Pi_{\xi}u^{\ast}(X_{i})\|\Big]\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}|\Delta f(X_{i})|\ \Big|\|f\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\rho\Big]\right]

\displaystyle+{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\|\Delta\Pi_{\xi}u^{\ast}(X_{i})\|\Big]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d}{2}}+\sqrt{\frac{\|\Pi_{\xi}f\|_{H^{2}}^{2}}{n}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d}{2}}+\sqrt{\frac{\xi^{-2(s-1)}}{n}}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d}{2}}+\frac{1}{n}+\xi^{-2(s-2)}

Thus, the localization radius
\hat{r}
can be determined as follows:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d}{2}}+\frac{1}{n}+\xi^{-2(s-2)}\simeq\rho\Rightarrow\hat{r}\simeq\frac{\xi^{d}}{n}+\frac{1}{n}+\xi^{-2(s-2)},

On the other hand, by taking
\alpha=s
and
\beta=1
in Lemma
4
and applying strong convexity of the DRM objective function proved in Theorem
22
, we can upper bound the approximation error
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}
as below:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}\lesssim\xi^{-2(s-2)},

By equating the two terms above, we can solve for
\xi
that yields the desired bound:

\displaystyle\frac{\xi^{d}}{n}\simeq\xi^{-2(s-2)}\Rightarrow\xi\simeq n^{\frac{1}{d+2s-4}},

Plugging in the expression of
\xi
gives the final upper bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}[\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{n}]\lesssim\hat{r}+\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}\lesssim\frac{\xi^{d}}{n}+\xi^{-2(s-2)}\simeq n^{-\frac{2s-4}{d+2s-4}}.

Remark.

•

There is a common belief that Machine learning based PDE solvers can break the curse of dimensionality
[
15
,
24
,
40
]
. However we obtained an
n^{-\frac{2s-2}{2s-4+d}}
convergence rate which can become super slow in high dimension. Our analysis showed that it is essential to constrain the function space to break the curse of dimensionality.
[
47
]
considered the DRM in Barron spaces.
[
56
]
showed that functions in the Barron space enjoy a smoothness
s
at the same magnitude as
d
, which will also leads to convergence rate independent of the dimension using our upper bound. Neural network can also approximate mixed sparse grid spaces
[
52
,
70
]
, function on manifold
[
55
,
9
]
without curse of dimensionality. Combined with these approximation bounds, we can also achieve a bound that breaks the curse of dimensionality using Theorem
17
and
14
. In this paper, we aim to consider the statistical power of the loss function in common function spaces and leave the curse of dimensionality as a separate topic.

•

Our bound is faster than the concurrent bound
[
14
,
34
]
for we provided a fast rate
O(1/n)
by utilizing the strong convexity of the objective function and improves the convergence rate from
n^{-\frac{2s-2}{d+4s-4}}
to
n^{-\frac{2s-2}{d+2s-2}}
for Deep Ritz and from
n^{-\frac{2s-4}{d+4s-8}}
to
n^{-\frac{2s-4}{d+2s-4}}
for PINN. Comparing to the lower bound provided in Section
3
