, we show that our bound for PINN is near optimal and we’ll let our bound for DRM become near optimal in the next section.

•

For upper bound of DRM, due to a technical issue, we assume the observation we access is clean,
i.e

f_{i}=f(X_{i})
. We conjecture that add noising on observation will not effect the rate and leave this to future work.

5
Modified Deep Ritz Methods

Comparing the lower bound in Section
3
and the upper bound in Section
4
, we find out that the Physics Informed Neural Network achieved min-max optimality while the Deep Ritz Method does not. In this section, we proposed a modified version of deep Ritz which can be statistically optimal.

As discussed in Appendix
B
, the reason behind the suboptimality of DRM comes from the high complexity introduced via the uniform concentration bound of the gradient term in the variational form. At the same time, we further observed that the
\int\|\nabla u\|^{2}dx
does not require any query from the right hand side function
f
, which means that we can easily make another splitted sample to approximate the
\int\|\nabla u\|^{2}dx
term more precisely.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Big[|\Omega|\cdot\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla u(X_{j}^{\prime})\|^{2}\Big]+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big[|\Omega|\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{2}V(X_{j})|u(X_{j})|^{2}-f_{j}u(X_{j})\Big)\Big]

(5.1)

Once we sampled more data for approximating
\int|\nabla u|^{2}dx
, we can achieve an near optimal bound for the Truncated Fourier Estimator when
\frac{N}{n}\gtrsim n^{\frac{2}{d+2s-4}}
. The proof is based on a similar meta-theorem as following.

Theorem 20
(Meta-theorem for Upper Bounds of Modified Deep Ritz Method).

Let
u^{\ast}\in H^{s}(\Omega)
denote the true solution to the PDE model with Dirichlet boundary condition:

\displaystyle-\Delta u+Vu

\displaystyle=f\text{ on }\Omega,

(5.2)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega,

where
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
V\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
with
0<V_{\min}\leq V(x)\leq V_{\max}>0
.
For a fixed function space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
, consider the empirical loss induced by the Modified Deep Ritz Method
(N\geq n)
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Big[|\Omega|\cdot\frac{1}{2}|\nabla u(X_{i}^{\prime})|^{2}\Big]+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big[|\Omega|\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{2}V(X_{j})|u(X_{j})|^{2}-f(X_{j})u(X_{j})\Big)\Big],

(5.3)

where
\{X_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i=1}^{N}
and
\{X_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
are datapoints uniformly and independently sampled from the domain
\Omega
. Then the Modified Deep Ritz estimator associated with function space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
is defined as the minimizer of
{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(u)
over the function space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
:

\displaystyle\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}}=\min_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(u)

Moreover, we assume that there exists some constant
C>0
such that all function
u
in the function space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
, the real solution
u^{\ast}
and
f,V
satisfy the following two conditions.

•

The gradients and function value are uniformly bounded

\max\Big\{\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\|u^{\ast}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},V_{max},\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\Big\}\leq C.

(5.4)

•

All the functions in the function space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
satisfy the boundary condition

u=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega.

At the the same time, for any
\rho>0
, we assume the Rademacher complexity of two localized function spaces

{\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega):=\Big\{(h_{1},h_{2})\big|h1:=|\Omega|\cdot\left[\frac{1}{2}\Big(|\nabla u|^{2}-|\nabla u^{\ast}|^{2}\Big)\right],\ h2:=|\Omega|\cdot\left[\frac{1}{2}V(|u|^{2}-|u^{\ast}|^{2})-f(u-u^{\ast})\right],\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\leq\rho\Big\}

can be upper bounded by a sub-root function
\phi=\phi(\rho):[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
,
i.e.

\phi(4\rho)\leq 2\phi(\rho)\text{ and }R_{N,n}({\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega))\leq\phi(\rho)\ (\forall\ \rho>0),

(5.5)

where
R_{N,n}({\mathcal{S}}):=R_{N}(\{h_{1}|(h_{1},h_{2})\in{\mathcal{S}}\})+R_{n}(\{h_{2}|(h_{1},h_{2})\in{\mathcal{S}}\})
. For all constant
t>0
. We denote
r^{\ast}
to be the solution of the fix point equation of local Rademacher complexity
r=\phi(r)
.
There exist a constant
C_{p}
such that for probability
1-C_{p}\exp(-t)
, we have the following upper bound for the Modified Deep Ritz Estimator

\|\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim\inf_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)+\max\Big\{r^{*},\frac{t}{n}\Big\}.

Theorem 21
.

(Informal Upper Bound of DRM with Truncated Fourier Series Estimator)With proper assumptions, consider the Deep Ritz objective with a plug in Fourier Series estimator
\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}}^{\text{Fourier}}=\min_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\xi}(\Omega)}{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{\text{DRM}}(u)
with
\xi=\Theta(n^{\frac{1}{d+2s-4}})
and
\frac{N}{n}\gtrsim n^{\frac{2}{d+2s-4}}
, then we have

\displaystyle\|\hat{u}_{\text{DRM}}^{\text{Fourier}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim n^{-\frac{2s-2}{d+2s-4}}.

Proof.

On the one hand, from Lemma
9
and Lemma
10
proved above, we know that the function
\phi(\rho)
that upper bounds the local Rademacher complexity is dominated by the term
\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d}{2}}
for Truncated Fourier Series in
{\mathcal{F}}_{\xi}(\Omega)
. Following the same proof as shown for DRM upper bound, the localization radius
\hat{r}
can be determined as follows:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d-2}{2}}+\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{N}}\xi^{\frac{d}{2}}+\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{N}+\xi^{-2(s-1)}\simeq\rho.

For we have assumed
\frac{\xi^{d}}{N}<\frac{\xi^{d-2}}{n}
, the solution of the fixed point equation is
\hat{r}\simeq\frac{\xi^{d}}{n}
. On the other hand, by taking
\alpha=s
and
\beta=1
in Lemma
4
and applying strong convexity of the DRM objective function proved in Theorem
22
, we can upper bound the approximation error
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}
as below:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}\lesssim\xi^{-2(s-1)},

By equating the two terms above, we can solve for
\xi
that yields the desired bound:

\displaystyle\frac{\xi^{d-2}}{n}+\xi^{-2(s-1)}\simeq\xi^{-2(s-1)}\Rightarrow\xi\simeq n^{\frac{1}{d+2s-4}}+\xi^{-2(s-1)},

Plugging in the expression of
\xi
gives the final upper bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}[\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{n}]\lesssim\hat{r}+\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}\lesssim\frac{\xi^{d-2}}{n}+\xi^{-2(s-2)}\simeq n^{-\frac{2s-2}{d+2s-4}}.

Remark.

We still cannot achieve optimal rate for neural network even with modified DRM methods. The reason is because the number of neuron is not a good complexity measure for the gradient of the function thus the bound for
\psi(r)=R_{n}(\{\mathcal{I}(u)|\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\leq r\})
is not enough for achieving optimal convergence rate. However, following
[
63
,
70
,
33
,
9
,
18
]
using deep networks for estimating functions, we optimize the best neural network with constrained sparsity in our paper. Here we conjecture that there exists a computable complexity measure can makes DRM statistical optimal and leave finding the right complexity of the neural network’s gradient to be future work.

6
Experiments

In this section, we conduct several numerical experiments to verify our theory. We follow the neural network and hyper-parameter setting in
[
7
]
. Due to the page limit, we only put the experiments for Deep Ritz Methods here.

6.1
The Modified Deep Ritz Methods

In this section, we conduct experiments which substantiate our theoretical results for modified Deep Ritz methods. For simplicity, we take
V(x)=1
in our experiment. We conduct experiment in 2-dimension and select the solution of the PDE as
u^{\ast}=\sum_{z}\|z\|^{-s}\phi_{z}(x)\in H^{s}
. We show the log-log plot of the
H^{1}
loss against the number of sampled data for
s=4
in Figure
1
. We use an OLS estimator to fit the log-log plot and put the estimated slope and corresponding
\text{R}^{2}
score in Figure
1
. As our theory predicts, the modified Deep Ritz Method converges faster than the original one. All the derivation of the two estimators is listed in Appendix
B
.

Figure 1:
The Log-Log plot and estimated convergence slope for Modified DRM and DRM using Fourier basis, showing the median error over 5 replicates.

6.2
Dimension Dependent Scaling Law.

We conduct experiments to illustrate that the population loss of well-trained and well-tuned Deep Ritz method will scale with the
d
-dimensional training data number
N
as a power-law
\mathcal{L}\propto\frac{1}{N^{\alpha}}
. We also scan over a range of
d
and
\alpha
and verify an approximately
\alpha\propto\frac{1}{d}
scaling law as our theory suggests. We use the same test function in Section
6.1
as the solution of our PDE. For simplicity, we take
V(x)=1
in our experiment. We train the deep Ritz method on 20, 80, 320, 1280, 10240 sampled data points for 5,6,7,8,9,10 dimensional problems and we plot our results on the log-log scale. Results are shown in Figure
2
. We discover the
L\propto n^{\frac{1}{d+2}}
scaling law in practical situations.

Figure 2:
We verify the dimension dependent scaling law empirically. The multiplicative inverse of the scaling law coefficient is highly linear with the dimension
d
, showing the mean error over 2 replicates.

6.3
Adaptation To The Simpler Functions.

[
65
]
showed that the neural scaling law will adapt to the structure that the target function enjoys. This adaptivity enables the neural network to break the cure of the dimensionality for simple functions in high dimension.
[
72
,
8
]
also observed this theoretically. For solving PDEs, we also observed this adaptivity in practice. Here we tested the following two hypothesis

Figure 3:
Neural network have the ability to adapt to simple functions and achieves convergence without curse of dimensionality, showing the median error over 5 replicates.

•

Random Neural Network Teacher.
Following
[
65
]
, we also tested random neural network using He initialization
[
29
]
as the ground turth solution
u^{\ast}
.
[
13
]
showed that random deep neural networks are biased towards simple functions and in practice we observed a scaling law at the parametric rate. Specifically, we obtained a linear estimate with slope
\alpha=-0.50679429
and a
\text{R}^{2}
score
=0.96
in the log-log plot. See Figure
3
(a).

•

Simple Polynomials.
Neural network can approximate simple polynomials exponentially fast
[
76
]
. Thus, we select the ground truth solution to be the following simple polynomial in 10 dimensional spaces
u^{\ast}(x)=x_{1}x_{2}+\cdots+x_{9}x_{10}.
In this example, we obtained a linear estimate with slope
\alpha=-0.49755418
and a
\text{R}^{2}
score
=0.99
in the log-log plot. See Figure
3
(b).

7
Conclusion and Discussion

Conclusion

In this paper, We considered the statistical min-max optimality of solving a PDE from random samples. We improved the previous bounds
[
78
,
47
,
14
,
34
]
by providing the first fast rate generalization bound for learning PDE solutions via the strongly convex nature of the two objective functions. We achieved the optimal rate via the PINN and a modified Deep Ritz method. We verified our theory via numerical experiments and explored the dimension dependent scaling laws of Deep PDE solvers.

Discussion and Future Work

Here we discuss several drawbacks of our theory

•

We restricted our target function and estimators in
W^{1,\infty}
instead of
H^{1}
due to boundedness assumption made in the local Rademacher complexity arguments. However, typical functional used in physics is always unbounded, such as the Newtonian potential
\frac{1}{\|x-y\|^{d-2}}
, which limits the application of our theory.

•

This paper did not discuss any optimization aspect of the deep PDE solvers and always assumed achieves global optimum. However, it is important to investigate whether the optimization error
[
71
,
11
]
will finally dominate.

•

Instead of solving a single PDE, recent works
[
45
,
44
,
42
,
40
,
4
,
17
,
19
]
considered the so-called "operator learning", which aims to learn a family of PDE/inverse problems using a single network. It is interesting to investigate the generalization bound and neural scaling law there.

•

We find out that the sparsity of the neural network is not a good complexity measure of neural network’s gradient. We conjecture that there exists an oracle complexity measure, whose approximation and generalization bounds can lead Modified DRM to achieve the optimal convergence rate.

Appendix A
Proof of the Upper Bounds

A.1
Notations

In this section, we provide all the notations we need in the proof. Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be some open set. We denote
C(\Omega)
the space of continuous functions on
\Omega
and

C^{k}(\Omega)
the space of all functions that are
k
times continuously differentiable on
\Omega
(
\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
). For any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
(
\mathbb{N}_{0}:=\mathbb{Z}^{+}\cup\{0\}
is the set of all non-negative integers) and
1\leq p\leq\infty
, we define the Sobolev space
W^{n,p}(\Omega)
by

\displaystyle W^{n,p}(\Omega):=\{f\in L^{p}(\Omega):\ D^{\alpha}f\in L^{p}(\Omega),\ \forall\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\text{ with }|\alpha|\leq n\}.

In particular, when
p=2
, we define
H^{n}(\Omega):=W^{n,2}(\Omega)
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Moreover, for any
f\in W^{n,p}(\Omega)
with
1\leq p<\infty
, we define the Sobolev norm by:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{W^{n,p}(\Omega)}:=\Big(\sum_{0\leq|\alpha|\leq n}\|D^{\alpha}f\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

In particular, when
p=\infty
, we have:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{W^{n,\infty}(\Omega)}:=\max_{0\leq|\alpha|\leq n}\|D^{\alpha}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}.

Consider the Fourier expansion
f:=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}f_{z}\phi_{z}(x)
of the function
f\in W^{n,p}(\Omega)
. We can equivalently express the Sobolev norm as:

\|f\|_{W^{n,p}(\Omega)}=\Big(\sum_{z}\|z\|^{np}|f_{z}|^{p}\Big)^{1/p},

where
f_{z}=\int_{\Omega}f(x)\overline{\phi_{z}(x)}dx=\int_{\Omega}f(x)e^{-2\pi i\langle z,x\rangle}dx\ (x\in\Omega)
is the
z-
th Fourier coefficient of
f
.

Moreover, we use
W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
to denote the closure of
C_{c}^{1}(\Omega)
in
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
. In particular, when
p=2
, we define
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega):=W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega)
.

Furthermore, we use
\|\cdot\|
to present the vector 2 norm and, given a data sample
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\Omega
,
\|\cdot\|_{n,p}=\left({{\mathbb{E}}_{n}\cdot^{p}}\right)^{1/p}
denote the empirical
p
norm, where
{\mathbb{E}}_{n}:L^{2}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the corresponding empirical average operator defined as
{\mathbb{E}}_{n}f:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{i}),\ \forall\ f\in L^{2}(\Omega).
Given two quantities
X
and
Y
, we write
X\lesssim Y
when the inequality
X\leq CY
holds, where
C
is some constant. For two functions
f
and
g
mapping from
\mathbb{R}^{+}
to
\mathbb{R}
, we write
f=O(g)
when there exist two constants
C^{\prime}
and
x_{0}
independent of
f
and
g
, such that the inequality
f(x)\leq C^{\prime}g(x)
holds for any
x\geq x_{0}
. We use
X\simeq Y
to denote
X\lesssim Y
and
Y\lesssim X
.

A.2
Regularity Result For the PDE model.

Regularity Results of the DRM Objective Function

Theorem 22
.

We consider the static Schrödinger equation on the unit hypercube on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with the zero Direchlet boundary condition:

\displaystyle-\Delta u+Vu

\displaystyle=f\text{ on }\Omega,

(A.1)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega.

where
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
V\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
with
0<V_{\min}\leq V(x)\leq V_{\max}>0
. There exists a unique weak solution
u^{\ast}_{S}
to the equivalent variational problem
[
16
]
:

u^{\ast}_{S}=\argmin_{u\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)}{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{DRM}}_{S}(u):=\argmin_{u\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)}\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\Big[\|\nabla u\|^{2}+V|u|^{2}\Big]\ dx-\int_{\Omega}fudx\Big\}.

(A.2)

Then for any
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
, we have:

\frac{\min(1,V_{\min})}{2}\|u-u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\leq{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{DRM}}_{S}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{DRM}}_{S}(u^{\ast}_{S})\\
\leq\frac{\max(1,V_{\max})}{2}\|u-u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}_{H^{1}(\Omega)}.

(A.3)

Proof.

To show that
u^{\ast}_{S}
satisfies estimate
A.3
, we first claim that for any
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
,

{\mathcal{E}}_{S}^{\text{DRM}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}_{S}^{\text{DRM}}(u^{\ast}_{S})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u-\nabla u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}dx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}V(u^{\ast}_{S}-u)^{2}\ dx.

(A.4)

In fact, by plugging in the first equation of
A.1
, one has that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{S}^{\text{DRM}}(u^{\ast}_{S})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}dx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}V|u^{\ast}_{S}|^{2}dx-\int_{\Omega}fu_{S}^{\ast}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}dx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}V|u^{\ast}_{S}|^{2}dx+\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u^{\ast}_{S}-Vu^{\ast}_{S})u_{S}^{\ast}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}dx+\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u^{\ast}_{S})u_{S}^{\ast}dx-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}V|u^{\ast}_{S}|^{2}dx.

Furthermore, applying Green’s formula to the true solution
u^{\ast}_{S}
yields:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{S}^{\text{DRM}}(u^{\ast}_{S})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}dx+\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u^{\ast}_{S})u_{S}^{\ast}dx-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}V|u^{\ast}_{S}|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Omega}\frac{\partial u_{S}^{\ast}}{\partial n}u_{S}^{\ast}dx-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}dx-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}V|u^{\ast}_{S}|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}dx-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}V|u^{\ast}_{S}|^{2}dx,

where the last identity above follows from the second equality in
A.1
. Now for any
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
, applying Green’s formula to
u
and the true solution
u^{\ast}_{S}
implies:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{S}^{\text{DRM}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}_{S}^{\text{DRM}}(u^{\ast}_{S})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u\|^{2}dx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}V|u|^{2}dx-\int_{\Omega}fudx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}dx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}V|u^{\ast}_{S}|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u\|^{2}dx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}V|u|^{2}dx+\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u^{\ast}_{S}-Vu^{\ast}_{S})udx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}dx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}V|u^{\ast}_{S}|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u\|^{2}dx+\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u^{\ast}_{S})udx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}dx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}V\big(u^{\ast}_{S}-u\big)^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u\|^{2}dx+\int_{\partial\Omega}\frac{\partial u_{S}^{\ast}}{\partial n}udx-\int_{\Omega}\nabla u_{S}^{\ast}\cdot\nabla udx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}dx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}V\big(u^{\ast}_{S}-u\big)^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u-\nabla u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}dx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}V(u^{\ast}_{S}-u)^{2}\ dx,

where the last identity above again follows from the second equality in
A.1
. This completes our proof of identity
A.4
. Using the assumptions on the potential function
V
then implies:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{S}^{\text{DRM}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}_{S}^{\text{DRM}}(u^{\ast}_{S})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\max(1,V_{\max})}{2}\Big[\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u-\nabla u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}dx+\int_{\Omega}(u^{\ast}_{S}-u)^{2}\ dx\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{\max(1,V_{\max})}{2}\|u-u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}_{H^{1}(\Omega)},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{S}^{\text{DRM}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}_{S}^{\text{DRM}}(u^{\ast}_{S})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\max(1,V_{\min})}{2}\Big[\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u-\nabla u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}dx+\int_{\Omega}(u^{\ast}_{S}-u)^{2}\ dx\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{\max(1,V_{\min})}{2}\|u-u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}_{H^{1}(\Omega)}.

This completes our proof of
22
.

Regularity Results of the PINN Objective Function

Theorem 23
.

We consider the static Schrödinger equation on the unit hypercube on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with the Neumann boundary condition:

\displaystyle-\Delta u+Vu

\displaystyle=f\text{ on }\Omega,

(A.5)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega.

where
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
V\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
with
V-\frac{1}{2}\Delta V>C_{\min},0<C_{\min}<V(x)\leq V_{\max}
and
-\Delta V(x)\leq V_{\max}
. Then there exists a unique solution
u^{\ast}_{S}\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
to the following minimization problem
[
5
]
:

u^{\ast}_{S}=\argmin_{u\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)}{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{PINN}}_{S}(u):=\argmin_{u\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)}\Big\{\int_{\Omega}|\Delta u-Vu+f|^{2}dx\Big\}.

(A.6)

Then for any
u\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
, we have:

\min\{1,C_{\min}\}\|u-u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\Omega)}\leq{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{PINN}}_{S}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{PINN}}_{S}(u^{\ast}_{S})\\
\leq 2(1+V_{\max}+V_{\max}^{2})\|u-u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\Omega)}.

(A.7)

Proof.

For any
u\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
, we let
\tilde{u}=u-u^{\ast}
, then we have
\tilde{u}\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{PINN}}_{S}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{PINN}}_{S}(u^{\ast}_{S})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}|\Delta u-Vu-\Delta u^{\ast}+Vu^{\ast}|^{2}dx=\int_{\Omega}|\Delta\tilde{u}-V\tilde{u}|^{2}dx

(A.8)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}(\Delta\tilde{u})^{2}dx+\int_{\Omega}V^{2}\tilde{u}^{2}dx-2\int_{\Omega}V\tilde{u}\Delta\tilde{u}dx.

Using Green’s formula, we have:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}V\tilde{u}\Delta\tilde{u}dx+\int_{\Omega}\nabla(V\tilde{u})\cdot\nabla\tilde{u}dx=\int_{\partial\Omega}\frac{\partial\tilde{u}}{\partial n}V\tilde{u}ds=0,

where the last equality above follows from the fact that
\tilde{u}\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
. This further implies:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{PINN}}_{S}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{PINN}}_{S}(u^{\ast}_{S})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}(\Delta\tilde{u})^{2}dx+\int_{\Omega}V^{2}\tilde{u}^{2}dx+2\int_{\Omega}\nabla(V\tilde{u})\cdot\nabla\tilde{u}dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}(\Delta\tilde{u})^{2}dx+\int_{\Omega}V^{2}\tilde{u}^{2}dx+2\int_{\Omega}V\|\nabla\tilde{u}\|^{2}dx+2\int_{\Omega}\tilde{u}\nabla V\cdot\nabla\tilde{u}dx.

Using Green’s formula again, we have:

\displaystyle 2\int_{\Omega}\tilde{u}\nabla V\cdot\nabla\tilde{u}dx=\int_{\Omega}\nabla(u^{2})\cdot\nabla Vdx=\int_{\partial\Omega}\frac{\partial V}{\partial n}\tilde{u}^{2}ds-\int_{\Omega}\tilde{u}^{2}\Delta Vdx=-\int_{\Omega}\tilde{u}^{2}\Delta Vdx.

Then we can further deduce that:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{PINN}}_{S}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{PINN}}_{S}(u^{\ast}_{S})=\int_{\Omega}(\Delta\tilde{u})^{2}dx+\int_{\Omega}(V^{2}-\Delta V)\tilde{u}^{2}dx+2\int_{\Omega}V\|\nabla\tilde{u}\|^{2}dx.

For we have assumed
V\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
with
0<C_{\min}<V^{2}-\Delta V,0<C_{\min}<V(x)\leq V_{\max}
and
-\Delta V(x)\leq V_{\max}
, thus we have

\min\{1,C_{\min}\}\|u-u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\Omega)}\leq{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{PINN}}_{S}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{PINN}}_{S}(u^{\ast}_{S})\\
\leq 2(1+V_{\max}+V_{\max}^{2})\|u-u^{\ast}_{S}\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\Omega)}.

(A.9)

A.3
Auxiliary definitions and lemmata On Generalization Error

To bound the generalization error, we use the localized Rademacher complexity
[
3
]
. Recall that the Rademacher complexity of a function class
{\mathcal{G}}
is defined by

R_{n}({\mathcal{G}})={\mathbb{E}}_{Z}{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{g\in{\mathcal{G}}}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}g(Z_{j})\Big|\;\Big|\;Z_{1},\cdots,Z_{n}\Big],

where
Z_{i}
are i.i.d samples according to the data distributions and
\sigma_{j}
are i.i.d Rademacher random variables which take the value
1
with probability
\frac{1}{2}
and value
-1
with probability
\frac{1}{2}
.

The following important symmetrization lemma makes the connection between the uniform law of large numbers and the Rademacher complexity.

Lemma 24
(Symmetrization Lemma).

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a set of functions. Then

{\mathbb{E}}\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}u(X_{j})-{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim{\mathcal{P}}_{\Omega}}u(X)\Big|\leq 2R_{n}({\mathcal{F}}).

Lemma 25
(Ledoux-Talagrand contraction
[
41
, Theorem 4.12]
).

Assume that
\phi:{\mathbb{R}}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{R}}
is
L
-Lipschitz with
\phi(0)=0
. Let
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be independent
Rademacher random variables. Then for any
T\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})\in T}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\phi(t_{i})\Big]\leq 2L\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})\in T}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}t_{i}\Big].

Let
(E,\rho)
be a metric space with metric
\rho
. A
\delta
-cover
of a set
A\subset E
with respect to
\rho
is a collection of points
\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\}\subset A
such that for every
x\in A
, there exists
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
such that
\rho(x,x_{i})\leq\delta
. The
\delta
-covering number

{\mathcal{N}}(\delta,A,\rho)
is the cardinality of the smallest
\delta
-cover of the set
A
with respect to the metric
\rho
. Equivalently, the
\delta
-covering number

{\mathcal{N}}(\delta,A,\rho)
is the minimal number of balls
B_{\rho}(x,\delta)
of radius
\delta
needed to cover the set
A
.

Theorem 26
(Dudley’s Integral theorem).

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a function class such that
\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}}\|f\|_{n,2}\leq M
. Then the Rademacher complexity
R_{n}({\mathcal{F}})
satisfies that

R_{n}({\mathcal{F}})\leq\inf_{0\leq\delta\leq M}\Big\{4\delta+\frac{12}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\delta}^{M}\sqrt{\log{\mathcal{N}}({\varepsilon},{\mathcal{F}},\|\cdot\|_{n,2})}\,d{\varepsilon}\Big\}.

Lemma 27
(Talagrand Concentration Inequality).

Consider a function class
\mathcal{F}
defined on a probability measure
\mu
such that for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
, we have
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq\beta,\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[f]=0,\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[f^{2}]\leq\sigma^{2}
. Then for any
t>0
, we can have the following concentration results.

\mathbb{P}_{z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}\sim\mu}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(z_{i})\geq 2\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbb{E}_{z_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,z_{n}^{\prime}\sim\mu}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(z_{i}^{\prime})+\sqrt{\frac{2t\sigma^{2}}{n}}+\frac{2t\beta}{n}\right]\leq e^{-t}.

Lemma 28
(Peeling lemma
[
3
]
).

Consider some measurable function class
\mathcal{F}
. Assume that there exists a sub-root function
\phi(r)
satisfying

R_{n}(\{f\in\mathcal{F}\ |\ {\mathbb{E}}[f]\leq r\})\leq\phi(r)\ (\forall\ r>0).

(A.10)

Then we have

\mathbb{E}_{\sigma_{i},z_{n}}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f(z_{i})}{{\mathbb{E}}[f]+r}\right]\leq\frac{4\phi(r)}{r}.

Proof.

Denote
\mathcal{F}(r)=\{f\in\mathcal{F}\ |\ {\mathbb{E}}[f]\leq r\}
to be the localized set with radius
r
. Then for a fixed set of datapoints
\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and a fixed set of Rademacher random variables
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\sigma_{i},z_{n}}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f(z_{i})}{{\mathbb{E}}[f]+r}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\sigma_{i},z_{n}}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}(r)}\frac{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f(z_{i})}{r}\right]+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\sigma_{i},z_{n}}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}(r4^{j+1})\backslash\mathcal{F}(r4^{j})}\frac{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f(z_{i})}{r4^{j}+r}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{R_{n}(\mathcal{F}(r))}{r}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{R_{n}(\mathcal{F}(r4^{j+1}))}{r4^{j}+r}\leq\frac{\phi(r)}{r}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\phi(r4^{j+1})}{r4^{j}+r}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\phi(r)}{r}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^{j+1}\phi({r})}{r4^{j}+r}\leq\frac{4\phi(r)}{r}.

We also modify the peeling lemma above, as we aim to apply it to derive the upper bound for the Modified Deep Ritz Method (MDRM).

Lemma 29
(Peeling Lemma For MDRM).

Given some measurable function class
\mathcal{F}
and two continuous mappings
g,h:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we define a set
\mathcal{F}
of vector functions by:

\mathcal{F}:=\{(g\circ f,h\circ f)\ |\ f\in{\mathcal{F}}\}.

For any
r>0
, the localized set
\mathcal{F}_{r}
is defined by:

\mathcal{F}_{r}=\{(g_{f},h_{f})\in\mathcal{F}\ |\ {\mathbb{E}}_{x}[g_{f}(x)]+{\mathbb{E}}_{y}[h_{f}(y)]\leq r\}.

Moreover, the modified Rademacher Complexity of
\mathcal{F}_{r}
is defined by:

R_{n,m}(\mathcal{F}_{r}):=R_{n}\Big(\{g_{f}|(g_{f},h_{f})\in\mathcal{F}_{r}\}\Big)+R_{m}(\{h_{f}|(g_{f},h_{f})\in\mathcal{F}_{r}\}\Big).

Assume that there exists some function
\phi:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
and some
r^{\star}>0
, such that for any
r>r^{\star}
, we have:

\phi(4r)\leq 2\phi(r)\text{ and }R_{n,m}(\mathcal{F}_{r})\leq\phi(r).

Then for any
r>r^{\star}
, we have:

{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma,\tau}\Big[{\mathbb{E}}_{x,y}[\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}}\frac{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}g_{f}(x_{i})+\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tau_{j}h_{f}(y_{j})}{{\mathbb{E}}_{x}[g_{f}(x)]+{\mathbb{E}}_{y}[h_{f}(y)]+r}]\Big]\leq\frac{4\phi(r)}{r}.

Proof.

The proof is the same as the original peeling lemma, thus we omit the detailed proof here.

A.3.1
Local Rademacher Complexity of Truncated Fourier Basis

Definition 30
.

(Fourier Series)
Given a domain
\Omega\subseteq[0,1]^{d}
. For any
z\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
, we consider the corresponding Fourier basis function
\phi_{z}(x):=e^{2\pi i\langle z,x\rangle}\ (x\in\Omega)
. With respect to the Fourier basis, any function
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
can be decomposed as the following sum:

f(x):=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}f_{z}\phi_{z}(x).

(A.11)

where for any
z\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
, the Fourier coefficient
f_{z}=\int_{\Omega}f(x)\overline{\phi_{z}(x)}dx
.

Definition 31
.

(Truncated Fourier Series) For a fixed positive integer
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, we define the space
F_{\xi}(\Omega)
of truncated Fourier series as follows:

F_{\xi}(\Omega):=\Big\{f=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}f_{z}\phi_{z}\ \Big|\ f_{z}=0,\ \forall\ \|z\|_{\infty}>\xi\Big\}.

(A.12)

Equivalently, we can decompose any
f\in F_{\xi}(\Omega)
as
f:=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}f_{z}\phi_{z}
.

Lemma 32
.

(Local Rademacher Complexity of Localized Truncated Fourier Series)
For a fixed
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, we consider a localized class of functions
{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)=\Big\{f\in F_{\xi}(\Omega)\ \Big|\ \|f\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\rho\Big\}
, where
\rho>0
is fixed. Then we have the following upper bound on the local Rademacher complexity:

R_{n}({\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega))={\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f(X_{i})\ \Big|\ X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]\right]\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d-2}{2}}.

(A.13)

Proof.

Take an arbitrary function
f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)
. Let
f=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}f_{z}\phi_{z}
be the Fourier basis expansion of
f
.
\rho\geq\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}
implies constraint
\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}|f_{z}|^{2}\|z\|^{2}\lesssim\rho
on the Fourier coefficients
[
1
]
.

On the other hand, substituting the Fourier expansion into the average sum
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f(X_{i})
and using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality imply:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f(X_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}f_{z}\phi_{z}(X_{i})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f_{z}\phi_{z}(X_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\Big(\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}|f_{z}|^{2}\|z\|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\|z\|}\phi_{z}(X_{i})\Big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\rho}}{n}\Big(\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\|z\|}\phi_{z}(X_{i})\Big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

where we have used the constraint
\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}|f_{z}|^{2}\|z\|^{2}\lesssim\rho
in the last step above. Moreover, by taking expectation with respect to the i.i.d Rademacher random variables
\sigma_{i}\ (1\leq i\leq n)
and the uniformly sampled data points
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
on both sides and applying Jensen’s inequality, we can deduce that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X}\mathbb{E}_{\sigma}\Big[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f(X_{i})\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\rho}}{n}\mathbb{E}_{X,\sigma}\left[\Big(\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\|z\|}\phi_{z}(X_{i})\Big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{\rho}}{n}\left(\mathbb{E}_{X,\sigma}\Big[\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\|z\|}\phi_{z}(X_{i})\Big|^{2}\Big]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Using independence between the random variables
\sigma_{i}\ (1\leq i\leq n)
, we can further simplify the expectation inside the square root above as below:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X,\sigma}\Big[\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\|z\|}\phi_{z}(X_{i})\Big|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\mathbb{E}_{X,\sigma}\Big[\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\|z\|}\phi_{z}(X_{i})\Big|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{X,\sigma}\Big[\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{\|z\|^{2}}\Big|\phi_{z}(X_{i})\Big|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{|\Omega|}{\|z\|^{2}}\lesssim n\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\frac{1}{\|z\|^{2}}\lesssim n\frac{\xi^{d}}{\xi^{2}}=n\xi^{d-2}.

Combining the two bounds above yields the desired upper bound:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f(X_{i})\ \Big|\ X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]\right]\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\rho}}{n}\sqrt{n\xi^{d-2}}=\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d-2}{2}}.

Lemma 33
.

(Local Rademacher Complexity of Localized Truncated Fourier Series’ Gradient)
For a fixed
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, we consider a localized class of functions
{\mathcal{G}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)=\{\|\nabla f\|\ |\ f\in F_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)\}
, where
\rho>0
is fixed. Then for any sample
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\Omega
, we have the following upper bound on the local Rademacher complexity:

R_{n}({\mathcal{G}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega))={\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\|\nabla f(X_{i})\|\ \Big|\ X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]\right]\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d}{2}}.

(A.14)

Proof.

Take an arbitrary function
f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)
. Let
f=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}f_{z}\phi_{z}
be the Fourier basis expansion of
f
. Similarly, the norm restriction condition
\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\rho
can be reduced to the following condition about Fourier coefficients:

\displaystyle\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}|f_{z}|^{2}\|z\|^{2}\lesssim\rho.

Moreover, substituting the Fourier expansion into the average sum
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\|\nabla f(X_{i})\|
and using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality imply:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\|\nabla f(X_{i})\|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\|\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}f_{z}\nabla\phi_{z}(X_{i})\|\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\|f_{z}\nabla\phi_{z}(X_{i})\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\Big(\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}|f_{z}|^{2}\|z\|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\|z\|}\|\nabla\phi_{z}(X_{i})\|\Big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\rho}}{n}\Big(\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\|z\|}\|\nabla\phi_{z}(X_{i})\|\Big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

where we have used the constraint
\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}|f_{z}|^{2}\|z\|^{2}\lesssim\rho
in the last step above. Moreover, by taking expectation with respect to the i.i.d Rademacher random variables
\sigma_{i}\ (1\leq i\leq n)
and the uniformly sampled data points
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
on both sides and applying Jensen’s inequality, we can deduce that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X}\mathbb{E}_{\sigma}\Big[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\|\nabla f(X_{i})\|\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\rho}}{n}\mathbb{E}_{X,\sigma}\left[\Big(\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\|z\|}\|\nabla\phi_{z}(X_{i})\|\Big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{\rho}}{n}\left(\mathbb{E}_{X,\sigma}\Big[\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\|z\|}\|\nabla\phi_{z}(X_{i})\|\Big|^{2}\Big]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Using independence between the random variables
\sigma_{i}\ (1\leq i\leq n)
, we can further simplify the expectation inside the square root above as below:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X,\sigma}\Big[\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\|z\|}\|\nabla\phi_{z}(X_{i})\|\Big|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\mathbb{E}_{X,\sigma}\Big[\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\|z\|}\|\nabla\phi_{z}(X_{i})\|\Big|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{X,\sigma}\Big[\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{\|z\|^{2}}\|\nabla\phi_{z}(X_{i})\|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\Omega|\frac{4\pi^{2}\|z\|^{2}}{\|z\|^{2}}\lesssim n\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}1\lesssim n\xi^{d}.

Combining the two bounds above yields the desired upper bound:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\|\nabla f(X_{i})\|\ \Big|\ X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]\right]\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\rho}}{n}\sqrt{n\xi^{d}}=\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d}{2}}.

Lemma 34
.

(Local Rademacher Complexity of Localized Truncated Fourier Series’ Laplacian)
For a fixed
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, we consider a localized class of functions
{\bm{J}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega):=\Big\{f\in F_{\xi}(\Omega)\ \Big|\ \|f\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\rho\Big\}
, where
\rho>0
is fixed. Correspondingly, we define a localized class of Laplacians
{\bm{K}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega):=\{\Delta f\ |\ f\in J_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)\}
. Then for any sample
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\Omega
, we have the following upper bound on the local Rademacher complexity:

R_{n}({\bm{K}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega))={\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\Delta f(X_{i})\ \Big|\ X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]\right]\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d}{2}}.

(A.15)

Proof.

Take an arbitrary function
f\in{\bm{J}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)
. Let
f=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}f_{z}\phi_{z}
be the Fourier basis expansion of
f
. Similarly, the norm restriction condition
\|f\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\rho
can be reduced to the following condition about fourier coefficients:

\displaystyle\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}|f_{z}|^{2}\|z\|^{4}\lesssim\rho.

Moreover, substituting the Fourier expansion into the average sum
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\Delta f(X_{i})
and using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality imply:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\Delta f(X_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}f_{z}\Delta\phi_{z}(X_{i})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f_{z}\Delta\phi_{z}(X_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\Big(\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}|f_{z}|^{2}\|z\|^{4}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\|z\|^{2}}\Delta\phi_{z}(X_{i})\Big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\rho}}{n}\Big(\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\|z\|^{2}}\Delta\phi_{z}(X_{i})\Big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

where we have used the constraint
\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}|f_{z}|^{2}\|z\|^{4}\lesssim\rho
in the last step above. Moreover, by taking expectation with respect to the i.i.d Rademacher random variables
\sigma_{i}\ (1\leq i\leq n)
and the uniformly sampled data points
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
on both sides and applying Jensen’s inequality, we can deduce that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X}\mathbb{E}_{\sigma}\Big[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\Delta f(X_{i})\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\rho}}{n}\mathbb{E}_{X,\sigma}\left[\Big(\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\|z\|^{2}}\Delta\phi_{z}(X_{i})\Big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{\rho}}{n}\left(\mathbb{E}_{X,\sigma}\Big[\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\|z\|^{2}}\Delta\phi_{z}(X_{i})\Big|^{2}\Big]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Using independence between the random variables
\sigma_{i}\ (1\leq i\leq n)
, we can further simplify the expectation inside the square root above as below:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X,\sigma}\Big[\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\|z\|^{2}}\Delta\phi_{z}(X_{i})\Big|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\mathbb{E}_{X,\sigma}\Big[\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\|z\|^{2}}\Delta\phi_{z}(X_{i})\Big|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{X,\sigma}\Big[\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{\|z\|^{4}}|\Delta\phi_{z}(X_{i})|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\Omega|\frac{16\pi^{4}\|z\|^{4}}{\|z\|^{4}}\lesssim n\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\xi}1\lesssim n\xi^{d}.

Combining the two bounds above yields the desired upper bound:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho,\xi}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\Delta f(X_{i})\ \Big|\ X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]\right]\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\rho}}{n}\sqrt{n\xi^{d}}=\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n}}\xi^{\frac{d}{2}}.

A.3.2
Local Rademacher Complexity of the Deep Neural Network Model

In this section we aim to bound the local Rademacher Complexity of a Deep Neural Network. We first bound the covering number of the function space composed by the gradient of all possible neural networks and then apply a Duley Integral to achieve the final bound.

Definition 35
.

Let
\eta_{l}
denote the
l
-ReLU activiation function. Here we use
\eta_{3}:=\max\{0,x\}^{3}
[
15
]
as the activation function to ensure smoothness. We can define the space consisting of all neural network models with depth
L
, width
W
, sparsity constraint
S
and norm constraint
B
as follows:

\displaystyle\Phi(L,W,S,B):=\Big\{(\mathcal{W}^{(L)}\eta_{3}(\cdot)+b^{(L)})\cdots(\mathcal{W}^{(1)}x+b^{(1)})\ |\ \mathcal{W}^{(L)}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times W},b^{(L)}\in\mathbb{R},

(A.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{W\times d},b^{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{W},\mathcal{W}^{(l)}\in\mathbb{R}^{W\times W},b^{(l)}\in\mathbb{R}^{W}(1<l<L),

(A.17)

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{L}(\|\mathcal{W}^{(l)}\|_{0}+\|b^{(l)}\|_{0})\leq S,\max_{l}\|\mathcal{W}^{(l)}\|_{\infty,\infty}\vee\|b^{(l)}\|_{\infty}\leq B\Big\}.

(A.18)

where
\|\cdot\|_{0}
measures the number of nonzero entries in a matrix and
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\infty}
measures the maximum of the absolute values of the entries in a matrix.

For any
d\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, we refer to an arbitrary element in
\Phi(L,W,S,B)
as a ReLU3 Deep Neural Network. Then for any index
1\leq k\leq L
, we use
F_{k}
to denote the
k-
ReLU3 Deep Neural Network composed by the first
k
layers, i.e:

\displaystyle F_{k}(x):=(\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(\cdot)+b_{F}^{(k)})\cdots(\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(1)}x+b_{F}^{(1)}).

Also, we use
\Phi_{k}(L,W,S,B)
to denote the space consisting of all
F_{k}
. In particular, when
k=L
, we have:

\displaystyle F(x):=F_{L}(x)=(\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(L)}\eta_{3}(\cdot)+b_{F}^{(L)})\cdots(\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(1)}x+b_{F}^{(1)}),\text{ and }\Phi_{L}(L,W,S,B)=\Phi(L,W,S,B).

Furthermore, given that the domain
\Omega\subset[0,1]^{d}
is bounded, we have
\sup_{x\in\Omega}\|x\|_{\infty}=1
.

Lemma 36
.

(Upper bound on
\infty
-norm of functions in DNN space)

For any
1\leq k\leq L
, the following inequality holds:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\Omega,\ F_{k}\in\Phi_{k}(L,W,S,B)}\|F_{k}(x)\|_{\infty}\leq W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-1}{2}-k+1}.

Proof.

We use induction to prove this claim.

Base cases: When
k=1
, we have that for any
x\in\Omega
and any
F_{1}\in\Phi_{1}(L,W,S,B)
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\|F_{1}(x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(1)}x+b_{F}^{(1)}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(1)}\|_{\infty}\|x\|_{\infty}+\|b_{F}^{(1)}\|_{\infty}

(A.19)

\displaystyle\leq d\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(1)}\|_{\infty,\infty}+B\leq dB+B\leq 2(B\vee d)^{2}.

When
k=2
, we have that for any
x\in\Omega
and any
F_{2}\in\Phi_{2}(L,W,S,B)
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\|F_{2}(x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(2)}\eta_{3}(F_{1}(x))+b_{F}^{(2)}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(2)}\|_{\infty}\|\eta_{3}(F_{1}(x))\|_{\infty}+\|b_{F}^{(2)}\|_{\infty}\leq W\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(2)}\|_{\infty,\infty}\|F_{1}(x)\|_{\infty}^{3}+B.

By applying the bound proved in the case when
k=1
, we have:

\displaystyle\|F_{2}(x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq WB(dB+B)^{3}+B=WB^{4}(d+1)^{3}+B

\displaystyle=WB^{4}(d^{3}+3d^{2}+3d+1)+B\leq 8W(B\vee d)^{7}.

where the last inequality follows from the assumption that
W\geq 2
.

Inductive Step: Now we assume that the claim has been proved for
k-1
, where
3\leq k\leq L
. Similarly, for any
x\in\Omega
and any
F_{k}\in\Phi_{k}(L,W,S,B)
, we have:

\displaystyle\|F_{k}(x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(F_{k-1}(x))+b_{F}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}\|\eta_{3}(F_{k-1}(x))\|_{\infty}+\|b_{F}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq W\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}\|_{\infty,\infty}\|F_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}^{3}+B\leq WB\|F_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}^{3}+B.

Using inductive hypothesis, we can further deduce that:

\displaystyle\|F_{k}(x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq WB\times W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-3}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-3}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-3}{2}-3k+6}+B

\displaystyle\leq W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-3}{2}-3k+6}+B\vee d

\displaystyle\leq W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}[2^{\frac{3^{k}-3}{2}-3k+6}+1]

\displaystyle\leq W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-3}{2}-k+2}\ (k\geq 3)

\displaystyle=W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-1}{2}-k+1}.

Taking supremum with respect to
x\in\Omega
and
F_{k}\in\Phi_{k}(L,W,S,B)
on the LHS implies that the given upper bound also holds for
k
. By induction, the claim is proved.

We also need to show that the ReLU3 activation function is a Lipschitzness functions over a bounded domain.

Lemma 37
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, consider the
k-
ReLU activation function
\eta_{k}
defined on some bounded domain
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
(i.e,
\sup_{x\in\mathcal{D}}\|x\|_{\infty}\leq C
for some
C>0
). Then we have that for any
x,y\in\mathcal{D}
, the following inequalities hold:

\displaystyle\|\eta_{1}(x)-\eta_{1}(y)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|x-y\|_{\infty},

\displaystyle\|\eta_{2}(x)-\eta_{2}(y)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 2C\|x-y\|_{\infty},

\displaystyle\|\eta_{3}(x)-\eta_{3}(y)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 3C^{2}\|x-y\|_{\infty}.

Proof.

This is because
|\nabla\eta_{1}(x)|=|\max\{1,0\}|=1
,
|\nabla\eta_{2}(x)|=|2\max\{x,0\}|\leq 2C
and
|\nabla\eta_{3}(x)|=|3\max\{x,0\}^{2}|\leq 3C^{2}
.

Lemma 38
.

(Relation between the covering number of DNN space and parameter space)

For any
1\leq k\leq L
, suppose that a pair of different two networks
F_{k},G_{k}\in\Phi_{k}(L,W,S,B)
are given by:

\displaystyle F_{k}(x)

\displaystyle:=(\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(\cdot)+b_{F}^{(k)})\cdots(\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(1)}x+b_{F}^{(1)}),

\displaystyle G_{k}(x)

\displaystyle:=(\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(\cdot)+b_{G}^{(k)})\cdots(\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(1)}x+b_{G}^{(1)}).

Furthermore, assume that the
\|\ \|_{\infty}
norm of the distance between the parameter spaces of
F_{k}
and
G_{k}
is uniformly upper bounded by
\delta
, i.e

\|W_{F}^{(l)}-W_{G}^{(l)}\|_{\infty,\infty}\leq\delta,\ \|b_{F}^{(l)}-b_{G}^{(l)}\|_{\infty}\leq\delta,\ (\forall\ 1\leq l\leq k).

(A.20)

Then we have:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\Omega}\|F_{k}(x)-G_{k}(x)\|_{\infty}\leq\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-1}{2}-k+1}3^{k-1}.

(A.21)

Proof.

Let’s prove the claim by using induction on
k
.

Base Case: When
k=1
, we have that for any
x\in\Omega
and any
F_{1},G_{1}\in\Phi_{1}(L,W,S,B)
satisfying constraint
A.20
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\|F_{1}(x)-G_{1}(x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(1)}x+b_{F}^{(1)}-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(1)}x-b_{G}^{(1)}\|_{\infty}

(A.22)

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(1)}-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(1)}\|_{\infty}\|x\|_{\infty}+\|b_{F}^{(1)}-b_{G}^{(1)}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\delta d+\delta=\delta(d+1)\leq 2\delta(B\vee d)\leq 2\delta(B\vee d)^{2}.

When
k=2
, we have that for any
x\in\Omega
and any
F_{2},G_{2}\in\Phi_{2}(L,W,S,B)
satisfying constraint
A.20
, the following inequality holds:

\displaystyle\|F_{2}(x)-G_{2}(x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(2)}\eta_{3}(F_{1}(x))+b_{F}^{(2)}-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(2)}\eta_{3}(G_{1}(x))-b_{G}^{(2)}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(2)}\eta_{3}(F_{1}(x))-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(2)}\eta_{3}(G_{1}(x))\|_{\infty}+\|b_{F}^{(2)}-b_{G}^{(2)}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(2)}\eta_{3}(F_{1}(x))-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(2)}\eta_{3}(F_{1}(x))\|_{\infty}+\|\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(2)}\eta_{3}(F_{1}(x))-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(2)}\eta_{3}(G_{1}(x))\|_{\infty}+\delta.

By applying the upper bound proved in equation
A.19
, we can upper bound the first part
\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(2)}\eta_{3}(F_{1}(x))-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(2)}\eta_{3}(F_{1}(x))\|_{\infty}
by:

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(2)}\eta_{3}(F_{1}(x))-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(2)}\eta_{3}(F_{1}(x))\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(2)}-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(2)}\|_{\infty}\|\eta_{3}(F_{1}(x))\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq W\delta\|F_{1}(x)\|_{\infty}^{3}\leq\delta W[2(B\vee d)^{2}]^{3}.

By applying the Lipschitz condition proved in Lemma
37
and the bound proved in equation
A.22
, we can further upper bound the second part
\|\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(2)}\eta_{3}(F_{1}(x))-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(2)}\eta_{3}(G_{1}(x))\|_{\infty}
by:

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(2)}\eta_{3}(F_{1}(x))-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(2)}\eta_{3}(G_{1}(x))\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(2)}\|_{\infty}\|\eta_{3}(F_{1}(x))-\eta_{3}(G_{1}(x))\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq WB\times 3\sup_{F_{1}\in\Phi_{1}(L,W,S,B)}\|F_{1}(x)\|_{\infty}^{2}\times\|F_{1}(x)-G_{1}(x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq WB\times 3[2(B\vee d)^{2}]^{2}\times 2\delta(B\vee d)

\displaystyle\leq 24\delta W(B\vee d)^{6}.

Summing the two upper bounds above yields:

\displaystyle\|F_{2}(x)-G_{2}(x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 8\delta W(B\vee d)^{6}+24\delta W(B\vee d)^{6}+\delta\leq 24\delta W(B\vee d)^{7}.

where we again use the assumption
d\geq 2
in the last step.

Inductive Step: Now we assume that the claim has been proved for
k-1
, where
k\geq 3
. For any
x\in\Omega
and
F_{k}\in\Phi_{k}(L,W,S,B)
, we have that:

\displaystyle\|F_{k}(x)-G_{k}(x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(F_{k-1}(x))+b_{F}^{(k)}-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(G_{k-1}(x))-b_{G}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(F_{k-1}(x))-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(G_{k-1}(x))\|_{\infty}+\|b_{F}^{(k)}-b_{G}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(F_{k-1}(x))-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(G_{k-1}(x))\|_{\infty}+\delta.

Applying triangle inequality helps us upper bound the first term above as follows:

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(F_{k-1}(x))-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(G_{k-1}(x))\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(F_{k-1}(x))-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(F_{k-1}(x))\|_{\infty}+\|\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(F_{k-1}(x))-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(G_{k-1}(x))\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}\|\eta_{3}(F_{k-1}(x))\|_{\infty}+\|\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}\|\eta_{3}(F_{k-1}(x))-\eta_{3}(G_{k-1}(x))\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\delta W\|F_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}^{3}+BW\|\eta_{3}(F_{k-1}(x))-\eta_{3}(G_{k-1}(x))\|_{\infty}.

From Lemma
36
, we can upper bound the first term
\delta W\|F_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}^{3}
by:

\displaystyle\delta W\|F_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}^{3}\leq\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-3}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-3}{2}-3k+6}.

Moreover, applying Lemma
37
and the inductive hypothesis let us upper bound the second term
BW\|\eta_{3}(F_{k-1}(x))-\eta_{3}(G_{k-1}(x))\|_{\infty}
as follows:

\displaystyle BW\|\eta_{3}(F_{k-1}(x))-\eta_{3}(G_{k-1}(x))\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq BW\times 3\sup_{x\in\Omega,\ F_{k-1}\in\Phi_{k-1}(L,W,S,B)}\|F_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}^{2}\times\|F_{k-1}(x)-G_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 3BW\times W^{3^{k-2}-1}(B\vee d)^{5\times 3^{k-2}-1}2^{3^{k-1}-1-2k+4}\|F_{k-1}(x)-G_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 3BW\times W^{3^{k-2}-1}(B\vee d)^{5\times 3^{k-2}-1}2^{3^{k-1}-1-2k+4}\times\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-2}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}-k+2}3^{k-2}

\displaystyle\leq 3^{k-1}\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\times 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-1}{2}-3k+5}.

Combining the two upper bounds derived above yields:

\displaystyle\|F_{k}(x)-G_{k}(x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-3}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-3}{2}-3k+6}

\displaystyle+3^{k-1}\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\times 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-1}{2}-3k+5}+\delta

\displaystyle\leq\delta 3^{k-1}W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\times 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-1}{2}-k+1},

where the last inequality above follows from
k\geq 3
. Taking supremum with respect to
x\in\Omega
on the LHS implies the given upper bound also holds for
k
. By induction, the claim is proved.

Theorem 39
.

(Bounding the DNN space covering number)

Fix some sufficiently large
N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. Consider a Deep Neural Network space
\Phi(L,W,S,B)
with
L=O(1),W=O(N),S=O(N)
and
B=O(N)
. Then the
\log
value of the covering number of this DNN space with respect to the inf-norm
\|F(x)\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}|F(x)|
, which is denoted by
\mathcal{N}(\delta,\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
, can be upper bounded by:

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{N}(\delta,\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})=O\left(S\Big[\log(\delta^{-1})+3^{L}\log(WB)\Big]\right).

(A.23)

Proof.

We firstly fix a sparsity pattern (i.e, the locations of the non-zero entries are fixed). By picking
k=L
in Lemma
38
, we get the following upper bound on the covering number with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
:

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\delta}{3^{L-1}W^{\frac{3^{L-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\times 3^{L-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{L}-1}{2}-L+2}}\Big)^{-S}.

Furthermore, note that the number of feasible configurations is upper bounded by
{(W+1)^{L}\choose S}\leq(W+1)^{LS}
.
[
63
,
18
]
Plug in the previous inequality and yields:

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{N}(\delta,\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})

\displaystyle\leq\log\left[(W+1)^{LS}\Big(\frac{\delta}{3^{L-1}W^{\frac{3^{L-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\times 3^{L-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{L}-1}{2}-L+1}}\Big)^{-S}\right]

\displaystyle\leq S\log\Big[\delta^{-1}(W+1)^{L}3^{L-1}W^{\frac{3^{L-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\times 3^{L-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{L}-1}{2}-L+1}\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim S\Big[\log(\delta^{-1})+L\log(3W)+3^{L}\log(W(B\vee d))+3^{L}\log 2\Big].

Note that here the dimension
d
is some constant. Thus, by plugging in thee given magnitudes
L=O(1),W=O(N),S=O(N)
and
B=O(N)
, we can further deduce that:

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{N}(\delta,\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\lesssim S\Big[\log(\delta^{-1})+3^{L}\log(WB)\Big].

This finishes our proof.

Now let’s consider upper bounding the covering number of the
l_{2}
norm of the sparse Deep Neural Networks’ gradients. Note that for any
1\leq k\leq L-1
, any
k-
ReLU3 Deep Neural Network
F_{k}\in\Phi_{k}(L,W,S,B)
is a map from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}^{W}
. For any
1\leq l\leq W
, we use
F_{k,l}(x)
to denote the
l
-th component of the map
F_{k}
. This helps us write the map
F_{k}(x)
and its Jacobian matrix
J[F_{k}](x)
explicitly as:

\displaystyle F_{k}(x)

\displaystyle=[F_{k,1}(x),F_{k,2}(x),\cdots,F_{k,W}(x)]^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{W}.

\displaystyle J[F_{k}](x)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}F_{k,1}(x)&\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}F_{k,1}(x)&\cdots&\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{d}}F_{k,1}(x)\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}F_{k,2}(x)&\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}F_{k,2}(x)&\cdots&\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{d}}F_{k,2}(x)\\
\cdots&\cdots&\ddots\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}F_{k,W}(x)&\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}F_{k,W}(x)&\cdots&\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{d}}F_{k,W}(x)\\
\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{W\times d}.

In particular, when
k=L
, we have that any
F_{L}\in\Phi_{L}(L,W,S,B)=\Phi(L,W,S,B)
is a map from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}
. Thus, its Jacobian can be explicitly written as the following row vector:

\displaystyle J[F_{L}](x)=[\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}F_{L}(x),\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}F_{L}(x),\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{d}}F_{L}(x)]\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d}.

Lemma 40
.

(Upper bound on
\infty
-norm of Jacobian/Gradient of elements in the DNN space)

For any
1\leq k\leq L
, the following inequality holds:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\Omega,F_{k}\in\Phi_{k}(L,W,S,B)}\|J[F_{k}](x)\|_{\infty}\leq W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-1}{2}-k+1}3^{k-1}.

Proof.

We use induction on
k
to prove the claim.

Base case:
k=1
. By the definition of Jacobian matrix, we have that for any
x\in\Omega
and any
F_{1}\in\Phi_{1}(L,W,S,B)
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\|J[F_{1}](x)\|_{\infty}=\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(1)}\|_{\infty}\leq dB\leq 2(B\vee d)^{2}.

Inductive Step: Assume that the claim has been proved for
k-1
, where
2\leq k\leq L
. For any
x\in\Omega
and any
F_{k}\in\Phi_{k}(L,W,S,B)
, by applying the Chain Rule, we can write the Jacobian matrix
J[F_{k}](x)
as
J[F_{k}](x)=\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)
, where the ReLU3 activation function
\eta_{3}
is applied to each component
F_{k-1,l}\ (1\leq l\leq W)
of the map
F_{k}
. Then we have the following upper bound:

\displaystyle\|J[F_{k}](x)\|_{\infty}\leq\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}\|J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}\leq WB\|J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}.

(A.24)

Note that the composition
\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}
is a map from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}^{W}
. Hence, the Jacobian matrix
J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)
is of shape
\mathbb{R}^{W\times d}
. Applying the Chain Rule again implies:

\displaystyle\|J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}=\sup_{1\leq l\leq W}(\sum_{j=1}^{d}|3\eta_{2}(F_{k-1,l}(x))\frac{\partial F_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}|).

Furthermore, for any
1\leq l\leq W
, the summation on the RHS above can be upper bounded by:

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{d}|3\eta_{2}(F_{k-1,l}(x))\frac{\partial F_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}|

\displaystyle\leq 3\|F_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}^{2}(\sum_{j=1}^{d}|\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)|)\leq 3\|F_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}^{2}\|J[F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}.

Now let’s take supremum with respect to
l
and apply the inductive hypothesis and Lemma
36
. This yields:

\displaystyle\|J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 3W^{3^{k-2}-1}(B\vee d)^{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}2^{3^{k-1}-1-2k+4}\times W^{\frac{3^{k-2}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}-k+2}3^{k-2}

(A.25)

\displaystyle=W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}-1}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}-1}2^{\frac{3^{k}-1}{2}-3k+5}3^{k-1}.

By substituting equation
A.25
into equation
A.24
, we can derive the final bound:

\displaystyle\|J[F_{k}](x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq WB\|J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}\leq W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-1}{2}-3k+5}3^{k-1}

\displaystyle\leq W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-1}{2}-k+1}3^{k-1}.

where the last inequality above follows from
k\geq 2
. Taking supremum with respect to
x\in\Omega
and
F_{k}\in\Phi_{k}(L,W,S,B)
on the LHS implies that the given upper bound also holds for
k
. By induction, the claim is proved.

For the convenience of the following proof, we first prove this lemma for vector 2 norm and
\infty
norm.

Lemma 41
.

Given any two row vectors
\bm{u},\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d}
, we have:

\displaystyle\Big|\|\bm{u}\|-\|\bm{v}\|\Big|\leq\|\bm{u}-\bm{v}\|_{\infty}.

Proof.

Assume that the two vectors
\bm{u},\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be explicitly written as
\bm{u}=[u_{1},u_{2},\cdots,u_{d}]
and
v=[v_{1},v_{2},\cdots,v_{d}]
, respectively. By applying Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have:

\displaystyle\Big|\|\bm{u}\|-\|\bm{v}\|\Big|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left|\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}u_{i}^{2}}-\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}v_{i}^{2}}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}u_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}v_{i}^{2}-2\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}u_{i}^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}v_{i}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}u_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}v_{i}^{2}-2\sum_{i=1}^{d}u_{i}v_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}|u_{i}-v_{i}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{d}|u_{i}-v_{i}|\Big)^{2}=\|\bm{u}-\bm{v}\|_{\infty}^{2}.

Taking the square root on both sides yields the desired inequality.

Then we upper bound the Lipschitz constant of the gradient of the neural network. Given a DNN space
\Phi(L,W,S,B)
, we define a corresponding DNN Gradient space
\nabla\Phi(L,W,S,B)
as:

\nabla\Phi(L,W,S,B):=\{\|\nabla F\|\ |\ F\in\Phi(L,W,S,B)\}.

(A.26)

Lemma 42
.

(Relation between the covering number of the DNN Gradient space and parameter space)

For any
1\leq k\leq L
, suppose that a pair of different two networks
F_{k},G_{k}\in\Phi_{k}(L,W,S,B)
are given by:

\displaystyle F_{k}(x)

\displaystyle:=(\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(\cdot)+b_{F}^{(k)})\cdots(\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(1)}x+b_{F}^{(1)}),

\displaystyle G_{k}(x)

\displaystyle:=(\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(\cdot)+b_{G}^{(k)})\cdots(\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(1)}x+b_{G}^{(1)}).

Furthermore, assume that the
\|\ \|_{\infty}
norm of the distance between the parameter spaces is uniformly upper bounded by
\delta
, i.e

\|W_{F}^{(l)}-W_{G}^{(l)}\|_{\infty,\infty}\leq\delta,\ \|b_{F}^{(l)}-b_{G}^{(l)}\|_{\infty}\leq\delta,\ (\forall\ 1\leq l\leq k).

(A.27)

Then we have:

\sup_{x\in\Omega}\|J[F_{k}](x)-J[G_{k}](x)\|_{\infty}\leq\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-1}{2}-k+1}3^{2k-2}.

(A.28)

In particular, when
k=L
, we have:

\sup_{x\in\Omega}\Big|\|\nabla F_{L}(x)\|-\|\nabla G_{L}(x)\|\Big|\leq\delta W^{\frac{3^{L-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{L-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{L}-1}{2}-L+1}3^{2L-2}.

(A.29)

Proof.

We use induction on
k
to prove the claim.

Base case: When
k=1
, we have that for any
x\in\Omega
and any
F_{1},G_{1}\in\Phi_{1}(L,W,S,B)
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\|J[F_{1}](x)-J[G_{1}](x)\|_{\infty}=\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(1)}-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(1)}\|_{\infty}\leq\delta d\leq 2\delta(B\vee d)^{2}.

Inductive Step: assume that the claim has been proved for
k-1
, where
2\leq k\leq L
. Then for any
x\in\Omega
and
F_{k},G_{k}\in\Phi_{k}(L,W,S,B)
satisfying constraint
A.27
, applying the Chain Rule and triangle inequality help us upper bound the inf-norm
\|J[F_{k}](x)-J[G_{k}](x)\|_{\infty}
by:

\displaystyle\|J[F_{k}](x)-J[G_{k}](x)\|_{\infty}=\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}J[\eta_{3}\circ G_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}

(A.30)

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}+\|\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}J[\eta_{3}\circ G_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}\|J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}+\|\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}\|J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)-J[\eta_{3}\circ G_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\delta W\|J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}+BW\|J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)-J[\eta_{3}\circ G_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}.

Using equation
A.25
helps us upper bound the first term by:

\delta W\|J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}\leq\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}-1}2^{\frac{3^{k}-1}{2}-3k+5}3^{k-1}.

(A.31)

Note that the two compositions
\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}
and
\eta_{3}\circ G_{k-1}
both map from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}^{W}
. Hence, the two Jacobian matrices
J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)
and
J[\eta_{3}\circ G_{k-1}](x)
are of shape
\mathbb{R}^{W\times d}
. Applying the Chain Rule again implies:

\displaystyle\|J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)-J[\eta_{3}\circ G_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}=\sup_{1\leq l\leq W}(\sum_{j=1}^{d}|3\eta_{2}(F_{k-1,l}(x))\frac{\partial F_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}-3\eta_{2}(G_{k-1,l}(x))\frac{\partial G_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}|).

For any
1\leq l\leq W
, the summation on the RHS above can be upper bounded by:

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{d}|3\eta_{2}(F_{k-1,l}(x))\frac{\partial F_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}-3\eta_{2}(G_{k-1,l}(x))\frac{\partial G_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}|3\eta_{2}(F_{k-1,l}(x))\frac{\partial F_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}-3\eta_{2}(G_{k-1,l}(x))\frac{\partial F_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}|

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{d}|3\eta_{2}(G_{k-1,l}(x))\frac{\partial F_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}-3\eta_{2}(G_{k-1,l}(x))\frac{\partial G_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}|3\eta_{2}(F_{k-1,l}(x))-3\eta_{2}(G_{k-1,l}(x))\|\frac{\partial F_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}|+\sum_{j=1}^{d}|3\eta_{2}(G_{k-1,l}(x))\|\frac{\partial F_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}-\frac{\partial G_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}|.

We denote the two summations above by
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, respectively:

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{j=1}^{d}|3\eta_{2}(F_{k-1,l}(x))-3\eta_{2}(G_{k-1,l}(x))\|\frac{\partial F_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}|,

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{j=1}^{d}|3\eta_{2}(G_{k-1,l}(x))\|\frac{\partial F_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}-\frac{\partial G_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}|.

For the first sum
T_{1}
, applying Lemma
36
, Lemma
37
, Lemma
38
and Lemma
40
yields the following upper bound:

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 6\Big(\sup_{x\in\Omega,\ F_{k-1}\in\Phi_{k-1}(L,W,S,B)}\|F_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}\Big)\|F_{k-1}(x)-G_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{d}|\frac{\partial F_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}|

\displaystyle\leq 6\Big(\sup_{x\in\Omega,\ F_{k-1}\in\Phi_{k-1}(L,W,S,B)}\|F_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}\Big)\|F_{k-1}(x)-G_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}\|J[F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 3\times W^{\frac{3^{k-2}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}-k+2}\times\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-2}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}-k+2}3^{k-2}

\displaystyle\times W^{\frac{3^{k-2}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}-k+2}3^{k-2}=\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-3}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-3}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-3}{2}-3k+6}3^{2k-3}.

For the second sum
T_{2}
, applying Lemma
36
and inductive hypothesis yields:

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 3\Big(\sup_{x\in\Omega,\ G_{k-1}\in\Phi_{k-1}(L,W,S,B)}\|G_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}\Big)^{2}\|J[F_{k-1}](x)-J[G_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 3\times W^{3^{k-2}-1}(B\vee d)^{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}2^{3^{k-1}-1-2k+4}\times\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-2}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}-k+2}3^{2k-4}

\displaystyle=\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-3}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-3}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-3}{2}-3k+6}3^{2k-3}.

Combining the two upper bounds on
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
yields:

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{d}|3\eta_{2}(F_{k-1,l}(x))\frac{\partial F_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}-3\eta_{2}(G_{k-1,l}(x))\frac{\partial G_{k-1,l}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}|

\displaystyle\leq T_{1}+T_{2}\leq 2\times\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-3}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-3}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-3}{2}-3k+6}3^{2k-3}.

By taking supremum with respect to
1\leq l\leq W
on the LHS yields:

\displaystyle BW\|J[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)-J[\eta_{3}\circ G_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-1}{2}-3k+6}3^{2k-3}.

(A.32)

By adding the two upper bounds in
A.31
and
A.32
, we can deduce that:

\displaystyle\|J[F_{k}](x)-J[G_{k}](x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}-1}2^{\frac{3^{k}-1}{2}-3k+5}3^{k-1}

\displaystyle+\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-1}{2}-3k+6}3^{2k-3}

\displaystyle\leq\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k}-1}{2}-k+1}3^{2k-2}.

where the last inequality above follows from
k\geq 2
. Taking supremum with respect to
x\in\Omega
on the LHS implies the given upper bound also holds for
k
. By induction, the claim is proved.

In particular, when
k=L
, we have
\nabla F_{L}(x)=J[F_{L}](x)^{T}
for any
x\in\Omega
. Applying Lemma
41
then yields:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\Omega}\Big|\|\nabla F_{L}(x)\|-\|\nabla G_{L}(x)\|\Big|

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in\Omega}\Big|\|\nabla J[F_{L}](x)^{T}\|-\|\nabla J[G_{L}](x)^{T}\|\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in\Omega}\|J[F_{L}](x)-J[G_{L}](x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\delta W^{\frac{3^{L-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{L-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{L}-1}{2}-k+1}3^{2L-2}.

This finishes our proof of the Lemma.

Theorem 43
.

(Bounding the DNN Gradient space covering number)

Fix some sufficiently large
N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. Consider a Deep Neural Network space
\Phi(L,W,S,B)
with
L=O(1),W=O(N),S=O(N)
and
B=O(N)
. Then the
\log
value of the covering number of the DNN Gradient space with respect to the
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
norm
\|F(x)\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}|F(x)|
, which is denoted by
\mathcal{N}(\delta,\nabla\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
, can be upper bounded by:

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{N}(\delta,\nabla\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})=O\left(S\Big[\log(\delta^{-1})+3^{L}\log(WB)\Big]\right).

(A.33)

Proof.

We firstly fix a sparsity pattern (i.e, the locations of the non-zero entries are fixed). Using equation
A.29
in Lemma
42
, yields the following upper bound on the covering number with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
:

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\delta}{W^{\frac{3^{L-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{L-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{L}-1}{2}-L+1}3^{2L-2}}\Big)^{-S}.

Furthermore, note that the number of feasible configurations is upper bounded by:

{(W+1)^{L}\choose S}\leq(W+1)^{LS}.
[
63
,
18
]
. Plug this inequality into the previous estimation then yields:

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{N}(\delta,\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})

\displaystyle\leq\log\left[(W+1)^{LS}\Big(\frac{\delta}{W^{\frac{3^{L-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{L-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{L}-1}{2}-L+1}3^{2L-2}}\Big)^{-S}\right]

\displaystyle\leq S\log\Big[\delta^{-1}(W+1)^{L}3^{2L-2}W^{\frac{3^{L-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{L-1}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{L}-1}{2}-L+1}\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim S\Big[\log(\delta^{-1})+2L\log(3W)+3^{L}\log(W(B\vee d))+3^{L}\log 2\Big].

Note that here the dimension
d
is some constant. Thus, by plugging in thee given magnitudes
L=O(1),W=O(N),S=O(N)
and
B=O(N)
, we can further deduce that:

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{N}(\delta,\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\lesssim S\Big[\log(\delta^{-1})+3^{L}\log(WB)\Big].

This finishes our proof.

Now let’s consider upper bounding the covering number of the Laplacian of the sparse Deep Neural Networks. Note that for any
1\leq k\leq L-1
, any
k-
ReLU3 Deep Neural Network
F_{k}\in\Phi_{k}(L,W,S,B)
is a vector-valued function mapping from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}^{W}
. Moreover, we define the Laplacian of
F_{k}(x)
, which is denoted by
\Delta[F_{k}](x)
, as follows:

\displaystyle\Delta[F_{k}](x)

\displaystyle=[\Delta F_{k,1}(x),\Delta F_{k,2}(x),\cdots,\Delta F_{k,W}(x)]^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{W},

where for any
1\leq l\leq W
, we have:

\displaystyle\Delta F_{k,l}(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}F_{k,l}(x).

In particular, when
k=L
, we have that any
F_{L}\in\Phi_{L}(L,W,S,B)=\Phi(L,W,S,B)
is a scalar-valued function mapping from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}
. Thus, its Laplacian can be explicitly written as:

\displaystyle\Delta[F_{L}](x)=\Delta F_{L}(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}F_{L}(x).

For both Lemma
44
and Lemma
45
below, we consider a fixed Deep Neural Network space
\Phi(L,W,S,B)
with
L=O(1),W=O(N),S=O(N)
and
B=O(N)
, where
N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
is fixed and sufficiently large.

Lemma 44
.

(Upper bound on
\infty
-norm of Laplacian of elements in the DNN space)

For any
1\leq k\leq L
, we have the following upper bound:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\Omega,F_{k}\in\Phi_{k}(L,W,S,B)}\|\Delta[F_{k}](x)\|_{\infty}=O\Big(W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}\Big).

Proof.

We use induction on
k
to prove the claim.

Base case:
k=1
. Note that any
F_{1}\in\Phi_{1}(L,W,S,B)
is a linear transform, so the Laplacian
\Delta[F_{1}](x)
must be the zero vector for any
x\in\Omega
. This implies:

\displaystyle\|\Delta[F_{1}](x)\|_{\infty}=0\lesssim(B\vee d)^{2}.

Inductive Step: Assume that the claim has been proved for
k-1
, where
2\leq k\leq L
. For any
x\in\Omega
and any
F_{k}\in\Phi_{k}(L,W,S,B)
, using linearity of the Laplacian operator implies:

\displaystyle\Delta[F_{k}](x)=\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x).

Taking the inf-norm on both sides of the identity above implies:

\displaystyle\|\Delta[F_{k}](x)\|_{\infty}\leq\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}\|\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}\leq WB\|\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}.

It now remains to upper bound the term
\|\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}
. For any
1\leq l\leq W
, we will use the Chain Rule to write the
l
-th component
\Big(\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\Big)_{l}
in an explicit form. For any
1\leq j\leq d
, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\eta_{3}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]=3\eta_{2}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x).

Differentiating with respect to
x_{j}
on both sides above yields:

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}\eta_{3}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]=6\eta_{1}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}+3\eta_{2}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}F_{k-1,l}(x).

(A.34)

Summing the expression above from
j=1
to
j=d
implies:

\displaystyle\left|\Big(\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\Big)_{l}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}\eta_{3}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]\right|

\displaystyle=\left|6\eta_{1}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}+3\eta_{2}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 6\Big|\eta_{1}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]\Big|\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big|\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big|\right)^{2}+3\Big|\eta_{2}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]\Big|\left|\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\right|.

We denote the two summations above by
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
, respectively:

\displaystyle U_{1}

\displaystyle:=6\Big|\eta_{1}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]\Big|\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big|\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big|\right)^{2}.

\displaystyle U_{2}

\displaystyle:=3\Big|\eta_{2}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]\Big|\left|\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\right|.

On the one hand, by applying Lemma
36
and Lemma
40
, we can upper bound
U_{1}
by:

\displaystyle U_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 6\Big(\sup_{x\in\Omega,\ F_{k-1}\in\Phi_{k-1}(L,W,S,B)}\|F_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}\Big)\Big(\sup_{x\in\Omega,F_{k-1}\in\Phi_{k-1}(L,W,S,B)}\|J[F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 6\times W^{\frac{3^{k-2}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}-k+2}\times W^{3^{k-2}-1}(B\vee d)^{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}2^{3^{k-1}-1-2k+4}3^{2k-4}

\displaystyle\lesssim W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-3}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-3}{2}},

where the last step above follows from
k\leq L
and
L=O(1)
.

On the other hand, by applying Lemma
36
and the inductive hypothesis, we have:

\displaystyle U_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 3\Big(\sup_{x\in\Omega,\ F_{k-1}\in\Phi_{k-1}(L,W,S,B)}\|F_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}\Big)^{2}\|\Delta[F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim 3\times W^{3^{k-2}-1}(B\vee d)^{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}2^{3^{k-1}-1-2k+4}\times W^{\frac{3^{k-2}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-3}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-3}{2}},

where the last step above follows from
k\leq L
and
L=O(1)
.

Summing the two bounds on
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
implies that for any
1\leq l\leq W
, we have:

\left|\Big(\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\Big)_{l}\right|\leq U_{1}+U_{2}\lesssim W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-3}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-3}{2}}.

(A.35)

Taking supremum with respect to
1\leq l\leq W
then yields:

\displaystyle\|\Delta[F_{k}](x)\|_{\infty}\leq WB\|\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}\lesssim W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}.

Taking supremum with respect to
x\in\Omega
and
F_{k}\in\Phi_{k}(L,W,S,B)
on the LHS implies that the given upper bound also holds for
k
. By induction, the claim is proved.

Lemma 45
.

(Relation between the covering number of the DNN Laplacian space and parameter space)

For any
1\leq k\leq L
, suppose that a pair of different two networks
F_{k},G_{k}\in\Phi_{k}(L,W,S,B)
are given by:

\displaystyle F_{k}(x)

\displaystyle:=(\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(\cdot)+b_{F}^{(k)})\cdots(\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(1)}x+b_{F}^{(1)}),

\displaystyle G_{k}(x)

\displaystyle:=(\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\eta_{3}(\cdot)+b_{G}^{(k)})\cdots(\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(1)}x+b_{G}^{(1)}).

Furthermore, assume that the
\|\ \|_{\infty}
norm of the distance between the parameter spaces is uniformly upper bounded by
\delta
, i.e

\|W_{F}^{(l)}-W_{G}^{(l)}\|_{\infty,\infty}\leq\delta,\ \|b_{F}^{(l)}-b_{G}^{(l)}\|_{\infty}\leq\delta,\ (\forall\ 1\leq l\leq k).

(A.36)

Then we have:

\sup_{x\in\Omega}\|\Delta[F_{k}](x)-\Delta[G_{k}](x)\|_{\infty}=O\Big(\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}\Big).

(A.37)

Proof.

We use induction on
k
to prove the claim.

Base case:
k=1
. Note that any
F_{1}\in\Phi_{1}(L,W,S,B)
is a linear transform, so the Laplacian
\Delta[F_{1}](x)
must be the zero vector for any
x\in\Omega
. Hence, for any
x\in\Omega
and any
F_{1},G_{1}\in\Phi_{1}(L,W,S,B)
, we have:

\displaystyle\|\Delta[F_{1}](x)-\Delta[G_{1}](x)\|_{\infty}=0\lesssim\delta(B\vee d)^{2}.

Inductive Step: assume that the claim has been proved for
k-1
, where
2\leq k\leq L
. Then for any
x\in\Omega
and
F_{k},G_{k}\in\Phi_{k}(L,W,S,B)
satisfying constraint
A.36
, applying linearity of the Laplacian operator indicates:

\displaystyle\|\Delta[F_{k}](x)-\Delta[G_{k}](x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ G_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\|\Big(\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\Big)\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle+\left\|\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\Big(\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)-\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ G_{k-1}](x)\Big)\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}\|\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle+\|\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}\|\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)-\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ G_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}.

For the first term
\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}\|\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}
, applying the bound in equation
A.35
and equation
A.36
yields:
