\displaystyle\|\mathcal{W}_{F}^{(k)}-\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}\|\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta W\times W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-3}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-3}{2}}

(A.38)

\displaystyle=\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-3}{2}}.

For the second term
\|\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}\|\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)-\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ G_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}
, we need to upper bound the norm
\|\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)-\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ G_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}
at first. Note that for any
1\leq l\leq W
, we can use equation
A.34
to write the
l
-th component of
\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)-\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ G_{k-1}](x)
as:

\displaystyle\Big(\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)

\displaystyle-\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ G_{k-1}](x)\Big)_{l}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}\eta_{3}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]-\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}\eta_{3}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]

\displaystyle=6\eta_{1}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}-6\eta_{1}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}G_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle+3\eta_{2}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}F_{k-1,l}(x)-3\eta_{2}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}G_{k-1,l}(x)

\displaystyle=6\eta_{1}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}-6\eta_{1}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle+6\eta_{1}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}-6\eta_{1}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}G_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle+3\eta_{2}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}F_{k-1,l}(x)-3\eta_{2}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}F_{k-1,l}(x)

\displaystyle+3\eta_{2}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}F_{k-1,l}(x)-3\eta_{2}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}G_{k-1,l}(x).

We denote the four summations above by
V_{1},V_{2},V_{3}
and
V_{4}
, respectively:

\displaystyle V_{1}

\displaystyle:=6\eta_{1}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}-6\eta_{1}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2},

\displaystyle V_{2}

\displaystyle:=6\eta_{1}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}-6\eta_{1}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}G_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2},

\displaystyle V_{3}

\displaystyle:=3\eta_{2}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}F_{k-1,l}(x)-3\eta_{2}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}F_{k-1,l}(x),

\displaystyle V_{4}

\displaystyle:=3\eta_{2}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}F_{k-1,l}(x)-3\eta_{2}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}G_{k-1,l}(x).

By applying Lemma
36
, Lemma
37
, Lemma
38
and Lemma
40
, we can upper bound
V_{1}
by:

\displaystyle V_{1}

\displaystyle=6\Big(\eta_{1}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]-\eta_{1}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\Big)\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 6|F_{k-1,l}(x)-G_{k-1,l}(x)|\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big|\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big|\right)^{2}\leq 6\|F_{k-1}(x)-G_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}\|J[F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-2}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}-k+2}3^{k-2}\times W^{3^{k-2}-1}(B\vee d)^{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}2^{3^{k-1}-1-2k+4}3^{2k-4}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-3}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-3}{2}}.

where the last step above follows from
k\leq L
and
L=O(1)
.

Furthermore, note that for any
1\leq j\leq d
, we can upper bound the difference
\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}-\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}G_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}
as follows:

\displaystyle\Big(

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}-\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}G_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}\leq\left|\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}-\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}G_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}\right|

(A.39)

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)+\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}G_{k-1,l}(x)\right|\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}G_{k-1,l}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\Big|\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big|+\Big|\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}G_{k-1,l}(x)\Big|\right)\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}G_{k-1,l}(x)\right|.

Note that
\eta_{1}(G_{k-1,l}(x))\geq 0
. Combining the non-negativity with equation
A.39
, Lemma
36
, Lemma
40
and Lemma
42
helps us upper bound
V_{2}
by:

\displaystyle V_{2}

\displaystyle=6\eta_{1}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left[\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}-\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}G_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 6\|G_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(\Big|\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big|+\Big|\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}G_{k-1,l}(x)\Big|\right)\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}G_{k-1,l}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 6\|G_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big|\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big|+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big|\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}G_{k-1,l}(x)\Big|\right)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big|\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}F_{k-1,l}(x)-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}G_{k-1,l}(x)\Big|\right)

\displaystyle\leq 6\|G_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}\Big(\|J[F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}+\|J[G_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}\Big)\Big\|J[F_{k-1}](x)-J[G_{k-1}](x)\Big\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 6W^{\frac{3^{k-2}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}-k+2}\times 2W^{\frac{3^{k-2}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}-k+2}3^{k-2}

\displaystyle\times\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-2}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}-k+1}3^{2k-4}\lesssim\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-3}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-3}{2}}.

where the last step above follows from
k\leq L
and
L=O(1)
.

Moreover, using Lemma
36
, Lemma
37
and Lemma
44
helps us upper bound
V_{3}
by:

\displaystyle V_{3}

\displaystyle=\Big(3\eta_{2}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]-3\eta_{2}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\Big)\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}F_{k-1,l}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\Big|3\eta_{2}[F_{k-1,l}(x)]-3\eta_{2}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\Big|\Big|\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}F_{k-1,l}(x)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq 6\Big(\sup_{x\in\Omega,\ F_{k-1}\in\Phi_{k-1}(L,W,S,B)}\|F_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}\Big)\|F_{k-1}(x)-G_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}\|\Delta[F_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim 6W^{\frac{3^{k-2}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}-k+2}\times\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-2}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}{2}}2^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}-k+2}3^{k-2}

\displaystyle\times W^{\frac{3^{k-2}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}{2}}\lesssim\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-3}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-3}{2}}

where the last step above follows from
k\leq L
and
L=O(1)
.

Finally, applying Lemma
36
and inductive hypothesis helps us upper bound
V_{4}
by:

\displaystyle V_{4}

\displaystyle=3\eta_{2}[G_{k-1,l}(x)]\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}F_{k-1,l}(x)-\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}G_{k-1,l}(x)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 3\|G_{k-1}(x)\|_{\infty}^{2}\|\Delta[F_{k-1}](x)-\Delta[G_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim 3W^{3^{k-2}-1}(B\vee d)^{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}2^{3^{k-1}-1-2k+4}\times\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-2}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-2}-1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-3}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-3}{2}}

where the last step above follows from
k\leq L
and
L=O(1)
.

Combining the four bounds on
V_{1},V_{2},V_{3}
and
V_{4}
implies:

\displaystyle\Big(\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)

\displaystyle-\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ G_{k-1}](x)\Big)_{l}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}V_{i}\lesssim\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-3}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-3}{2}}

Taking supremum with respect to
1\leq l\leq W
gives us an upper bound on the second term
\|\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}\|\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)-\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ G_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}
:

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{W}_{G}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}\|\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ F_{k-1}](x)-\Delta[\eta_{3}\circ G_{k-1}](x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim WB\times\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-3}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-3}{2}}

(A.40)

\displaystyle=\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}

Combining the two bounds derived in equation
A.38
and equation
A.40
then implies:

\displaystyle\|\Delta[F_{k}](x)-\Delta[G_{k}](x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-3}{2}}+\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta W^{\frac{3^{k-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{k-1}-1}{2}}

Taking supremum with respect to
x\in\Omega
on the LHS implies that the given upper bound also holds for
k
. By induction, the claim is proved.

Given a Neural Network function space
\Phi(L,W,S,B)
, we define a corresponding Neural Network Laplacian space
\Delta\Phi(L,W,S,B)
as:

\Delta\Phi(L,W,S,B):=\{\Delta F\ |\ F\in\Phi(L,W,S,B)\}.

(A.41)

Theorem 46
.

(Bounding the Neural Network Laplacian space covering number)

Fix some sufficiently large
N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. Consider a Deep Neural Network space
\Phi(L,W,S,B)
with
L=O(1),W=O(N),S=O(N)
and
B=O(N)
. Then the
\log
value of the covering number of the DNN Laplacian space with respect to the
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
norm
\|F(x)\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}|F(x)|
, which is denoted by
\mathcal{N}(\delta,\Delta\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
, can be upper bounded by:

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{N}(\delta,\Delta\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})=O\left(S\Big[\log(\delta^{-1})+3^{L}\log(WB)\Big]\right)

(A.42)

Proof.

We firstly fix a sparsity pattern (i.e, the locations of the non-zero entries are fixed). Applying Lemma
45
yields that there exists some constant
C=O(1)
, such that the covering number with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
can be upper bounded by:

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\delta}{CW^{\frac{3^{L-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{L-1}-1}{2}}}\Big)^{-S}

Furthermore, note that the number of feasible configurations is upper bounded by
{(W+1)^{L}\choose S}\leq(W+1)^{LS}
[
63
,
18
]
. Then we plug this into the pervious estimation and yields:

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{N}(\delta,\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})

\displaystyle\leq\log\left[(W+1)^{LS}\Big(\frac{\delta}{CW^{\frac{3^{L-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{L-1}-1}{2}}}\Big)^{-S}\right]

\displaystyle\leq S\log\Big[\delta^{-1}(W+1)^{L}W^{\frac{3^{L-1}-1}{2}}(B\vee d)^{\frac{5\cdot 3^{L-1}-1}{2}}\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim S\Big[\log(\delta^{-1})+L\log(W)+3^{L}\log(W(B\vee d))\Big]

Note that here the dimension
d
is some constant. Thus, by plugging in thee given magnitudes
L=O(1),W=O(N),S=O(N)
and
B=O(N)
, we can further deduce that:

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{N}(\delta,\Delta\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\lesssim S\Big[\log(\delta^{-1})+3^{L}\log(WB)\Big]

This finishes our proof.

Lemma 47
(Local Rademacher Complexity Bound for Deep Ritz Method).

Consider a Deep Neural Network space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)=\Phi(L,W,S,B)
with
L=O(1),W=O(N),S=O(N)
and
B=O(N)
, where
N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
is fixed to be sufficiently large. Moreover, assume that the gradients and function value of
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega),V
and
f
are uniformly bounded

\max\Big\{\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\|u^{\ast}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},V_{max},\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\Big\}\leq C.

(A.43)

For any
\rho>0
, we consider a localized set
L_{\rho}
defined by:

{\bm{L}}_{\rho}(\Omega):=\{u:u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega),\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\leq\rho\}.

Then for any
\rho\gtrsim n^{-2}
, the Rademacher complexity of a localized function space
{\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega):=\Big\{h:=|\Omega|\cdot\left[\frac{1}{2}\Big(\|\nabla u\|^{2}-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|^{2}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}V(|u|^{2}-|u^{\ast}|^{2})-f(u-u^{\ast})\right]\ \ \Big|\ u\in L_{\rho}(\Omega)\Big\}
can be upper bounded by a sub-root function

\phi(\rho):=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{S3^{L}\rho}{n}\log\left(BWn\right)}\right).

i.e.
we have

\phi(4\rho)\leq 2\phi(\rho)\text{ and }R_{n}({\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega))\leq\phi(\rho).\

(A.44)

holds for all
\rho\gtrsim n^{-2}
.

Proof.

Firstly, we will check that for any
u\in L_{\rho}(\Omega)
, the corresponding function
h
in
{\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega)
is Lipschitz with respect to
u-u^{\ast}
and
\|\nabla u\|-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|
. Note that for any
u_{1},u_{2}\in L_{\rho}(\Omega)
with corresponding functions
h_{1},h_{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega)
, applying boundedness condition
4.10

yields:

\displaystyle|h_{1}(x)-h_{2}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\Big|\|\nabla u_{1}(x)\|^{2}-\|\nabla u_{2}(x)\|^{2}\Big|+\frac{1}{2}|V(x)||u_{1}(x)^{2}-u_{2}(x)^{2}|+|f(x)||u_{1}(x)-u_{2}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq C\Big|\|\nabla u_{1}(x)\|-\|\nabla u_{2}(x)\|\Big|+(C^{2}+C)|u_{1}(x)-u_{2}(x)|

\displaystyle=C\left|\Big(\|\nabla u_{1}(x)\|-\|\nabla u^{\ast}(x)\|\Big)-\Big(\|\nabla u_{2}(x)\|-\|\nabla u^{\ast}(x)\|\Big)\right|

\displaystyle+(C^{2}+C)\Big|(u_{1}(x)-u^{\ast}(x))-(u_{2}(x)-u^{\ast}(x))\Big|.

Let’s pick
L=C^{2}+C>C
. Applying the Talagrand Contraction Lemma
25
helps us upper bound the local Rademacher complexity
R_{n}({\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega))
by

\displaystyle R_{n}({\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega))

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{x}{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{u\in{\bm{L}}_{\rho}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\Big[\frac{1}{2}\Big(\|\nabla u\|^{2}-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|^{2}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}V(|u|^{2}-|u^{\ast}|^{2})-f(u-u^{\ast})\Big]\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2L{\mathbb{E}}_{x}{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{u\in{\bm{L}}_{\rho}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\Big(u(x_{i})-u^{\ast}(x_{i})\Big)\right]

\displaystyle+2L{\mathbb{E}}_{x^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma^{\prime}}\left[\sup_{u\in{\bm{L}}_{\rho}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}^{\prime}\Big(\|\nabla u(x_{i}^{\prime})\|-\|\nabla u^{\ast}(x_{i}^{\prime})\|\Big)\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim R_{n}\left(\Big\{u-u_{*}:u\in{\bm{L}}_{\rho}\Big\}\right)+R_{n}\left(\Big\{\|\nabla u\|-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|:u\in{\bm{L}}_{\rho}\Big\}\right)

From the localization constraint
\rho\geq\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}=\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|\nabla u-\nabla u^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}
, we can deduce that

\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\sqrt{\rho}\text{ and }\|\nabla u-\nabla u^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\sqrt{\rho}

(A.45)

Moreover, note that
\Omega\subset[0,1]^{d}
. Applying triangle inequality yields:

\displaystyle\Big\|\|\nabla u\|-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|\Big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\Big|\|\nabla u(x)\|-\|\nabla u^{\ast}(x)\|\Big|^{2}dx\leq\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u(x)-\nabla u^{\ast}(x)\|^{2}dx

(A.46)

\displaystyle=\|\nabla u-\nabla u^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\rho\Rightarrow\Big\|\|\nabla u\|-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|\Big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\sqrt{\rho}

Using inequality
A.45
and inequality
A.46
, we have:

\displaystyle R_{n}({\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega))

\displaystyle\lesssim R_{n}\left(\Big\{u-u_{*}:u\in{\bm{L}}_{\rho}\Big\}\right)+R_{n}\left(\Big\{\|\nabla u\|-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|:u\in{\bm{L}}_{\rho}\Big\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq R_{n}\left(\Big\{u-u^{\ast}:u\in\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\sqrt{\rho}\Big\}\right)

\displaystyle+R_{n}\left(\Big\{\|\nabla u\|-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|:u\in\Phi(L,W,S,B),\Big\|\|\nabla u\|-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|\Big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\sqrt{\rho}\Big\}\right)

[
20
,
59
]
showed a “upper isometry” property, where the metric
\|\cdot\|_{L_{2}}
is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{n,2}
with high probability. Combining this fact with Theorem
26
, we can bound the local Rademacher complexities using Dudley integral:

\displaystyle R_{n}({\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega))

\displaystyle\lesssim R_{n}\left(\Big\{u-u^{\ast}:u\in\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\sqrt{\rho}\Big\}\right)

\displaystyle+R_{n}\left(\Big\{\|\nabla u\|-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|:u\in\Phi(L,W,S,B),\Big\|\|\nabla u\|-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|\Big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\sqrt{\rho}\Big\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq R_{n}\left(\Big\{u-u^{\ast}:u\in\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{n,2}\leq 2\sqrt{\rho}\Big\}\right)

\displaystyle+R_{n}\left(\Big\{\|\nabla u\|-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|:u\in\Phi(L,W,S,B),\Big\|\|\nabla u\|-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|\Big\|_{n,2}\leq 2\sqrt{\rho}\Big\}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\inf_{0<\alpha<2\sqrt{\rho}}\big\{4\alpha+\frac{12}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\alpha}^{2\sqrt{\rho}}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\delta,\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{n})}d\delta\big\}

\displaystyle+\inf_{0<\alpha<2\sqrt{\rho}}\big\{4\alpha+\frac{12}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\alpha}^{2\sqrt{\rho}}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\delta,\nabla\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{n,2})}d\delta\big\}

\displaystyle\lesssim\inf_{0<\alpha<2\sqrt{\rho}}\big\{4\alpha+\frac{12}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\alpha}^{2\sqrt{\rho}}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\delta,\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})}d\delta\big\}

\displaystyle+\inf_{0<\alpha<2\sqrt{\rho}}\big\{4\alpha+\frac{12}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\alpha}^{2\sqrt{\rho}}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\delta,\nabla\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})}d\delta\big\}

For any
\rho\gtrsim\frac{1}{n^{2}}
, we pick
\alpha=\frac{1}{n}\lesssim\sqrt{\rho}
and plug in the upper bounds proved in Theorem
39
and Theorem
43
, which implies:

\displaystyle R_{n}({\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega))

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\frac{1}{n}}^{2\sqrt{\rho}}\sqrt{S\Big[\log(\delta^{-1})+3^{L}\log(WB)\Big]}d\delta+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\frac{1}{n}}^{2\sqrt{\rho}}\sqrt{S\Big[\log(\delta^{-1})+3^{L}\log(WB)\Big]}d\delta

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{S3^{L}\rho}{n}\log\left(BWn\right)}

Lemma 48
(Local Rademacher Complexity Bound for Physics Informed Neural Network).

Consider a Deep Neural Network space
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)=\Phi(L,W,S,B)
with
L=O(1),W=O(N),S=O(N)
and
B=O(N)
, where
N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
is fixed to be sufficiently large. Moreover, assume that the gradients and function value of
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega),V
and
f
are uniformly bounded

\max\Big\{\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\|\Delta u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\|u^{\ast}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},\|\Delta u^{\ast}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},V_{max},\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\Big\}\leq C.

(A.47)

For any
\rho>0
, we consider a localized set
M_{\rho}
defined by:

{\mathcal{M}}_{\rho}(\Omega):=\{u:u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega),\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\leq\rho\}.

Then for any
\rho\gtrsim n^{-2}
, the Rademacher complexity of a localized function space
{\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}(\Omega):=\Big\{h:=|\Omega|\cdot\left[(\Delta u-Vu+f)^{2}-(\Delta u^{\ast}-Vu^{\ast}+f)^{2}\right]\ \ \Big|\ u\in M_{\rho}(\Omega)\Big\}
can be upper bounded by a sub-root function

\phi(\rho):=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{S3^{L}\rho}{n}\log\left(BWn\right)}\right).

i.e.
we have

\phi(4\rho)\leq 2\phi(\rho)\text{ and }R_{n}({\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}(\Omega))\leq\phi(\rho).\

(A.48)

holds for all
\rho\gtrsim n^{-2}
.

Proof.

Firstly, we will check that for any
u\in L_{\rho}(\Omega)
, the corresponding function
h
in
{\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega)
is Lipschitz with respect to
u-u^{\ast}
and
\Delta u-\Delta u^{\ast}
. Note that for any
u_{1},u_{2}\in L_{\rho}(\Omega)
with corresponding functions
h_{1},h_{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\rho}(\Omega)
, applying boundedness condition
4.12
yields:

\displaystyle|h_{1}(x)-h_{2}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq|\Delta u_{1}-\Delta u_{2}-V(u_{1}-u_{2})||\Delta u_{1}-Vu_{1}+\Delta u_{2}-Vu_{2}+2f|

\displaystyle\leq(2C^{2}+4C)\left(|\Delta u_{1}-\Delta u_{2}|+C|u_{1}-u_{2}|\right)

\displaystyle=(2C^{2}+4C)\Big|(\Delta u_{1}(x)-\Delta u^{\ast}(x))-(\Delta u_{2}(x)-\Delta u^{\ast}(x))\Big|

\displaystyle+(2C^{3}+4C^{2})\Big|(u_{1}(x)-u^{\ast}(x))-(u_{2}(x)-u^{\ast}(x))\Big|

Let’s pick
L=\max\{2C^{2}+4C,2C^{3}+4C^{2}\}
. Applying the Talagrand Contraction Lemma
25
helps us upper bound the local Rademacher complexity
R_{n}({\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}(\Omega))
by

\displaystyle R_{n}({\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}(\Omega))

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{x}{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\Big[\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\rho}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\left[(\Delta u-Vu+f)^{2}-(\Delta u^{\ast}-Vu^{\ast}+f)^{2}\right]\Big]

\displaystyle\leq 2L{\mathbb{E}}_{x}{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\rho}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\Big(u(x_{i})-u^{\ast}(x_{i})\Big)\right]

\displaystyle+2L{\mathbb{E}}_{x^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma^{\prime}}\left[\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\rho}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}^{\prime}\Big(\Delta u(x_{i}^{\prime})-\Delta u^{\ast}(x_{i}^{\prime})\Big)\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim R_{n}\left(\Big\{u-u_{*}:u\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\rho}\Big\}\right)+R_{n}\left(\Big\{\Delta u-\Delta u^{\ast}:u\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\rho}\Big\}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim R_{n}\left(\Big\{u-u^{\ast}:u\in\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\sqrt{\rho}\Big\}\right)

\displaystyle+R_{n}\left(\Big\{\Delta u-\Delta u^{\ast}:u\in\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\Delta u-\Delta u^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\sqrt{\rho}\Big\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq R_{n}\left(\Big\{u-u^{\ast}:u\in\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{n,2}\leq 2\sqrt{\rho}\Big\}\right)

\displaystyle+R_{n}\left(\Big\{\Delta u-\Delta u^{\ast}:u\in\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\Delta u-\Delta u^{\ast}\|_{n,2}\leq 2\sqrt{\rho}\Big\}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\inf_{0<\alpha<2\sqrt{\rho}}\big\{4\alpha+\frac{12}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\alpha}^{2\sqrt{\rho}}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\delta,\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{n})}d\delta\big\}

\displaystyle+\inf_{0<\alpha<2\sqrt{\rho}}\big\{4\alpha+\frac{12}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\alpha}^{2\sqrt{\rho}}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\delta,\Delta\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{n,2})}d\delta\big\}

\displaystyle\lesssim\inf_{0<\alpha<2\sqrt{\rho}}\big\{4\alpha+\frac{12}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\alpha}^{2\sqrt{\rho}}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\delta,\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})}d\delta\big\}

\displaystyle+\inf_{0<\alpha<2\sqrt{\rho}}\big\{4\alpha+\frac{12}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\alpha}^{2\sqrt{\rho}}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\delta,\Delta\Phi(L,W,S,B),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})}d\delta\big\}

For any
\rho\gtrsim\frac{1}{n^{2}}
, we pick
\alpha=\frac{1}{n}\lesssim\sqrt{\rho}
and plug in the upper bounds proved in Theorem
39
and Theorem
43
, which implies:

\displaystyle R_{n}({\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}(\Omega))

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\frac{1}{n}}^{2\sqrt{\rho}}\sqrt{S\Big[\log(\delta^{-1})+3^{L}\log(WB)\Big]}d\delta+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\frac{1}{n}}^{2\sqrt{\rho}}\sqrt{S\Big[\log(\delta^{-1})+3^{L}\log(WB)\Big]}d\delta

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{S3^{L}\rho}{n}\log\left(BWn\right)}

A.4
Proof of The Meta-Theorem for PINN

Proof.

To upper bound the excess risk
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}
, following
[
78
,
47
,
14
]
, we decompose the excess risk into approximation error and generalization error with probability
1-e^{-t}
:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}(\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}})={\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})

\displaystyle=\big[{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}})\big]+\big[{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})\big]

(A.49)

\displaystyle+\big[{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})\big]+\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]

\displaystyle\leq\big[{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}})\big]+\big[{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})\big]+\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]

\displaystyle\leq\big[{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})+{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u^{\ast})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}})\big]

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{2}\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]+\frac{t}{2n},

where the expectation is on all sampled data. The inequality of the third line is because the
u
is the minimizer of the empirical loss
{\mathcal{E}}_{n}
in the solution set
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
, so we have
{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u)\leq{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})
. The last inequality is based on the Bernstein inequality. The variance of
h=|\Omega|\cdot\left[(\Delta u-Vu+f)^{2}-(\Delta u^{\ast}-Vu^{\ast}+f)^{2}\right]
can be bounded by
\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]
due to the strong convexity of the variation objective (
A.51
). According to the Brenstein inequality, we know with probability
1-e^{-t}
we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u^{\ast})-{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})+{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})\leq\sqrt{\frac{t\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]}{n}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]+\frac{t}{2n}.

Note that
A.53
holds for all function lies in the function space
{\mathcal{F}}
. Thus, we can take
u_{{\mathcal{F}}}:=\arg\min_{u_{0}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{0})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)
and finally get

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})+{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u^{\ast})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}})}_{\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{gen}}}+\frac{3}{2}\underbrace{\inf_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)}_{\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}}+\frac{t}{2n}.

This inequality decompose the excess risk to the generalization error
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{gen}}:={\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})+{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u^{\ast})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}})
and the approximation error
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}=\inf_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)
.

We’ll focus on providing fast rate upper bounds of the generalization error for the two estimators using the localization techinque
[
3
,
78
]
. To achieve the fast generalization bound, we focus on the following normalized empirical process

\displaystyle\tilde{{\mathcal{T}}}_{r}(\Omega):=\big\{\tilde{h}(x):=\frac{\mathbb{E}[h]-h(x)}{{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r}\ |\ h\in{\mathcal{T}}(\Omega)\big\}\ (r>0).

First, we try to bound the expectation of the normalized empirical process. Applying the Symmetrization Lemma
24
, we can first bound the expectation as

\displaystyle\sup_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{T}_{r}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}_{x^{\prime}}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{i}^{\prime})\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{x^{\prime}}\left[\sup_{h\in T(\Omega)}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{h(x_{i}^{\prime})-{\mathbb{E}}[h]}{{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r}\Big|\right]\leq 2R_{n}(\hat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{r}(\Omega)).

where the function class
\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega)
is defined as:

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{r}(\Omega):=\big\{\hat{h}(x):=\frac{h(x)}{{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r}\ |\ h\in{\mathcal{T}}(\Omega)\big\},

where
{\mathcal{T}}(\Omega)=\Big\{h:=|\Omega|\cdot\left[(\Delta u-Vu+f)^{2}-(\Delta u^{\ast}-Vu^{\ast}+f)^{2}\right]\Big\}.
Then Applying the Peeling Lemma to any function
h\in{\mathcal{T}}(\Omega)
helps us upper bound the local Rademacher complexity
R_{n}(\hat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{r}(\Omega))
with the function
\phi
defined in equation
4.26
:

R_{n}(\hat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{r}(\Omega))={\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{x}\Big[\sup_{h\in{\mathcal{T}}(\Omega)}\frac{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}h(x_{i})}{{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r}\Big]\right]\leq\frac{4\phi(r)}{r}.

Combining all inequalities derived above yields:

\sup_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{T}_{r}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}_{x^{\prime}}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{i}^{\prime})\right]\leq 2R_{n}(\hat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{r}(\Omega))\leq\frac{8\phi(r)}{r}\ (r>0).

(A.50)

Secondly we’ll apply the Talagrand concentration inequality, which requires us to verify the condition needed. We will first check that the expectation value
{\mathbb{E}}[h]
is always non-negative for any
h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[h]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\int_{\Omega}|\Omega|\cdot(\frac{1}{2}|\nabla u(x)|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}V(x)|u(x)|^{2}-f(x)u(x))dx

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\int_{\Omega}|\Omega|\cdot(\frac{1}{2}|\nabla u^{\star}(x)|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}V(x)|u^{\star}(x)|^{2}-f(x)u^{\star}(x))dx

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\geq 0\Rightarrow{\mathbb{E}}[h]\geq 0.

We will proceed to verify that any
\tilde{h}=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h]-h}{{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{T}}}_{r}(\Omega)
is of bounded inf-norm. We need to prove that any
h\in{\mathcal{T}}(\Omega)
is of bounded inf-norm beforehand. Using boundedness condition listed in equation
4.25
implies:

\displaystyle\|h\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=|\Omega|\cdot\|(\Delta u-Vu+f)^{2}-(\Delta u^{\ast}-Vu^{\ast}+f)^{2}\|_{\infty}=|\Omega|\cdot\|(\Delta u-Vu+f)^{2}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq|\Omega|\cdot(\|\Delta u\|_{\infty}+V_{\text{max}}\|u\|_{\infty}+\|f\|_{\infty})^{2}\leq|\Omega|(V_{\text{max}}+2)^{2}C^{2}

By taking
M:=|\Omega|(V_{\text{max}}+2)^{2}C^{2}
, we then have
\|h\|_{\infty}\leq M
for all
h\in{\mathcal{T}}(\Omega)
. Note that the denominator can be lower bounded by
|{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r|\geq r>0
. Combining these two inequalities help us upper bound the inf-norm
\|\tilde{h}\|_{\infty}=\sup_{x\in\Omega}|\tilde{h}(x)|
as follows:

\displaystyle\|\tilde{h}\|_{\infty}=\frac{\|{\mathbb{E}}[h]-h\|_{\infty}}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r|}\leq\frac{2\|h\|_{\infty}}{r}\leq\frac{2M}{r}=:\beta.

We will then check the normalized functions
\frac{\mathbb{E}[h]-h(x)}{{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r}
in
\tilde{T}_{r}(\Omega)
have bounded second moment, which is satisfied because of the regularity results of the PDE. We aim to show that there exist some constants
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}>0
, such that for any
h\in{\mathcal{T}}(\Omega)
, the following inequality holds:

\alpha\mathbb{E}[h^{2}]\leq\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\alpha^{\prime}\mathbb{E}[h].

(A.51)

The RHS of the inequality follows from strong convexity of the PINN objective function proved in Theorem
23
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[h]={\mathcal{E}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})\geq\frac{1}{\min\{1,C_{\min}\}}\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}

The LHS of the inequality follows from boundedness condition listed in equation
4.25
and the QM-AM inequality:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[h^{2}]

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\left[(\Delta u-Vu+f)^{2}-(\Delta u^{\ast}-Vu^{\ast}+f)^{2}\right]^{2}dx=\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u-Vu+f)^{4}dx

\displaystyle\leq M^{2}\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u-Vu-\Delta u^{\ast}+Vu^{\ast})^{2}dx\leq 2M^{2}\int_{\Omega}[(\Delta u-\Delta u^{\ast})^{2}+V^{2}(u-u^{\ast})^{2}]dx

\displaystyle\leq 2M^{2}\max\{1,V_{\text{max}}^{2}\}\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}

By picking
\alpha^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\min\{1,C_{\min}\}}
and
\alpha=\frac{1}{M^{2}\max\{1,V_{\text{max}}^{2}\}}
, we have finished proving inequality
A.51
. Then we can can upper bound the expectation
{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{h}^{2}]
as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\tilde{h}^{2}]=\frac{\mathbb{E}[(h-\mathbb{E}[h])^{2}]}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r|^{2}}=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h^{2}]-{\mathbb{E}}[h]^{2}}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r|^{2}}\leq\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h^{2}]}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r|^{2}}.

Using the fact that
{\mathbb{E}}[h]\geq 0
and inequality
A.51
, we can lower bound the denominator
|{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r|^{2}
as follows:

\displaystyle|{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r|^{2}\geq 2{\mathbb{E}}[h]r\geq\frac{2r\alpha}{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}[h^{2}].

Therefore, we can deduce that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\tilde{h}^{2}]\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[h^{2}]}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r|^{2}}\leq\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h^{2}]}{\frac{2r\alpha}{\alpha^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}[h^{2}]}=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2r\alpha}=:\sigma^{2}.

Hence, any function in the localized class
\tilde{{\mathcal{T}}}_{r}(\Omega)
is of bounded second moment.

It is easy to check that for any
\tilde{h}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{T}}}_{r}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{h}]=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h]-{\mathbb{E}}[h]}{{\mathbb{E}}[h]+r}=0,

i.e.
any function in the localized class
\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega)
is of zero mean.

Now we have verified that any function
\tilde{h}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega)
satisfies all the required conditions. By taking
\mu
to be the uniform distribution on the domain
\Omega
and applying Talagrand’s Concentration inequality given in Lemma
27
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\sup_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{T}}}_{r}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{i})\geq 2\sup_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{T}}}_{r}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}_{x^{\prime}}\Big[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{i}^{\prime})\Big]+\sqrt{\frac{2t\sigma^{2}}{n}}+\frac{2t\beta}{n}\right]\leq e^{-t}.

By using the upper bound deduced above and plugging in the expressions of
\beta
and
\sigma
, we can rewrite Talagrand’s Concentration Inequality in the following way. With probability at least
1-e^{-t}
, the inequality below holds:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{i})\leq\sup_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}_{x^{\prime}}\Big[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{i}^{\prime})\Big]+\sqrt{\frac{2t\sigma^{2}}{n}}+\frac{2t\beta}{n}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{16\phi(r)}{r}+\sqrt{\frac{t\alpha^{\prime}}{n\alpha r}}+\frac{4Mt}{nr}=:\psi(r)

Let’s pick the critical radius
r_{0}
to be:

\displaystyle r_{0}=\max\{2^{14}r^{\ast},\frac{24Mt}{n},\frac{36\alpha^{\prime}t}{\alpha n}\}.

(A.52)

Note that concavity of the function
\phi
implies that
\phi(r)\leq r
for any
r\geq r^{\ast}
. Combining this with the first inequality listed in
4.26
yields:

\displaystyle\frac{16\phi(r)}{r}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{11}\phi(\frac{r_{0}}{2^{14}})}{2^{14}\frac{r_{0}}{2^{14}}}=\frac{1}{8}\times\frac{\phi(\frac{r_{0}}{2^{14}})}{\frac{r_{)}}{2^{14}}}\leq\frac{1}{8}.

On the other hand, applying equation
A.52
yields:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}t}{n\alpha r_{0}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}t}{n\alpha}\frac{\alpha n}{36\alpha^{\prime}t}}=\frac{1}{6},

\displaystyle\frac{4Mt}{nr_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4Mt}{n}\times\frac{n}{24Mt}=\frac{1}{6}.

Summing the three inequalities above implies:

\displaystyle\psi(r_{0})=\frac{16\phi(r_{0})}{r_{0}}+\sqrt{\frac{t\alpha^{\prime}}{n\alpha r_{0}}}+\frac{4Mt}{nr_{0}}\leq\frac{1}{8}+\frac{1}{6}+\frac{1}{6}<\frac{1}{2}.

By picking
r=r_{0}
, we can further deduce that for any function
u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
, the following inequality holds with probability
1-e^{-t}
:

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathcal{E}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u)+{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u^{\ast})}{{\mathcal{E}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})+r_{0}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\psi(r_{0})<\frac{1}{2}.

Multiplying the denominator on both sides indicates:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{gen}}={\mathcal{E}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u)+{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u^{\ast})\leq\frac{1}{2}\Big[{\mathcal{E}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})\Big]+\frac{1}{2}r_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}+\frac{1}{2}r_{0}.

Substituting the upper bound above into the decomposition
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}\leq\Delta E_{\text{gen}}+\frac{3}{2}\Delta E_{\text{app}}+\frac{t}{2n}
yields that with probability
1-e^{-t}
, we have:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}\leq\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{gen}}+\frac{3}{2}\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}+\frac{t}{2n}\leq\frac{1}{2}\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}+\frac{1}{2}r_{0}+\frac{3}{2}\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}+\frac{t}{2n}.

Simplifying the inequality above yields that with probability
1-e^{-t}
, we have:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\leq r_{0}+3\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}+\frac{t}{n}=3\inf_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)+\max\{2^{14}r^{\ast},24M\frac{t}{n},\frac{36\alpha^{\prime}}{\alpha}\frac{t}{n}\}+\frac{t}{n}

\displaystyle\lesssim\inf_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)+\max\Big\{r^{*},\frac{t}{n}\Big\}

Moreover, using strong convexity of the PINN objective function proved in Theorem
22
implies:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}={\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})\geq\frac{1}{\{1,C_{\min}\}}\|\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}

Combining the two bounds above yields that with probability
1-e^{-t}
, we have:

\displaystyle\|\hat{u}_{\text{PINN}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\lesssim\inf_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)+\max\Big\{r^{*},\frac{t}{n}\Big\}

A.5
Proof of The Meta-Theorem for MDRM

Proof.

To upper bound the excess risk
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(N,n)}:={\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})
, following
[
78
,
47
,
14
]
, we decompose the excess risk into approximation error and generalization error with probability
1-e^{-t}
:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(N,n)}=\big[{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]

\displaystyle=\big[{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}})-{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}})\big]+\big[{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}})-{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})\big]

(A.53)

\displaystyle+\big[{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})\big]+\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]

\displaystyle\leq\big[{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}})-{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}})\big]+\big[{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})\big]+\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]

\displaystyle\leq\big[{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})+{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(u^{\ast})-{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}})]\big]

\displaystyle+2\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]+\frac{4t}{\min\{N,n\}},

where the expectation is on all sampled data. The inequality of the third line is because
\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}}
is the minimizer of the empirical loss
{\mathcal{E}}_{n}
in the solution set
{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
, so we have
{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}})\leq{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})
. The last inequality is based on the Bernstein inequality. For any
u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
, we use
h_{{\mathcal{F}},1},h_{{\mathcal{F}},2}
to denote the following two functions:

\displaystyle h_{{\mathcal{F}},1}:

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\|\nabla u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\|^{2}-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|^{2}\Big),

\displaystyle h_{{\mathcal{F}},2}:

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}V(|u_{{\mathcal{F}}}|^{2}-|u^{\ast}|^{2})-f(u_{{\mathcal{F}}}-u^{\ast}).

Applying Bernstein’s inequality twice to
h_{{\mathcal{F}},1}
and
h_{{\mathcal{F}},2}
implies that there exists some constant
C_{q}
, such that with probability
1-2e^{-C_{q}t}
, the following two inequalities hold simultaneously:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{N}(h_{{\mathcal{F}},1})-{\mathcal{E}}(h_{{\mathcal{F}},1})

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{t\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{\prime}}{\mathcal{E}}[h_{{\mathcal{F}},1}^{2}]}{N}},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(h_{{\mathcal{F}},2})-{\mathcal{E}}(h_{{\mathcal{F}},2})

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{t\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{\prime}}{\mathcal{E}}[h_{{\mathcal{F}},2}^{2}]}{n}}.

Note that the variance sum
{\mathcal{E}}[h_{{\mathcal{F}},1}^{2}]+{\mathcal{E}}[h_{{\mathcal{F}},2}^{2}]
can be upper bounded by
\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\alpha}\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]
due to the strong convexity of the variation objective (
A.57
). Adding the two inequalities above implies with probability
1-2e^{-C_{q}t}
we have:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(u^{\ast})-{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})+{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}_{N}(h_{{\mathcal{F}},1})-{\mathcal{E}}(h_{{\mathcal{F}},1})+{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(h_{{\mathcal{F}},2})-{\mathcal{E}}(h_{{\mathcal{F}},2})

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{t\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{\prime}}{\mathcal{E}}[h_{{\mathcal{F}},1}^{2}]}{N}}+\sqrt{\frac{t\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{\prime}}{\mathcal{E}}[h_{{\mathcal{F}},2}^{2}]}{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{2t\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{\prime}}\Big({\mathcal{E}}[h_{{\mathcal{F}},1}^{2}]+{\mathcal{E}}[h_{{\mathcal{F}},1}^{2}]\Big)}{\min\{N,n\}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{2t\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]}{\min\{N,n\}}}\leq\big[{\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\big]+\frac{4t}{\min\{N,n\}}.

Note that
A.53
holds for all function lies in the function space
{\mathcal{F}}
. Thus, we can take
u_{{\mathcal{F}}}:=\arg\min_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)
and finally get:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(N,n)}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})+{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(u^{\ast})-{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}})}_{\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{gen}}}+2\underbrace{\inf_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)}_{\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}}+\frac{4t}{n}.

This inequality decomposes the excess risk to the generalization error
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{gen}}:={\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})+{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(u^{\ast})-{\mathcal{E}}_{N,n}(\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}})
and the approximation error
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}=\inf_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)
.
From the lemmata proved in Section
, we already have an estimation of the approximation error’s convergence rate. So now we’ll focus on providing fast rate upper bounds of the generalization error for the two estimators using the localization techinque
[
3
,
78
]
. To achieve the fast generalization bound, we focus on the following two normalized empirical processes:

\displaystyle\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,1}(\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{\tilde{h}_{1}(x):=\frac{\mathbb{E}[h_{1}]-h_{1}(x)}{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r}\ |\ (h_{1},h_{2})\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)\big\}\ (r>0),

\displaystyle\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,2}(\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{\tilde{h}_{2}(x):=\frac{\mathbb{E}[h_{2}]-h_{2}(x)}{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r}\ |\ (h_{1},h_{2})\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)\big\}\ (r>0).

where the space
{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)
is defined as:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega):=\Big\{(h_{1},h_{2})\big|

\displaystyle h_{1}:=|\Omega|\cdot\left[\frac{1}{2}\Big(\|\nabla u\|^{2}-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|^{2}\Big)\right],

\displaystyle h_{2}:=|\Omega|\cdot\left[\frac{1}{2}V(|u|^{2}-|u^{\ast}|^{2})-f(u-u^{\ast})\right],u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)\Big\}.

First, we try to bound the expectation of the two normalized empirical processes. Applying the Symmetrization Lemma
24
, we can first bound the two expectations as:

\displaystyle\sup_{\tilde{h}_{1}\in\tilde{S}_{r,1}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}_{y^{\prime}}\left[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{h}_{1}(y_{i}^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{y^{\prime}}\left[\sup_{h_{1}\in S_{1}(\Omega)}\Big|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{h_{1}(y_{i}^{\prime})-{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]}{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r}\Big|\right]\leq 2R_{N}(\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,1}(\Omega)),

\displaystyle\sup_{\tilde{h}_{2}\in\tilde{S}_{r,2}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}_{y}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{h}_{2}(y_{j})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{y}\left[\sup_{h_{2}\in S_{2}(\Omega)}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{h_{2}(y_{j})-{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]}{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r}\Big|\right]\leq 2R_{n}(\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,2}(\Omega)).

where the function classes
\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,k}(\Omega)\ (1\leq k\leq 2)
are defined as:

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,1}(\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{\hat{h}_{1}(x):=\frac{h_{1}(x)}{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r}\ |\ (h_{1},h_{2})\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,2}(\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{\hat{h}_{2}(x):=\frac{h_{2}(x)}{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r}\ |\ (h_{1},h_{2})\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)\big\}.

Applying the modified Peeling Lemma
29
to any function
h=(h_{1},h_{2})\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)
helps us upper bound the sum of the two local Rademacher complexities
R_{N}(\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,1}(\Omega))+R_{n}(\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,2}(\Omega))
with the function
\phi
defined in equation
5.5
:

\displaystyle R_{N}(\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,1}(\Omega))+R_{n}(\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,2}(\Omega))

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{y}\Big[\sup_{h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)}\frac{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}h_{1}(y_{i})}{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r}\Big]\right]+{\mathbb{E}}_{\tau}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{y^{\prime}}\Big[\sup_{h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)}\frac{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tau_{j}h_{2}(y_{j}^{\prime})}{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r}\Big]\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{y,y^{\prime}}\Big[\sup_{h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)}\frac{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}h_{1}(y_{i})}{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r}+\sup_{h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)}\frac{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tau_{j}h_{2}(y_{j}^{\prime})}{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r}\Big]\right]

\displaystyle=R_{N,n}(\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega))\leq\frac{4\phi(r)}{r}.

Combining all inequalities derived above yields:

\displaystyle\sup_{\tilde{h}_{1}\in\tilde{S}_{r,1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{y^{\prime}}\left[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{h}_{1}(y_{i}^{\prime})\right]+\sup_{\tilde{h}_{2}\in\tilde{S}_{r,2}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}_{y}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{h}_{2}(y_{j})\right]

(A.54)

\displaystyle\leq 2R_{N}(\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,1}(\Omega))+2R_{n}(\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,2}(\Omega))=2R_{N,n}(\hat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r}(\Omega))\leq\frac{8\phi(r)}{r}\ (r>0).

Secondly we’ll apply the Talagrand concentration inequality to the two function classes
\tilde{S}_{r,1}(\Omega)
and
\tilde{S}_{r,2}(\Omega)
, which requires us to verify the conditions needed. We will first check that the expectation sum
{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]
is always non-negative for any
(h_{1},h_{2})\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\int_{\Omega}|\Omega|\cdot(\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla u(x)\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}V(x)|u(x)|^{2}-f(x)u(x))dx

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\int_{\Omega}|\Omega|\cdot(\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla u^{\star}(x)\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}V(x)|u^{\star}(x)|^{2}-f(x)u^{\star}(x))dx

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\geq 0\Rightarrow{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]\geq 0.

Next, We will verify that
\tilde{S}_{r,1}(\Omega)
satisfies all three requirements. At first, we will show that any
\tilde{h}_{1}=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]-h_{1}}{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,1}(\Omega)
is of bounded inf-norm. We need to prove that any
h_{1}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}(\Omega)
is of bounded inf-norm beforehand. Using boundedness condition listed in equation
5.4
implies:

\displaystyle\|h_{1}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\|\frac{1}{2}\Big(\|\nabla u\|^{2}-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|^{2}\Big)\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{2}\Big(\|\nabla u\|_{\infty}^{2}+\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|_{\infty}^{2}\Big)\leq C^{2}.

By taking
M_{1}:=C^{2}
, we then have
\|h_{1}\|_{\infty}\leq M_{1}
for all
h_{1}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}(\Omega)
. Note that the denominator of
\tilde{h}_{1}
can be lower bounded by
|{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r|\geq r>0
. Combining these two inequalities help us upper bound the inf-norm
\|\tilde{h}_{1}\|_{\infty}=\sup_{x\in\Omega}|\tilde{h}_{1}(x)|
as follows:

\displaystyle\|\tilde{h}_{1}\|_{\infty}=\frac{\|{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]-h_{1}\|_{\infty}}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r|}\leq\frac{2\|h_{1}\|_{\infty}}{r}\leq\frac{2M_{1}}{r}=:\beta_{1}.

Also, it is easy to check that for any
\tilde{h}_{1}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,1}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{h}_{1}]=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]-{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]}{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r}=0,

i.e.
any function in the localized class
\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,1}(\Omega)
is of zero mean.

Moreover, we take
\sigma_{1}^{2}=\sup_{\tilde{h}_{1}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,1}(\Omega)}{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{h}_{1}^{2}]
to be the upper bound on the second moment of functions in
\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,1}(\Omega)
. Now we have verified that any function
\tilde{h}_{1}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,1}(\Omega)
satisfies all the required conditions. By taking
\mu
to be the uniform distribution on the domain
\Omega
and applying Talagrand’s Concentration inequality given in Lemma
27
, we have:

\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\sup_{\tilde{h}_{1}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,1}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{h}_{1}(x_{i})\geq 2\sup_{\tilde{h}_{1}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,1}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{h}_{1}(y_{i})\Big]+\sqrt{\frac{2t\sigma_{1}^{2}}{N}}+\frac{2t\beta_{1}}{N}\right]\leq e^{-t}.

(A.55)

Moreover, We will verify that
\tilde{S}_{r,2}(\Omega)
also satisfies all three requirements. At first, we will show that any
\tilde{h}_{2}=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]-h_{2}}{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,2}(\Omega)
is of bounded inf-norm. We need to prove that any
h_{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{2}(\Omega)
is of bounded inf-norm beforehand. Using boundedness condition listed in equation
5.4
implies:

\displaystyle\|h_{2}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\|\frac{1}{2}V(|u|^{2}-|u^{\ast}|^{2})-f(u-u^{\ast})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}V_{\text{max}}\Big(\|u\|_{\infty}^{2}+\|u^{\ast}\|_{\infty}^{2}\Big)+\|f\|_{\infty}\Big(\|u\|_{\infty}+\|u^{\ast}\|_{\infty}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}V_{\text{max}}\times 2C^{2}+2C^{2}=(V_{\text{max}}+2)C^{2}.

By taking
M_{2}:=(V_{\text{max}}+2)C^{2}
, we then have
\|h_{2}\|_{\infty}\leq M_{2}
for all
h_{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{2}(\Omega)
. Note that the denominator of
\tilde{h}_{2}
can be lower bounded by
|{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r|\geq r>0
. Combining these two inequalities help us upper bound the inf-norm
\|\tilde{h}_{2}\|_{\infty}=\sup_{x\in\Omega}|\tilde{h}_{2}(x)|
as follows:

\displaystyle\|\tilde{h}_{2}\|_{\infty}=\frac{\|{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]-h_{2}\|_{\infty}}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r|}\leq\frac{2\|h_{2}\|_{\infty}}{r}\leq\frac{2M_{2}}{r}=:\beta_{2}.

Also, it is easy to check that for any
\tilde{h}_{2}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,2}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{h}_{2}]=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]-{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]}{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r}=0,

i.e.
any function in the localized class
\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,2}(\Omega)
is of zero mean.

Moreover, we take
\sigma_{2}^{2}=\sup_{\tilde{h}_{2}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,2}(\Omega)}{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{h}_{2}^{2}]
to be the upper bound on the second moment of functions in
\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,2}(\Omega)
. Now we have verified that any function
\tilde{h}_{2}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,2}(\Omega)
satisfies all the required conditions. By taking
\mu
to be the uniform distribution on the domain
\Omega
and applying Talagrand’s Concentration inequality given in Lemma
27
, we have:

\mathbb{P}_{x^{\prime}}\left[\sup_{\tilde{h}_{2}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,2}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{h}_{2}(x_{j}^{\prime})\geq 2\sup_{\tilde{h}_{2}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,2}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}_{y^{\prime}}\Big[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{h}_{2}(y_{j}^{\prime})\Big]+\sqrt{\frac{2t\sigma_{2}^{2}}{n}}+\frac{2t\beta_{2}}{n}\right]\leq e^{-t}.

(A.56)

By applying a union bound to the two inequalities derived in
A.55
and
A.56
, we can derive that with probability at least
1-2e^{-t}
, the inequality below holds:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{h}_{1}(x_{i}^{\prime})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{j})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\tilde{h}_{1}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,1}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{h}_{1}(x_{i})+\sup_{\tilde{h}_{2}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,2}(\Omega)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{h}_{2}(x_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{\tilde{h}_{1}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,1}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{h}_{1}(y_{i})\Big]+\sqrt{\frac{2t\sigma_{1}^{2}}{N}}+\frac{2t\beta_{1}}{N}

\displaystyle+2\sup_{\tilde{h}_{2}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,2}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}_{y^{\prime}}\Big[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{h}_{2}(y_{j}^{\prime})\Big]+\sqrt{\frac{2t\sigma_{2}^{2}}{n}}+\frac{2t\beta_{2}}{n}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{16\phi(r)}{r}+\sqrt{\frac{2t}{n}}(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})+\frac{2t(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})}{n}.

By the definition of
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
, we have that the term
\frac{2t(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})}{n}
can be upper bounded by:

\displaystyle\frac{2t(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})}{n}=\frac{4t(M_{1}+M_{2})}{nr}\leq\frac{4(V_{\text{max}}+3)C^{2}t}{nr}.

Now we will derive some upper bound on the sum
\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}
. By definition we have that:

\displaystyle(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})^{2}\leq 2(\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle=2\Big[\sup_{\tilde{h}_{1}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,1}(\Omega)}{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{h}_{1}^{2}]+\sup_{\tilde{h}_{2}\in\tilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{r,2}(\Omega)}{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{h}_{2}^{2}]\Big]

\displaystyle=2\Big[\sup_{h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)}\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}^{2}]-{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]^{2}}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r|^{2}}+\sup_{h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)}\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}^{2}]-{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]^{2}}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r|^{2}}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq 4\sup_{h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)}\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}^{2}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}^{2}]}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r|^{2}}.

Now it suffices to derive an upper bound of
\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}^{2}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}^{2}]}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r|^{2}}
for any
h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)
. The existence of such an upper bound is guaranteed because of the regularity results of the PDE. We aim to show that there exist some constants
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}>0
, such that for any
h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)
, the following inequality holds:

\alpha(\mathbb{E}[h_{1}^{2}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}^{2}])\leq\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq\alpha^{\prime}({\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]).

(A.57)

The RHS of the inequality follows from strong convexity of the DRM objective function proved in Theorem
22
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]={\mathcal{E}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})\geq\frac{\min\{1,V_{\text{min}}\}}{4}\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}.

The LHS of the inequality follows from boundedness condition listed in equation
5.4
and the QM-AM inequality:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[h_{1}^{2}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{4}\Big(\|\nabla u\|^{2}-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|^{2}\Big)^{2}dx+\int_{\Omega}\left[\frac{1}{2}V(|u|^{2}-|u^{\ast}|^{2})-f(u-u^{\ast})\right]^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Omega}\Big(\|\nabla u\|^{2}-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|^{2}\Big)^{2}dx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}V^{2}(|u|^{2}-|u^{\ast}|^{2})^{2}dx+2\int_{\Omega}f^{2}(u-u^{\ast})^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Omega}\Big|\|\nabla u\|-\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|\Big|^{2}(\|\nabla u\|+\|\nabla u^{\ast}\|)^{2}dx+\frac{1}{2}V_{\text{max}}^{2}\int_{\Omega}\Big||u|-|u^{\ast}|\Big|^{2}(|u|+|u^{\ast}|)^{2}dx

\displaystyle+2C^{2}\int_{\Omega}(u-u^{\ast})^{2}dx\leq C^{2}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u-\nabla u^{\ast}\|^{2}dx+2C^{2}(1+V_{\text{max}}^{2})\int_{\Omega}|u-u^{\ast}|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq 2C^{2}(1+V_{\text{max}}^{2})\|u-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}.

By picking
\alpha^{\prime}=\frac{4}{\min\{1,V_{\text{min}}\}}
and
\alpha=\frac{1}{2C^{2}(1+V_{\text{max}}^{2})}
, we have finished proving inequality
A.57
. Then we can can upper bound the term
\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}^{2}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}^{2}]}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r|^{2}}
as:

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}^{2}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}^{2}]}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r|^{2}}\leq\frac{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\alpha}\Big({\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]\Big)}{2r\Big({\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]\Big)}\leq\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\alpha r}.

Combining the bounds derived above helps us upper bound the term
\sqrt{\frac{2t}{n}}(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})
as below:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2t}{n}}(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})\leq\sqrt{\frac{8t}{n}}\sqrt{\sup_{h\in{\mathcal{S}}(\Omega)}\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}^{2}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}^{2}]}{|{\mathbb{E}}[h_{1}]+{\mathbb{E}}[h_{2}]+r|^{2}}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{4\alpha^{\prime}t}{n\alpha r}}

Thus, using the two upper bounds on
\sqrt{\frac{2t}{n}}(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})
and
\frac{2t(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})}{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{h}_{1}(x_{i}^{\prime})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{j})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{16\phi(r)}{r}+\sqrt{\frac{2t}{n}}(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})+\frac{2t(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})}{n}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{16\phi(r)}{r}+\sqrt{\frac{4\alpha^{\prime}t}{n\alpha r}}+\frac{4(V_{\text{max}}+3)C^{2}t}{nr}=\psi(r)

Let’s pick the critical radius
r_{0}
to be:

\displaystyle r_{0}=\max\{2^{14}r^{\ast},\frac{24Mt}{n},\frac{144\alpha^{\prime}t}{\alpha n}\}.

(A.58)

Note that concavity of the function
\phi
implies that
\phi(r)\leq r
for any
r\geq r^{\ast}
. Combining this with the first inequality listed in
5.5
yields:

\displaystyle\frac{16\phi(r_{0})}{r_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{11}\phi(\frac{r_{0}}{2^{14}})}{2^{14}\frac{r_{0}}{2^{14}}}=\frac{1}{8}\times\frac{\phi(\frac{r_{0}}{2^{14}})}{\frac{r_{)}}{2^{14}}}\leq\frac{1}{8}.

On the other hand, applying equation
A.58
yields:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{4\alpha^{\prime}t}{n\alpha r_{0}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{4\alpha^{\prime}t}{n\alpha}\frac{\alpha n}{144\alpha^{\prime}t}}=\frac{1}{6},

\displaystyle\frac{4(V_{\text{max}}+3)C^{2}t}{nr_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4(V_{\text{max}}+3)C^{2}t}{n}\times\frac{n}{24(V_{\text{max}}+3)C^{2}t}=\frac{1}{6}.

Summing the three inequalities above implies:

\displaystyle\psi(r_{0})=\frac{16\phi(r_{0})}{r_{0}}+\sqrt{\frac{4\alpha^{\prime}t}{n\alpha r_{0}}}+\frac{4(V_{\text{max}}+3)C^{2}t}{nr_{0}}\leq\frac{1}{8}+\frac{1}{6}+\frac{1}{6}<\frac{1}{2}.

By picking
r=r_{0}
, we can further deduce that for any function
u\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)
, the following inequality holds with probability
1-e^{-t}
:

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathcal{E}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u)+{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u^{\ast})}{{\mathcal{E}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})+r_{0}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{h}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\psi(r_{0})<\frac{1}{2}.

Multiplying the denominator on both sides indicates:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{gen}}={\mathcal{E}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})-{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u)+{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(u^{\ast})\leq\frac{1}{2}\Big[{\mathcal{E}}(u)-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})\Big]+\frac{1}{2}r_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}+\frac{1}{2}r_{0}.

Substituting the upper bound above into the decomposition
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}\leq\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{gen}}+2\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}+\frac{4t}{n}
yields that with probability
1-3e^{-\min\{1,C_{q}\}t}
, we have:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}\leq\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{gen}}+\frac{3}{2}\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}+\frac{t}{2n}\leq\frac{1}{2}\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}+\frac{1}{2}r_{0}+\frac{3}{2}\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}+\frac{t}{2n}.

Simplifying the inequality above yields that with probability
1-3e^{-\min\{1,C_{q}\}t}
, we have:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\leq r_{0}+3\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{app}}+\frac{t}{n}=3\inf_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)+\max\{2^{14}r^{\ast},24M\frac{t}{n},\frac{36\alpha^{\prime}}{\alpha}\frac{t}{n}\}+\frac{t}{n}

\displaystyle\lesssim\inf_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)+\max\Big\{r^{*},\frac{t}{n}\Big\}

Moreover, using strong convexity of the DRM objective function proved in Theorem
22
implies:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{(n)}={\mathcal{E}}(\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\ast})\gtrsim\|\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}

Combining the two bounds above yields that with probability
1-3e^{-\min\{1,C_{q}\}t}
, we have:

\displaystyle\|\hat{u}_{\text{MDRM}}-u^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\lesssim\inf_{u_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega)}\Big({\mathcal{E}}(u_{{\mathcal{F}}})-{\mathcal{E}}(u^{\star})\Big)+\max\Big\{r^{*},\frac{t}{n}\Big\}

Appendix B
Intuition Behind the Sub-optimality of the Unmodified Deep Ritz Methods

In this section, we aim to discuss the intuition behind the sub-optimality of the unmodified DRM via using the truncation Fourier basis. To simplify the notation, in this section we consider the following simplest Poisson equation
\Delta u=f
on the hypercube with zero Dirichlet boundary condition. To illustrate the necessity of the modification we made, we consider the difference between the following two estimators

•

Estimator 1.
We use the truncated Fourier basis estimator to learn the right hand side function
f
and then we invert the PDE exactly to get the estimated
u
.

•

Estimator 2.
We plug in a parametrization of the truncated fourier basis into the empirical DRM objective

We would like to point out that
estimator 1 isn’t build for computational consideration
. Instead, we use it to consider the statistical limit of our sampled data. We first show that the estimator 1 can achieve the minimax optimal estimation error.

Error Of Estimator 1

Firstly, we show that if one wants to learn the function
u
in
H_{0}^{1}
norm, one need to learn the right hand side function
f
in
H_{0}^{-1}
norm. The
H_{0}^{-1}
norm is defined as the dual norm of the
H^{1}
norm,
i.e.

\|u\|_{H_{0}^{-1}}=\max_{\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}}\leq 1}\left<u,v\right>
. Once we assume we have an estimate
\hat{f}
of
f
in
H_{0}^{-1}
, we can have an estimate of
u
via
\hat{u}:=\left(\Delta\right)^{-1}\hat{f}
, whose distance to
u
in the
H^{1}
norm satisfies:

\displaystyle\|\nabla u-\nabla\hat{u}\|_{H_{0}^{1}}

\displaystyle=\max_{\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}}\leq 1}\left<\nabla u-\nabla\hat{u},\nabla v\right>

\displaystyle=\max_{\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}}\leq 1}\left<\Delta u-\Delta\hat{u},v\right>

\displaystyle=\max_{\|v\|_{H_{0}^{1}}\leq 1}\left<f-\hat{f},v\right>=\|f-\hat{f}\|_{H_{-1}}.

Estimator 1 using the truncated fourier estimator to estimate the right hand side function
f
. Suppose we can access a random sample of observed data as
\{x_{i},f(x_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, then the Fourier coefficient
f_{z}:=\left<u,\phi_{z}\right>
can be estimated as
\hat{f}_{z}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})\phi_{z}(x_{i})
. To bound the estimation error of
\hat{f}:=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq Z}\hat{f}_{z}\phi_{z}
in
H_{0}^{-1}
, we first apply the bias-variance decomposition:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|\hat{f}-f\|_{H_{0}^{-1}}^{2}\leq\|\mathbb{E}\hat{f}-\hat{f}\|_{H_{0}^{-1}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\|f-\mathbb{E}\hat{f}\|_{H_{0}^{-1}}^{2}

We first bound the bias term
\|\mathbb{E}\hat{f}-f\|_{H^{-1}}^{2}
. Given
\mathbb{E}\hat{f}=\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq Z}f_{z}\phi_{z}
, we have that for a truncation set
Z
of the from
\mathcal{Z}:=\{z\in\mathbb{N}^{d}|\|z\|_{\infty}\leq Z\}
, the bias term can be controlled by:

\displaystyle\|\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}>Z}f_{z}\phi_{z}\|_{H^{-1}}^{2}\leq C\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}>Z}f_{z}^{2}z^{-2}\leq\|z\|^{-2(s-1)}\|f\|_{H_{\alpha-2}}^{2}

Next we estimate the variance of the estimator by decomposing the variance into the following sum:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|f-\hat{f}\|_{H_{-1}}^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq Z}(\hat{f}_{z}-f_{z})^{2}\|\phi_{z}\|_{H_{-1}}^{2}\leq\sum_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq Z}|z|^{-1}\text{Var}(\hat{f}_{z}).

Finally we achieve a
Z^{-2(s-1)}+\frac{Z^{d-2}}{n}
upper bound for estimator 1. With optimal selection of
Z
, we can achieve the min-max optimal convergence rate
n^{-\frac{2s-2}{d+2s-4}}
.

Difference Between Estimator 1 and Estimator 2

Next we aim to understand the Deep Ritz Method objective function via plugging in a truncated Fourier series estimator. We consider an estimator of the form
u=\sum\hat{u}_{z}\phi_{z}(x)
, which lies in the space of truncated fourier series. Then the empirical DRM objective function can be expressed as

\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{z}\hat{u}_{z}\nabla\phi_{z}(x_{i})\right)^{2}+\sum_{z}\hat{u}_{z}\phi_{z}(x_{i})f(x_{i}).

(B.1)

We observe that (
B.1
) is a quadratic formula with respect to the Fourier coefficients
\bm{u}:=(u_{z})_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq Z}
. Thus, we can rewrite it as the following matrix form

\min\frac{1}{2}\bm{u}^{\top}\hat{A}\bm{u}+\bm{u}^{\top}\hat{f}\text{, where }\hat{A}=\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\nabla\phi_{i}(x_{i})\nabla\phi_{j}(x_{i})\right)_{\|i\|_{\infty}\leq Z,\|j\|_{\infty}\leq Z}.

(B.2)

Based on the matrix formulation
B.2
, we can compare the solution given by the two estimators

•

Estimator 1:
The Fourier coefficients of the solution of Estimator 1 are

\hat{\bm{u}}_{1}=\text{diag}\left(\|z\|^{2}\right)_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq Z}^{-1}\hat{f}.

(B.3)

•

Estimator 2:
The Fourier coefficients of the solution of Estimator 2 are

\hat{\bm{u}}_{2}=\hat{A}^{-1}\hat{f}.

(B.4)

Note that
\mathbb{E}\hat{A}=\left(\|z\|^{2}\right)_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq Z}
. Thus, we can further introduce another variance from the sampling of
A
. By directly estimating
\hat{\bm{u}}_{1}-\hat{\bm{u}}_{2}
, we will show that this term will be larger than the final convergence rate. Notice that

\|\hat{\bm{u}}_{1}-\hat{\bm{u}}_{2}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}=f^{\top}\left((\mathbb{E}\hat{A})^{-1}-\hat{A}^{-1}\right)^{\top}\text{diag}\left(\|z\|^{2}\right)_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq Z}\left((\mathbb{E}\hat{A})^{-1}-\hat{A}^{-1}\right)f

(B.5)

Next we aim to bound
\left((\mathbb{E}\hat{A})^{-1}-\hat{A}^{-1}\right)
. We first use the Matrix Bernstein Inequality
[
73
]
to bound the
H^{1}
distance between
\hat{\bm{u}}_{1}
and
\hat{\bm{u}}_{2}
. According to the Matrix Bernstein Inequality, we have that with probability
1-e^{-t}
, the following inequality holds

\left\|\left((\mathbb{E}\hat{A})-\hat{A}\right)\right\|_{\bm{H}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{Z^{d}}{n}}+\frac{t}{n},

(B.6)

where
\|\cdot\|_{\bm{H}}
is the matrix operator norm respect to the vector
\|\cdot\|_{\bm{H}}
defined as
\|z\|_{\bm{H}}^{2}=z^{\top}\text{diag}\left(\|z\|^{2}\right)_{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq Z}^{-1}z
. Note that

\left(I+(\mathbb{E}\hat{A})^{-1}\left(\hat{A}-(\mathbb{E}\hat{A})\right)\right)\left((\mathbb{E}\hat{A})^{-1}-\hat{A}^{-1}\right)=(\mathbb{E}\hat{A})^{-1}\left(\hat{A}-(\mathbb{E}\hat{A})\right)(\mathbb{E}\hat{A})^{-1}

(B.7)

When
n
is large enough, we know that
\frac{1}{2}I\leqslant I+(\mathbb{E}\hat{A})^{-1}\left(\hat{A}-(\mathbb{E}\hat{A})\right)\leqslant I
with high probability. Thus the term
\|\hat{u}_{1}-\hat{u}_{2}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}
is at the scale of
\left\|\left((\mathbb{E}\hat{A})-\hat{A}\right)\right\|_{\bm{H}}^{2}\approx\frac{Z^{d}}{n}
, which is of the same magnitude as what we get from the empirical process approach in our main proof. It is also larger than
\frac{Z^{d-2}}{n}
, which is the magnitude of the variance term for
\hat{u}_{1}
. Therefore, here we conjecture that the our bound for DRM itself is tight and leads to the sub-optimal convergence rate.

Appendix C
Preliminaries on Tools for Lower Bounds

In this section, we repeat the standard tools we use to establish the lower bound. The main tool we use is the Fano’s inequailty and the Varshamov-Gilber Lemma.

Lemma 49
(Fano’s methods).

Assume that
V
is a unifrom random variable over set
\mathcal{V}
, then for any markov chain
V\rightarrow X\rightarrow\hat{V}
, we always have

\mathcal{P}(\hat{V}\not=V)\geq 1-\frac{I(V;X)+\log 2}{\log(|\mathcal{V}|)}

Lemma 50
(Varshamov-Gillbert Lemma,
[
74
]
Theorem 2.9).

Let
D\geq 8
. There exists a subset
\mathcal{V}=\{\tau^{(0)},\cdots,\tau^{(2^{D/8})}\}
of
D-
dimensional hypercube
\mathcal{H}^{D}=\{0,1\}^{D}
such that
\tau^{(0)}=(0,0,\cdots,0)
and the
\ell_{1}
distance between every two elements is larger than
\frac{D}{8}

\sum_{l=1}^{D}\|\tau^{(j)}-\tau^{(k)}\|_{\ell_{1}}\geq\frac{D}{8}\text{, for all }0\leq j,k\leq 2^{D/8}
